The frontispieces

The Greatest Name, “Yá Bahá’u’l-Abhá”, was written in Persian script by the famous Bahá’í calligrapher Mishkn-Qalam in the days of Bahá’u’lláh Himself.  Later ‘Abdu’l-Bahá chose this design and advised the believers to place it in their homes as a blessing and protection.

This beautiful portrait of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá and was the one He Himself and Shoghi Effendi preferred and has been widely known for many years as the “Centre of the Covenant” photograph.

Both of these frontispieces have been reproduced in this book so that you can have a photograph of each of them made, to keep for yourself, and order copies whenever you wish to give a precious gift to other people.

“O that  could travel,

even though on foot and in the utmost

poverty, to these regions, and, raising

the call of Yá Bahá’u’l-Abhá in cities,

villages, mountains, deserts and oceans,

promote the Divine Teachings!  This, alas,

I cannot do.  How intensely I deplore it!

Please God, ye may achieve it!”

‘Abdu’l-Bahá

Preface to the Fourth Edition

Shortly after the establishment of the Universal House of Justice in 1963 and the completion of the Ten Year Crusade, Amatu’l-Bahá Rúhíyyih Khánum , Hand of the Cause of God and beloved consort of the Guardian of the Bahá’í Faith, embarked upon extensive and eventful travels across the five continents of the globe.  As a vivifying breeze, she infused a new spirit into the tired bodies of the pioneers.  We cannot say enough about the extraordinary impact these travels had on the Bahá’í communities, the institutions of the Faith, and on individual believers, particularly the pioneers, who, for many years and with great courage and oblivious of their own physical hardship, held the banner of the Faith aloft in every corner of the planet.

Pioneering—a phenomenon initially established by the Báb, Bahá’u’lláh and ‘Abdu’l-Bahá—was given a new dimension when the beloved Guardian announced the Ten Year Crusade in 1953, and pioneers opening certain new countries and islands were extolled by him as Knights of Bahá’u’lláh on the “Roll of Honour”.  The unexpected passing of Shoghi Effendi only a few years later shocked scores of Bahá’ís into arising in a wave of love as additional pioneers in order to fulfil the goals of his World Crusade.

By the time Amatu’l-Bahá began her travels, there were hundreds of pioneers of all ages who had left their native lands and settled in a variety of places:  the heartland of Africa, Latin America, the Pacific Islands, the countries of Europe.  The immense cultural differences and the constant struggles endured by living in inhospitable climates for

years caused fatigue in many of these dedicated pioneers.  Only the love of Bahá’u’lláh sustained these brave souls and enabled them to accept sacrifices in His path and remain at their posts.  It was in 1964 that Amatu’l-Bahá Rúhíyyih Khánum, in her sincere desire to teach and proclaim the Cause, combined with her love for these pioneers burning in her heart, began journeys  took her to isolated communities far in the bush, to cold, wind-swept islands, and to lone pioneers in humid villages deep in the jungle.  In the course of her historic travels, she shared the glad tidings of the Cause with unnumbered receptive souls, attracted them to the divine teachings, and encouraged the local believers in their efforts to expand and consolidate the Faith.  Likewise, in the eager hearts of the pioneers she met she instilled a fresh confidence and awareness of the inestimable importance of the service they were rendering to God and His Cause.

There were many occasions after Rúhíyyih Khánum’s international teaching travels when those who serve in the Holy Land had the opportunity to listen to her recounting thrilling stories about these travels in moving, inspiring and encouraging terms.  Many times we heard her express how proud she was of the achievements of the self-sacrificing pioneers, and at the same time she would, like a worrying mother, share her concern for their well-being and comfort.

While the practical advice offered in this book may not entirely be applicable to all friends everywhere, the tender love and affection of Amatu’l-Bahá are apparent.  She also brings to the attention of the friends the love and pride which the Bahá’ís of the world hold for those who arise to serve the Cause, particularly as pioneers, be it in a foreign land or on the homefront.

It is befitting that the Bahá’í Publishing Trust of India has

undertaken to publish the fourth edition of this book, as India was the country where Rúhíyyih Khánum chose to begin her extensive teaching visits.  Through this book, her tender concern and gift of common sense will continue to travel to the hearts of pioneers everywhere.

Hushmand Fatheazam

Haifa, 2002

[Blank page]

Foreword

This manual represents the personal experience and random observations of the author; it is neither exhaustive nor all-inclusive; it may, however, prove helpful to others who are increasingly arising from the East and from the West, and more recently from the North and from the South, to carry the Message of Bahá’u’lláh to their fellowmen.  The first great travelling teachers and settlers in foreign lands—who later became known as “pioneers”—were the Persian believers in the nineteenth century, followed, during the first decades of the twentieth century, by Bahá’ís primarily of North American origin.

During the final years of the First World War ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, the Son of the Founder of the Bahá’í Faith, Bahá’u’lláh, revealed His great teaching Tablets which became known as the “Divine Plan”.  In 1937 His successor Shoghi Effendi, the Guardian and Head of the Faith, inaugurated its first phase, given to the Bahá’í Community of North America, which marked the commencement of a systematic implementation of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s own instructions; this was followed by a second Plan and then a third Plan—a ten-year World Crusade—to carry the news of Bahá’u’lláh’s appearance and His teachings to every country, territory and major island of the world.  It was during the first year of this Plan that the Bahá’ís dispersed far and wide over the face of the globe and implanted the banner of Bahá’u’lláh in no less than 131 countries which His Cause had hitherto never reached.  From 1964 to 1973 a Nine Year Plan, inaugurated by the Supreme Body of the Bahá’í Faith, the Universal House of Justice, was

added to the process set in motion by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá Himself and carried systematically forward by Shoghi Effendi during his ministry.  We are now in yet another phase of the majestic unfoldment of this historic scheme designed to ensure that all men are given the opportunity to hear the Message of God for this Day.  During this latest phase there is probably a greater need for pioneers and travelling teachers than ever before in Bahá’í history.

In the course of my travels in Asia, Africa, Latin America and the Caribbean and Indian Ocean islands I have met a great number of pioneers and learned to admire their consecration and fortitude in the face of many difficulties and problems.  I saw them sometimes run-down, ill, discouraged and disillusioned, and occasionally heart-broken and hopeless, and longed to be able in some way to help them, to lighten their load and cheer them up and perhaps through suggestions make their road a little smoother and assist them to a better understanding of their task and some of the peoples they will live with and teach, for I believe a great many of their hardships, heartaches and problems are caused by their own ignorance and improper orientation to the tasks they have undertaken with so much devotion and enthusiasm.  That is why this book is such a mixture of quotations, findings, suggestions, recipes and admonitions, and what may appear to be aphorisms and even platitudes!  Often I wish people would spell things out clearly for me; I have tried here to at least say what I mean in unambiguous language that perchance it may be of help to others.

Rúhíyyih

Haifa, 1974

[Blank page]

[Blank page]

Selected quotations

Bahá’u’lláh tells us:  “Be unrestrained as the wind, while carrying the Message of Him Who hath caused the awn of Divine Guidance to break.  Consider how the wind, faithful to that which God hath ordained, bloweth upon all regions of the earth, be they inhabited or desolate.  Neither the sight of desolation, nor the evidences of prosperity, can either pain or please it.  It bloweth in every direction, as bidden by its Creator.”

Of those who arise to teach His Cause He says that they must “spread it abroad on the earth with high resolve, with minds that are wholly centred in Him, and with hearts that are completely detached from and independent of all things, and with souls that are sanctified from the world and its vanities.  It behoveth them to choose as the best provision for their journey reliance upon God, and to clothe themselves with the love of their Lord, the Most Exalted, the All-Glorious.  If they do so, their words shall influence their hearers.”

“They that have forsaken their country for the purpose of teaching Our Cause—these shall the Faithful Spirit strengthen through its power.  A company of Our chosen angels shall go forth with them, as bidden by Him Who is the Almighty, the All-Wise.  How great the blessedness that awaiteth him that hath attained the honour of serving the Almighty!  By My life!  No act, however great, can compare with it, except such deeds as have been ordained by God, the All-Powerful, the Most Mighty.  Such a service is, indeed, the prince of all goodly deeds, and the ornament of every goodly act. …

Whoso ariseth to teach Our Cause must needs detach himself from all earthly things, and regard, at all times, the triumph of Our Faith as his supreme objective.”

When one of His followers “determineth to leave his home, for the sake of the Cause of his Lord,” Bahá’u’lláh states, “let him put his whole trust in God, as the best provision for his journey, and array himself with the robe of virtue.”

“Whoso ariseth, in this Day, to aid Our Cause, and summoneth to his assistance the hosts of a praiseworthy character and upright conduct, the influence flowing from such an action will, most certainly, be diffused throughout the whole world.”

“Centre your energies in the propagation of the Faith of God.  Whoso is worthy of so high a calling, let him arise and promote it.  Whoso is unable, it is his duty to appoint him who will, in his stead, proclaim this Revelation …”

With all His encouragement to teach, however, Baha’u’llah adds this stern admonition:  “Whoso ariseth among you to teach the Cause of his Lord, let him, before all else, teach his own self, that his speech may attract the hearts of them that hear him.  Unless he teache his own self, the words of his mouth will not influence the heart of the seeker.  Take heed, O people, lest ye be of them that give good counsel to others but forget to follow it themselves.”

“God hath prescribed unto every one the duty of teaching His Cause.  Whoever ariseth to discharge this duty, must needs, ere he proclaimeth His Message, adorn himself with the ornament of an upright and praiseworthy character so that his words may attract the hearts of such as are receptive to his call.  Without it, he can never hope to influence his hearers.”

“Gird up the loins of thine endeavour” Baha’u’llah tells us, “that haply thou mayest guide thy neighbour to the law of God, the Most Merciful.  Such an act, verily, excelleth all other acts in the sight of God, the All-Possessing, the Most High.  Such must be thy steadfastness in the Cause of God, that no earthly thing whatsoever will have the power to deter thee from thy duty.  Though the powers of earth be leagued against thee, though all men dispute

with thee, thou must remain unshaken. …”

It behoveth anyone who is a real lover of God, He goes on to state, “to fix his gaze upon the fundamentals of His Faith, and to labour diligently for its propagation.  Wholly for the sake of God he should proclaim His Message, and with that same spirit accept whatever response his words may evoke in his hearer.  He who shall accept and believe, shall receive his reward; and he who shall turn away, shall receive none other than his own punishment.”

It is Bahá’u’lláh’s followers, Shoghi Effendi tells us, who are the true lovers of mankind, to such a degree that to them, “every foreign land is a fatherland, and every fatherland a foreign land.”  How much more must this be true of the pioneers who, forsaking their countries, journey to live among other people and put down their roots in a foreign soil?  They are of those who, the Guardian states, “warmed by the energizing influence of God’s creative love cherish His creatures for His sake, and recognize in every human face a sign of His reflected glory.”

“Now is the time”, Bahá’u’lláh asserts, “to cheer and refresh the down-cast through the invigorating breeze of love and fellowship, and the living waters of friendliness …

Show forbearance and benevolence and love to one another.  Should any one among you be incapable of grasping a certain truth, or be striving to comprehend it, show forth, when conversing with him, a spirit of extreme kindliness and good-will.  Help him to see and recognize the truth, without esteeming yourself to be, in the least, superior to him, or to be possessed of greater endowments.”

What must he the teacher’s attitude towards those he teaches?  In one of His prayers Baha’u’llah, addressing God, says that He is “summoning all the inmates of heaven and all the inhabitants of earth to the immensity of Thy mercy and the court of Thy grace.”  It is above all a message of hope,

forgiveness, love and happiness that the pioneer takes to others, and the main emphasis must be on unity:  “We love to see you at all times”, Baha’u’llah says, “consorting in amity and concord within the paradise of My goodpleasure, and to inhale from your acts the fragrance of friendliness and unity, of loving-kindness and fellowship.”  The entire keynote of His Revelation is unity, within the family, within the community, the clan, the tribe, the nation, the world.  “So powerful is the light of unity that it can illuminate the whole ”, He tells us.  It is not enough to read these words and say to ourselves, “How beautiful”, and dismiss them.  We should ponder what the light of the sun does when it shines all day long; unity, Bahá’u’lláh is telling us, is comparable to the sun’s light and its life-giving effect on the entire world.

What must be the teacher’s attitude towards himself—also a very important factor in pioneering?  Bahá’u’lláh Himself, as usual, has given us the key in His own wonderful words, expressing what we believers should be like:  “Adversities are incapable of estranging them from Thy Cause, and the vicissitudes of fortune can never cause them to stray from Thy pleasure.”  In brief words—full of divine compassion and understanding for His followers’ frailties and shortcomings—He writes in one of His prayers, “accept from Thy servants what they are capable of showing forth in Thy path”  Not what the ideal standard would be, but what we are capable of giving!

The Master was the greatest teacher of all; over and over Shoghi Effendi told the friends to study the teaching method of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, His talks, His example, His entire approach, which is essentially extremely direct, simple and lucid.  When He was leaving America He gave a farewell talk to the Bahá’ís, and some of His words and thoughts were as follows:  “I … give you my instructions and exhortations today

and these are none other than the teachings of Bahá’u’lláh.

You must manifest love and affection towards all mankind.  Do not exalt yourselves above others but consider all as your equals, recognizing them as the servants of one God.  Know that God is compassionate toward all; therefore love all from the depths of your hearts … ever speak disparagingly of others but praise without distinction.  Pollute not your tongues by speaking evil of another …  Let not your heart be offended with anyone …  Do not complain of others.  Refrain from reprimanding them and if you wish to give admonition or advice let it be offered in such a way that it will not burden the hearer.  Turn all your thoughts towards bringing joy to hearts.  Beware!  Beware! lest ye offend any heart …  Be the source of consolation to every sad one, assist every weak one … and shelter those who are overshadowed by fear.

In brief, let each one of you be as a lamp shining forth with the light of the virtues of the world of humanity.  Be trustworthy, sincere, affectionate and replete with chastity.  Be illumined, be spiritual, be divine, be glorious, be quickened of God, be a Bahá’í.”

Guidance

‘Abdu’l-Bahá said guidance was when the doors opened.  If a person wants to do a thing, prays about it, exerts every effort to do it, questions his own sincerity and desire to do it, and still, in spite of all this, the way does not open for him to do it, then he should accept that it was not the right thing for him to do.  However, to be sure it was not the right thing, the prayers, the motive and the effort to accomplish it must have been sincere and sustained in the first place.  If he makes a half-hearted attempt to do something and fails, he

cannot very well excuse himself by saying, “It was not the will of God”!

Where to pioneer

I would suggest certain things, particularly to pioneers.  Travelling teachers, as their stay is brief, may not find them applicable to themselves.

If an individual has a weak stomach and is prone to getting digestive and intestinal upsets, he should not go to those countries whose foods contain a great deal of chili, peppers and spices, such as Pakistan, India, Sri Lanka, Indonesia, Mexico and so on.  He may think he can avoid eating them, but it is impossible to do so unless he has a home of his own or eats in first class, expensive restaurants for tourists in the main cities.  As this is a very confining and expensive way of living, it is much better for a person to recognize his limitations before he gets involved in a way of pioneering that he cannot maintain.  A great deal of the time he will find almost no food that does not contain these spices.  I remember a personal experience in India:  I got quite ill travelling, had a high fever and was obliged to stop and go to bed in a Dak bungalow in a small town; with great difficulty a chicken was procured and given to the cook with strict instructions to boil it with a little rice and salt and nothing else, no curry or spices at all; as I was very weak and this was my first “meal” I looked forward to it very much; it was full of black pepper corns!  When the cook was scolded and reminded of his instructions by the dear Bahá’í who was taking care of me he lamented, “But I didn’t put anything in it—only pepper!”  Although I love hot curries and can eat chilis raw, that day I could not touch the broth.  Of course the exception to this

general advice is the person whose heart has such a love and longing for a place not really suitable for his health that he will either learn to endure or partly overcome his difficulties.

If a person really cannot stand intense heat (and some countries though not hot all the time have terrific hot seasons) he should not go to a place where he will waste his time through being completely enervated and unable to work, not to mention the waste of money involved in getting him there.  There is a tremendous need for pioneers all over the world; let him choose another place.  The same advice holds true for someone who has a marked intolerance for great cold; go somewhere else.

If an individual has high blood pressure or some heart condition for which his doctor considers high altitudes would be bad, let him avoid the mountains, as to go there would be wasting time and money and might do his health serious damage.

My personal opinion is that, with the exception of such extreme cases as those cited above, almost anyone, from a purely physical standpoint, can pioneer almost anywhere.  Man is the most adaptable creature on earth; our species exists anywhere and everywhere; we live amidst arctic snows and their depressing month-long nights; we cling to the bitter, wind-ravaged heights of barren mountains; we steam in suffocating tropical jungles and swamps.  Obviously, if a person is born an Eskimo, a Bushman, an Andean Indian or a Pygmy, he is better suited to his environment and native diet than a settler from somewhere else.  But even such extreme adaptations can take place, I believe, for three reasons.

First, physically:  the very blood in our veins adapts itself, getting thicker in cold climates and thinner in hot ones; a man learns to live where he is and eat what he can get;

circumstances may be extremely difficult, but the very nature of man is that he adapts to them.

Second, mentally:  much more of life takes place in our minds—conscious and subconscious—than we dream of.  If we want to do a thing, like doing it, believe we can do it, then usually we do do it without much trouble.  If a pioneer approaches a new country, a new environment, with prejudice, constantly comparing it to “back home”, disliking every single thing about it, complaining daily over its climate, it does not take him very long to hate everything, consider himself a martyr, look down on the people he has come to teach and become violently homesick.  All of this not only prevents him from showering love and encouragement on others but they sense this criticism, voiced or unvoiced, and are repelled rather than attracted, for a critical attitude does not attract one human being to another—it may possibly attract a scientist to a problem, but certainly not one heart to another!

I remember when I was first faced by the burning heat of the plains of India that strikes one like a blast from a furnace; I felt, being a North American child of the snows, I could not endure it, it shrivelled me.  Then I thought of my love for India, the beauty of India as a great centre of human culture; I thought of the sun as the life-giver of the world, the symbol of God Himself, and I opened my arms to it all, so to speak, and welcomed the heat as something dazzling and purifying and a blessing.  It was this mental change that enabled me to endure the climate.  When I found, in Africa, that I was running perspiration from every pore in the tropical, humid heat, I told myself that people pay money to take steam baths so they can perspire and eliminate many poisons by this method; and here am I doing it as a regular health cure free of charge!  I have found that if I resist these great

natural forces, I cannot stand them—neither the force nor my own struggle of resistance—but if I welcome them, I find that through being relaxed and receptive I can endure them relatively easily.

The third element of course is spiritual:  we have it all, all the guidance, all the challenge, all the encouragement, all the vision we will ever need, set forth in clear perspective by the Guardian.  The torch of the Faith, he said, must be carried “to regions so remote, so backward, so inhospitable that neither the light of Christianity or Islám has, after the revolution of centuries, as yet penetrated.”  “Braving the fearful cold of the Arctic regions and the enervating heat of the torrid zone; heedless of the hazards, the loneliness and the austerity of the deserts, the far-away islands and mountains wherein they will be called upon to dwell; undeterred by the clamour which the exponents of religious orthodoxy are sure to raise, or by the restrictive measures which political leaders may impose; undismayed by the smallness of their numbers …; placing their whole trust in the matchless potency of Bahá’u’lláh’s teachings, in the all-conquering power of His might and the infallibility of His glorious and oft-repeated promises, let them press forward …”  The winning to the Faith, Shoghi Effendi said, of fresh recruits, would presage the day prophesied by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá when we “will witness the entry by troops of people of divers nations and races into the Bahá’í world—a day which, viewed in its proper perspective, will be the prelude to that long-awaited hour when a mass conversion on the part of these same nations and races, and as a direct result of a chain of events, momentous and possibly catastrophic in nature, and which cannot as yet be even dimly visualized, will suddenly revolutionize the fortunes of the Faith, derange the equilibrium of the world, and reinforce a thousand-fold the

numerical strength as well as the material power and the spiritual authority of the Faith of Bahá’u’lláh.”

The task of the pioneers is to assist in laying the foundations of the Administrative Order of Bahá’u’lláh throughout the entire globe, under whose “sheltering shadow”, Shoghi Effendi stated, people of every race, religion, colour and country will be gathered.

There are two other factors in where to pioneer which I think should never be ignored and these are whether a person has a particular asset, or a predilection that would either contribute greatly to his services in a place or, on the contrary, mitigate against his success as a pioneer there.  For instance the need for French-speaking pioneers and travelling teachers is very great; we have far fewer French-speaking people available than English-speaking ones; therefore, if there are no other over-riding considerations it seems logical to send French speakers to those places where it is spoken in preference to one where English prevails; how much more should this be a consideration if a person knows a relatively restricted but important language, such as Quechua, spoken widely in Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador and neighbouring countries; or Chinese, which, though very widespread throughout the world, is a language which almost nobody willing to pioneer can speak.  That is the special asset factor; the predilection, I believe, should not be ignored, any more than the asset.  Some people are eager to serve the Cause of God and ready to go wherever they are needed; other people have a strong urge either towards or away from a race or a continent or a country; I think this must be taken into consideration by the individual himself and by those responsible for sending him out because the love for, the yearning of the heart of a person towards say, the Pacific islanders as opposed to the Africans, or vice versa, can mean the difference between the

pioneer remaining there and being a great success or never quite feeling in tune.  I am referring to really deep feelings of attraction, or of antipathy, and not to caprice, which may sweep over a pioneer and make him or her feel the other end of the world would have been better, so lets now go there!

A pioneer who plans to move into a goal and serve the Cause of God and earn his living there should carefully inquire, from proper Bahá’í sources as well as non-Bahá’í ones, what kind of a job, if any, he can get when he arrives.  The situation differs greatly from country to country; one place may never grant a residence visa to a person who has entered on only a tourist visa, obliging him, even if he has obtained a job, to go out and re-apply for a residence visa which he may never get.  Another country may require the pioneer to go there on a tourist visa first, obtain employment and then grant him a residence visa.  It is absolutely essential to be properly informed before one leaps out, full of devotion and enthusiasm, into the unknown!

There is also the all-important question of where to settle in the country one has pioneered to:  if a village—where the need is always very great—the strong probability is the pioneer must have a personal income or receive help from the Fund as jobs are likely to be non-existent; if he can get a job in that country, the secondary towns—small cities or large villages—should certainly have priority over the capital or other big cities.  Provincial capitals are very important because settling there means the pioneer can either open to the Faith a new rovince or tate or epartment, as the case may be, or assist and foster the work in a large area where he or his family are more than likely the only pioneers.  Far too often—to the great detriment of the Bahá’í work—one finds most of the pioneers congregated in the capital of the country, busy earning their living, having personality

problems with other pioneers and really, if they only knew it, spiritually frustrated because they are not accomplishing for Bahá’u’lláh what they set out to do when they left their native land for His sake.  So far from the path of ideal service they originally envisaged can this way of life in the big cities lead them that I have heard pioneers say they were in an embarrassing position because in the apartment house or district they lived in the poor village Bahá’ís could not come to see them or attend meetings in their homes because people of that class were not welcome in such places!  The pioneer must be on his guard, as much as lies within his power, not to be deflected from his spiritual course or ensnared in the traps of our moribund society as exemplified in the life of big cities.

When we arise to teach, whether as pioneers or travelling teachers, we are like little boats setting out on the down-flow of a mighty river  automatically carries us on by its own force.  We must always remember that by leaving our homes and our countries to serve the Cause of God divine confirmations become available to us, the Hosts of the Supreme Concourse become eager to help us, our meagre powers become infused by the divine outpourings from on High.

What to do when you get there

Many times the Guardian used to say, “This is the religion of the Golden Mean, the middle of the way”, in other words, balanced and not full of extremes.  What does this imply for a person arriving in a foreign place new to him in every way?  To me it means he should never lose his common sense.  Relying on God he must at the same time have enough sense to remember that God helps those who help themselves, that

He gave us a brain to use.  For instance, when all of us know perfectly well that everybody is honest in our own country, why should we suppose everyone is honest in the new place we have arrived in?  If we had enough sense at home to lock up the house and the automobile when we left them, thought twice about how we spent our money, and made an effort to get good value and not be cheated, why should we leave these sensible qualities behind when we arise to serve the Cause abroad?

One of the great arts of living, one we all strive to attain, is to be able to strike the happy medium between idealism and realism, between using good judgement personally and still, ultimately, placing all our affairs in the hands of God and letting Him guide us.  Too often we consider that to be spiritual is to not be practical!  Yet what could ever be more intensely practical than the Teachings Bahá’u’lláh gave us?  Do we ever stop to think that when He was alive He was the most practical, efficient and competent person in the whole world?  We are so overpowered by His spiritual station that we forget the many ways this manifested itself.

All my life I have been the witness of acts of extreme impracticability committed by Bahá’ís in the name of spirituality.  Let me give some examples of the kind of acts I am referring to:  normally people associate keeping in good health with certain sensible principles, but when they get carried away by their enthusiasm and “spirituality”, they ignore all these and often fall ill needlessly; normally they would ponder certain economic realities before embarking on a project like opening a school, starting a business, entering into a partnership, or making some substantial investment, but in the name of “Baha’u’llah will look after me” they leap in without proper investigation and forethought, often suffer humiliation and bankruptcy, involve others in

hardship and disgrace, and then are hurt and shaken to the core because they feel that God deserted them in their hour of need!  No wonder Shoghi Effendi constantly advised the friends that in technical matters they should seek the advice of experts.  I could write a good-sized book about the cases when they did not, and they and the Cause were the losers.  All this adds up to:  if you are ill get a good doctor; if you want to invest money consult a reliable banker or business adviser; if you plan to go into business with someone (a Bahá’í or non-Bahá’í) investigate them as to reliability and competence, honesty and capacity to really do what they claim they can and will do; and, above all, do it in a legal way.  Have a legal contract stating your relationship, affairs, and responsibilities.  When something, is in writing, properly signed and according to law, many future misunderstandings and consequent inharmony can be avoided, and all those remarks such as, “But you promised”, “I thought you meant”, “You were the one who was supposed to do that, not I …”, which are so often said when some understanding falls apart, need never be said.  This holds true in many cases for work being done for the Faith, aside from purely individual contracts.  I remember a pioneer who wanted to build (with his hard-won money from an underpaid school job) a centre in a village area.  A local believer assured him he was a competent contractor, showing him some pretty drawings, and the pioneer advanced quite a sum of money to order the mud bricks required.  Some time later, on visiting the site, he found a heap of deteriorated, improperly baked, useless bricks; when he called the contractor to account and demanded that he furnish proper bricks as he had not fulfilled his promise, the contractor wrote to him that he had been insulted and would have recourse to the law!  However dishonest this man’s conduct was, I blame the

well-educated, informed Bahá’í for not going out to supervise what was being done in the first place.  If one cannot carry something through properly it is often better not to start it at all.  In this case the devoted pioneer could not afford to replace the sum already paid out and start over again, the dishonest man gained successfully by his dishonesty and the villagers (aside from what may have gone on in their minds) had the unedifying sight of a huge pile of melting mud bricks to remind them of the centre they had hoped to have.

Aside from this general advice there are certain specific things that a pioneer or travelling teacher should be aware of:  when he arrives with no notification in a strange country and a strange city he can, through no previous arrangements having been made, find that a few days in a hotel can take almost all the ready money he has, or even the allowance for a whole month’s living expenses.  In developing countries in Africa, the islands of the Indian Ocean, the Pacific and other places, the cost of living for travellers is often exorbitant; the hotels available are either first class tourist ones, where rooms and meals are prohibitively expensive, or the native lodging houses—provided they exist at all, and often they do not—may not be at all suitable for newcomers, for many reasons, particularly if they have children; young men travellers might find they could take such accommodations, but for women alone or families it would be difficult.  After a person lives in a place for a while he learns what to do, but in the beginning everything is strange and new and he needs help and advice.  In order to ensure he gets this, careful plans, well in advance of his arrival, should be made.  The traveller should always bear in mind that the time it takes for mail to arrive and decisions to be made varies greatly from country to country and that what he, from his

past experience, believes will be handled in a few hours can take a few weeks or even a few months to be taken care of in some remote place.

It is here that a more serious and better organized effort needs to be made by both pioneers and travellers, as well as the Bahá’í bodies responsible for assisting them to go out or receiving them when they arrive.  Proper advance notice should be given to the National Spiritual Assembly, the Local Spiritual Assembly, or the committee which is responsible for incoming travellers and pioneers, so that someone is aware of when they will arrive and can, if they are asked to, arrange for a place for them to sleep.  In some cases it may be a local Bahá’í or pioneer who offers hospitality; in this case I feel a word should be said on their behalf.  Too often such kind and loving souls become victims of the thoughtlessness of the newcomers who do not, after a few days, go to them and suggest it might be better if they moved to a hotel or pension or place of their own; or failing this, at least openly and frankly insist on paying their share of the expenses.  I have met pioneers whose backs have been almost broken by a constant flow of arriving teachers and pioneers on their way to some place and who expect to be met at the airport, entertained, taken here and there, fed and bedded, as if by some royal right, or a mistaken belief that this exists in the Teachings when, as a matter of fact, the opposite is true, for Bahá’u’lláh enjoins us to enter no man’s house without his permission and without his wanting us there!

Before a person goes to a place, he should try to get reliable information about the climate.  Misinformation on this subject has been one of my own greatest problems in travelling.  People say it is very hot because it is in the tropics; what they fail to add is that at certain seasons, and often

after sunset—particularly, I found, even in the hottest jungles, 4 a.m. in the night—it can be chilly if not downright cold, and one needs a sweater and a blanket if one is at all inclined to be a chilly person.  Remember deserts and savannas are always really cold at night.  In the Andes I found that in the rarfied air of the heights if I sat in the sun with any wool on I began to perspire from the heat, but if I moved into the shade in a minute I was shivering from the cold.  It is extremely difficult to be prepared for all these changes in temperature, but long experience has taught me (being sensitive to the cold) to adopt what I call the onion system—layers—so that if I am hot I can peel down to something cool, and if I am cold I can add a sweater or coat or poncho to get warm.  Of course there are places, such as southern India, where it stays so hot that the only time we could take a shower was late at night because otherwise the water (from the “cold” tap) burned our skins!  But this I have found is the exception, so my own principle is:  if I am going to a hot country I have at least one or two wools I can add if required, and if I am going to a cold country (such as Alaska in the summer) I have at least one or two cottons to wear if it gets too hot, which it often does!  A cardigan sweater is an excellent investment, and many are the nights I have slept in mine.

If a person is in the habit of taking vitamins he had better take them with him, for, except in main cities, he may get none or they will be expensive.  Such things for ones health are rare whereas aspirin and cosmetics one can buy anywhere!  Likewise if the traveller or pioneer requires some special medication for a heart or skin condition, asthmatic attacks, and so on, he had better take a supply with him because no matter what anyone tells him to the contrary, he may not find it available where he is going.

Depression

When a person arises to serve the Cause of God, leaves his home and journeys to distant lands, he often feels Baha’u’llah very near.  This does not mean, however, that all of us are not subject to moments of depression, of wondering if we are doing the right thing, if we are in the right place, if we should not leave and go somewhere else.  There are many reasons for such feelings and many answers to such questions.

The body itself has its moments of better health and poorer health, and this in turn affects our minds and our outlook on life.  Often we get depressed because we are over-tired have a stomach upset are not sleeping well or some other purely physical condition; the passing of this physical condition, a change of environment for a few days, or even a change of occupation such as doing something relaxing we enjoy doing, will be sufficient to cure our depression, not to mention the spiritual assistance prayers can give us.

We must recognize our condition sensibly and objectively and not immediately jump to the conclusion that God has abandoned us, we are not worthy, we have made the wrong choice in going out to pioneer and had better go home!

It is admittedly difficult to judge what is caused by a purely physical, temporary condition and what is really fundamental.  A person can be really ill and need medical care; if he is suffering seriously from some condition, he should immediately consult the best doctor available; some doctors, alas, are themselves unduly pessimistic and willing to send a person home immediately—unaware that there are spiritual powers to help and sustain Bahá’ís and that maybe by treatment the condition could be ameliorated or cured without the pioneer having to go back to his own country.  Sometimes a

good native doctor—I mean one who is a properly trained medical graduate, an African in Africa, an Indian in India and so on—is better than a foreign doctor because he knows his own country well and the kinds of ailments people get in it, and he does not scare so easily or judge everything by an American or European standard.

There is another kind of depression which is caused by changes of temperature and which can have as much physical effect on us as on a piece of wood or violin strings or the head of a drum which contracts and expands according to the degree of heat, cold, humidity or dryness of the weather; rain depresses some people very much; storms, thunder and lightning depress others; a person may suffer an aggravation of his own personal idiosyncrasies, such as sleeplessness, headaches and so on, by arriving at the seashore or at a high altitude.

We must all learn to look at our own reactions objectively and dispassionately and not get all emotionally entangled, confusing purely physical reactions with spiritual things, and the state of our body with the state of our soul.

The thing the Cause of Bahá’u’lláh needs today is active servants to propel it forward more rapidly in these days of world crisis; the thing that humanity needs is to hear of Bahá’u’lláh; to dwell on ones self, to ponder ones own shortcomings, to spend precious time either despising ones self or pitying ones self is going to be of service to neither God nor man.  Though a little soul-searching every now and then is both necessary and salutary and spiritual stocktaking essential, these are not the times in which to sit idle, enclosed in a little personal cell of “depression”.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, according to what my mother told me, said “The secret of self-mastery is self-forgetfulness.”  Whether He said it or not it is certainly a profound truth, for the more one dwells on

ones self the more entangled, self-centred and frustrated one gets, whereas the more one serves this blessed Faith the greater becomes ones freedom from the bonds of self and unhappiness.  Bahá’u’lláh, in a wonderful passage of His Writings, says “Beware lest thou allow anything whatsoever to grieve thee.”  I quote it in full:

Wherefore, be thankful to God, for having strengthened thee to aid His Cause, for having made the flowers of knowledge and understanding to spring forth in the garden of thine heart.  Thus hath His grace encompassed thee, and encompassed the whole of creation.  Beware, lest thou allow anything whatsoever to grieve thee.  Rid thyself of all attachment to the vain allusions of men, and cast behind thy back the idle and subtle disputations of them that are veiled from God.  Proclaim, then, that which the Most Great Spirit will inspire thee to utter in the service of the Cause of thy Lord, that thou mayest stir up the souls of all men and incline their hearts unto this most blessed and allglorious Court.

Problems that face pioneers

In addition to where to pioneer and what work may be available when one gets there, in what city or town to reside and how to adapt to a new country and conditions, there are special factors of a purely Bahá’í nature which the settler or traveller should know something about.  The French have a proverb  is wise and, if not 100 per cent true all the time, at least holds good most of the time:  “tout connaître, c’est tout pardonner” or “to know all is to forgive all”.  Every year that passes I accumulate more examples from my own

experience of life of how true this is.  So often we—all of us—misjudge others by appearances, condemn them in our minds without a hearing (when we are supposed to not judge others but to love them and forgive them) and leap to false conclusions because our emotions are involved and we are upset over or hurt by something, occasionally with just provocation, but usually purely imaginary.

Bahá’ís coordinate their activities through their administrative institutions hey usually consult committees and their National Assemblies about their pioneering plans and when they get to where they are going they expect to render their services under the guidance and with the help advice and encouragement of the National Assembly of that country.  If the pioneer himself comes from a country where the Bahá’í administrative experience is relatively new and does not yet function very efficiently, he is neither very shocked nor surprised to find the same holds true of where he has arrived.  But if he comes from a country where an efficient administration answered his questions and letters and advised and helped him, he may find that he gets no answers, or long delayed ones, to his questions and letters, that there seems no administrative body set up to help or guide him and that in addition to being in a new country and entering upon a new life, it looks as if no one cared he was there anyway!  This is a plain statement of facts and not hypothetical.  We may well ask “What is wrong?  Why should such conditions exist?”  Well, there is nothing very wrong, just combinations of circumstances affecting other people’s lives and now, because we are involved with them, affecting our own.  One reason pioneers are so much needed all over the world, aside from the vast field open for mass teaching, is that in many countries what the word “administration”—Bahá’í or otherwise—means is not understood at all.  To administer is to

manage and coordinate something so that it goes ahead smoothly and produces the desired result.  In the economic field this implies trained, skilled experts and in the field of government, political parties and manoeuvres.  But in our Bahá’í administration, the people who run it are just ordinary people, elected, entirely secret ballot, because they seemed the best the believers could find.  This means that National Assembly members are usually busy earning their living, may have long distances to cover in order to attend meetings, are employees who are circumscribed in their time by their employment, are farmers from the interior, or villagers to whom time does not mean what it does to us.  All of this has nothing to do with the good-will, intelligence, spiritual capacity, faith or devotion of National Assembly members, Local Assembly members, or a committee’s.  It is just a self-explanatory statement of fact.  Many National Assemblies all over the world have difficulty in holding meetings at all.  I recall one vast country where it costs over a thousand dollars in air tickets to get an Assembly together and then it is seldom more than a bare quorum.  If an Assembly, labouring valiantly in the face of difficulties similar to this, does not promptly answer its letters or pay attention to pioneers there are reasons for it which the pioneer, putting himself in its place, can easily see and should make him more determined than ever to stay and teach in that country and help that national community and that National Assembly all he can.

Many National Assemblies have great trouble in finding, among their nine members, a person who will accept to act as secretary; mostly it is the secretary on whom the heaviest burden falls, who is expected to come and live at the National Centre and develop a National Secretariat.  I remember the case of a farmer who was chosen secretary of one Na-

tional Assembly; he had to leave his home over 200 kilometres away and, with only a rudimentary school education and an extremely limited knowledge of English, unable to type and not good at writing in his own language, take up his Bahá’í burden because that National Assembly could not find among its members anyone else to do it!  He grew into his job, and grew spiritually and matured, until he was scarcely to be recognized as his original self.  We cannot judge.  A pioneer must be loving patient and understanding and must persevere—the four cardinal characteristics required for this blessed and unique type of service to the Cause of God.

Pioneers are drawn from many categories:  sometimes old and experienced believers in both the teaching and administrative fields of service, and sometimes young, new ones with no experience and not much knowledge.  It does not matter; all are desperately needed, all over the world.  Each one, however, is likely to have his own kind of test!  For those with much experience it is maddening to know how to do something and not be able to see it is done right; and yet just here is where the older Bahá’í is supposed (one hopes!) to have acquired enough experience, and be a mature enough believer, not to scold and preach and say, “Do it as I tell you because I know what I am talking about!”  Patiently, gently, politely, lovingly, he should be of so much help to the Bahá’í that all will return that love and seek to benefit from his knowledge.  The newer and less experienced believer who goes pioneering, not having lived for years in a home community, is less likely to find himself tried and tested on such matters as Bahá’í administration and the conduct of affairs but may well feel disillusioned and unwanted when he finds no proper plans have been made for his services and he is getting neither the help nor advice he needs.  One of the reactions to all

such problems as these is that the pioneer immediately sits down and writes a long and often accusatory lament (I have read some of them) to the committee who sent him out, describing the degree, in his eyes, to which the National Assembly or the committees or the Local Assembly of the country he has arrived in, is not properly discharging its duty towards him and often towards everything else!  Such letters to an administrative body, while they undoubtedly relieve the pioneer of a lot of steam, can, in the repercussions they set off, create inharmony and even promote a lot of useless gossip about a national community on the other side of the world.  I am not saying the pioneers have no legitimate complaints.  I am trying to say that I believe at least 80 per cent of everything that seems wrong in the beginning will sort itself out within say a year, if the pioneer will be patient, loving, understanding and will persevere.  How often has each one of us regretted a hasty or thoughtless action when later more facts came to light or we got to know the other person and their problems and felt sympathy for them instead of condemnation?  The people of the world today, whether Bahá’ís or non-Bahá’ís—no matter how desperately they need everything in the Bahá’í administration—are not going to benefit by being banged on the head by rules and regulations; they need love and encouragement.  They need the spirit ‘Abdu’l-Bahá showered upon all men,  He is our Exemplar.  A good question for every one of us to ask our own selves is “Would the Master behave like this?”

There are many comforting words of our beloved Master to assist us when we find it is all just too much and we cannot bear it.  “Grief and sorrow”, He says, “do not come to us by chance, they are sent to us by the Divine Mercy for our own perfecting …  Men who suffer not, attain no perfection. …  The more a man is chastened, the

greater is the harvest of spiritual virtues shown forth by him.  Man is, so to speak, unripe the heat of the fire of suffering will mature him. …  To attain eternal happiness one must suffer.  He who has reached the state of self-sacrifice has true joy.”  As most people are suffering, one way or another, a great deal of the time, these words seem to fit all of us.  They fit the pioneer in his trials; they fit the National Assembly members and committee members in their trials, just as real but of a different category; they fit the ill, the diseased, the poor, the downtrodden and abandoned.  They give a glimpse of what life is all about, how to bear with it ourselves and help others to bear with it.

“Whenever you recognize the fault of another,” ‘Abdu’l-Bahá says, “think of yourself  What are my imperfections?and try to  them.”  “Contentment”, He says, “is real wealth.  If one develops within himself the quality of contentment he will become independent.  Contentment is the creator of happiness.  When one is contented he does not care either riches or poverty.  He lives above the influence of them and is indifferent to them.  When we were in Baghdad often with one pound of meat we served dinner to fifteen or twenty people.  We cooked with it Persian stew and filled the pot with water so everyone could have a bowl of soup.  Notwithstanding this we were all very happy and thought that ours was the most delicious dinner.”

In our teachings there is advice for all and for every occasion.  It seems to me the following quotations from ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s words apply equally to those elected to administrative bodies and individuals who find themselves in some position of importance—a pioneer, a Board member, a travelling teacher—as well as just the rank and file:  “The ones in real

authority are known by their humility and self-sacrifice and show no attitude of superiority over the friends.”  “he very first testthat of rejoicing over the success of  neighbour, at which God rejoices.  Only by such a sincere joy can the gift of God descend unto a pure heart y assisting in the success of another servant in the Cause does one in reality lay the foundation for one’s own success and aspirations”  “The friends must be prepared to efface themselves at all times eeking the approval of men is many times the cause of imperilling the approval of God.”  “Let the servant be known by his deeds, by his life  To be approved  God alone should be one’s aim.”  “Ambitions are an abomination before the Lord.”

Sometimes pioneers who have given years of faithful service in the land of their adoption not only get tired and discouraged but get exasperated because they feel that those they have taught, often a whole national community they have established, do not show them enough respect for their services or pay enough attention to their advice.  Yet all parents know that as their children grow up they pay them less respect and follow their advice less often!  Why should they be surprised if their spiritual children follow the same pattern?

Aside from this there are two points to always be borne in mind first, everyone who pioneers, or arises anywhere to teach the Cause of God, should do it for the sake of God, offering this service to God alone and expecting rewards from Him and no one else; if he has any other expectations he will invariably be hurt and disappointed.  Second, he should teach people that now they have accepted the Message of Baha’u’llah their relationship is a direct one to Him; He has become their Baha’u’llah, their own Messenger from God, and their connection with Him is not through the pio-

neer, or some foreign teaching committee in another place; they are on their own; though the pioneer will always be there to love, to help and to advise as needed, the relationship they have now established with the Cause of God is an independent one.  It is really all remarkably like a family:  the child grows up, begins to assert its freedom and the loving parents see it getting hurt and making mistakes which, if only it would listen, ould not happen!  But the child will not always listen and the parents cannot live its life for it.  The Bahá’í pioneer who is a spiritual parent must just resign himself to the same thing.

A short course on how to love your fellowmen

We are so different from each other in this world, by temperament and tastes and the way our minds work, that to love each other because we find the person opposite us sympathetic is often quite impossible; he is not sympathetic and we do not get on with him and consequently find ourselves disliking instead of liking him.  There are three principal remedies for this.  The first and most important is based on some wonderful words of Shoghi Effendi to a Bahá’í who told him quite frankly he did not like and was not happy in the Bahá’í community he was living in.  The Guardian said it was natural that we should not feel drawn to everyone, to people very unlike ourselves whom we really felt no love for at all.  He then pointed out that it was like the members of a large family who were often very different from each other and even disliked each other.  But, said Shoghi Effendi, if each child loved its father truly and deeply, because it knew the father loved all the children and wanted them to love each other, then, because of their love for their father, they would love

each other.  What in fact this means is that we can love each other with a vicarious love, not directly one to another, but with our love for Bahá’u’lláh which fills our hearts like a light, and this light of His love we turn upon others, thus loving them.

The second factor in loving our fellowmen is to take seriously what Bahá’u’lláh says about mentioning the virtues and not the faults of others:  if a person immediately reacts to another person in a negative manner—from not liking his teeth to not liking his mind—naturally he starts out with a feeling of prejudice and tends to go on adding other things to it; any feeling of love becomes only a vague theory; though his conscience says he should love that other person, as a matter of fact he does not do so but still feels antagonistic because all his reactions to that individual were negative from the very beginning, negative and critical.  But if, from the beginning, he had forced himself (and it often takes inner forcing to do it) to see the good points rather than the bad ones, a feeling of warmth, of understanding and love would have been fostered.

The third remedy or factor in learning to love our fellowmen is to appreciate that there is a natural animal-inherited tendency in all species—the human included—to fall back into a feeling of solidarity with its own kind, a drawing away from the other race, the other nationality, the other tribe or class for the paramount reason that they are not like you.  One must always, everywhere in the world, but particularly when pioneering, be on ones guard against this and aware of it for what it is—a strong animal trait inherited from our past.  It is what in the barn yard keeps chickens together with other chickens, ducks with ducks, pigs with pigs, cows with cows—and in their wild state crows with crows and lions with lions!  It is scarcely a quality a Bahá’í should tolerate in

himself!  And yet each one of us is likely—some of the time or, alas, most of the time—to fall into this pit of comparing others with ourselves (to their detriment, naturally) and of condemning their habits and way of life as compared to that which we were used to in our own country.  Far too often in foreign lands one finds a community where all the Persians are lumped together and associate with themselves (talking Persian all the time) and all the Americans associate with each other almost exclusively; yet if you asked either group why they do this you more than likely would hear indignant denials that it was so; we do such things often unconsciously, not even aware we are doing them, just following the old animal pattern inherited from millions of years of evolution:  stick to your kind.  Yet this is just what Bahá’u’lláh has destroyed, saying we are all the leaves of one tree, and that honour is his who loves mankind, not his tribe, his race or his nation.

I have thought about this subject a great deal, for all of us suddenly find we, at some time, are cleaving to our own kind with a sense of relief, self-righteousness and superiority.  I have come to a somewhat prosaic way of getting my own mind back on the Bahá’í track, so to speak.  I play a sort of mental game of what I visualize as matching cards:  an ace comes up, I put another ace on it; a 10 is turned over, I put another 10 on it.  What this amounts to is that whenever I find an event or a fact in the country and among the people I am visiting that arouses a violent reaction of criticism on my part I immediately try to find a counterpart in another part of the world, preferably my own.  I will give some examples:  I get black fungus in my ears and am distressed mentally and physically; my first reaction is a strong reproach to myself:  “Well, of course, if you will travel in the tropics!”  Then I at once remind myself I already had it four times, once in my

own home and my home is not in the tropics!  I have matched card with card—and feel quite calmed down as a result.  I go into a very dirty, smelly wash-room and naturally a feeling of enraged revulsion sweeps over me as I think of all the immaculate toilets in Switzerland and Canada.  Then I recall the unspeakably filthy toilets in many of the capital cities of Europe attached to good restaurants—just as dirty and just as smelly as this place—and I no longer have a quarrel with the country I am in.  I go to bed in the mud hut of a villager and the rain starts dripping on me through the thatch; as I put my raincoat over my mosquito net I begin to consider myself a martyr—until I visualize that time in Montreal when the spring thaw clogged up the roof drains and my newly painted ceiling was ruined, indeed all kinds of pots were set around on the floor catching the water dripping through it; and I remembered doing the same thing dozens of times over the years in the Master’s House in Haifa.  As I travel in Africa, Asia and Latin America I read about the terrific amount of stealing going on, as well as embezzlement of government monies.  I am shocked at these evidences of increasing crime and moral degeneration all about me—until I get a hold of an American or European newspaper and read of exactly the same things taking place there—only on a far bigger and better scale!  The only subject I can never find a parallel for in my own part of the world is the roads.  This matching cards process is a game that will keep you a much better, happier, more useful pioneer or travelling teacher than you ever were before you tried it.  Do not draw away from people, draw towards them; do not exclude, include them.

To not carp on what one disapproves of or finds missing in the new environment is of supreme importance for the pioneer; none of us like to have our country criticized; an “I am holier than thou” attitude is unbearable; so is an “I know

best” attitude.  Which one of us has not been subjected at some time to a guest that looked us and our house and way of life over and showed unspoken if not actually voiced disapproval?  And which one of us in our minds has not silently said, “I wish you were back in your place!  Since it is so much better than mine why don’t you go home?”

The people one will come in contact with in the mass conversion areas, often the so-called developing countries, are extremely sensitive to criticism, spoken or not even voiced.  They seem to almost have a sixth sense that catches radiations, and strong disapproval and criticism can be a wordless radiation that will hurt their feelings, dampen their spirits and cool off their zeal, indeed they can lead to the complete melting-away of the local Bahá’í community.

They are even more sensitive to inharmony, to such an extent that among some native peoples they literally seem to be incapable of standing it; they just cant take it.  As I myself have now become like this I deeply sympathize with them and understand their reaction, which is to withdraw from the sickening and intolerable atmosphere serious inharmony produces.

In His great teaching Tablets of the Divine Plan ‘Abdu’l-Bahá says:  “O ye friends!  Fellowship, fellowship!  Love, love!  Unity, unity!—o that the power of the Bahá’í Cause may appear and become manifest in the world of existence.”

The path of wisdom

“It began to dawn on me that, beyond the teeming romance that lies in the differences between men—the diversity of their homes, the multitude of their ways of life, the dividing strangeness of their faces and tongues, the thousand-fold

mysteries of their origins—there lies the still profounder romance of their kinship with each other, a kinship that springs from the immutable constancy of man’s need to share laughter and friendship, poetry and love in common.  A man may travel a long road, and suffer much loneliness, before he makes that discovery.  Some groping along dark byways, never have the good fortune to stumble upon it.”

Thus wrote Arthur Grimble in A Pattern of Islands, the fascinating account of the years he spent as a colonial administrator in the Gilbert and Ellice Islands of the Pacific Ocean.  I have quoted here some passages from his book because I feel any pioneer who goes out to teach among people still living in their tribal ways, still cast in the proud mould of their ancestors, can well profit by the wisdom and understanding, the gentleness and modesty of Sir Arthur and his ways.

All people have their own manners and my own belief is that villagers have much more strict codes of courtesy than exist any more in urban centres of civilization.  Grimble tells of a Gilbertese saying:  “Small is the voice of a chief” which meant, generally speaking, that gentleness and courtesy should walk hand in hand with power.  He notes how shocked some of the islanders were when the Resident Commissioner, out for a stroll down a village street, had passed between two women holding a conversation from one side to the other without so much as a “by your leave”—to the native people a terrible discourtesy, least expected from the highest ranking person among them.  In western countries to pass between two people speaking to each other, even strangers on the street, without saying “pardon me” is bad manners, but we would not carry it to the point of feeling we need excuse ourselves because we interrupted a line of vision and conversation from one house to another across a street.

It only shows that one should make an effort, in a new environment, to understand at least the main points of the etiquette of the people one is associating with.

Oratory, all over the world, by peoples of many different origins, has been prized as a great art; it was so among the ancient Greeks and equally so among the Polynesians and indeed, as Sir Arthur states, was true of the Gilbertese, to whom he pays this remarkable tribute:  “I have met in the Western Hemisphere no oratory in high debate that could compare, for average pithiness of content, beauty of diction and ease of delivery, with that of those rugged brown fishermen …” and goes on to assert that “… two readings aloud by the Island scribe of any letter or record in Gilbertese were enough to store the whole text of it in their minds forever.  The spread of Mission education brought with it in the long run a natural demand for literacy … yet, excellent as it was, it certainly produced no finer breed of justices or administrators than those unlettered and, more often than not, pagan native magistrates of old …”, pointing out, what I firmly believe from my own experience, that to judge of people’s wisdom, capacity and abilities on the basis of whether they can read and write or not is ridiculous.

“Most Europeans who believe in an after-life draw a clear horizon line between the worlds of the living and the dead.  The pagans of the Gilbert Islands, as I knew them, imagined no such comfortable partition.  The seen and the unseen made but one world for them … the belief was that the more recently departed could and did return …  There was love as well as fear in the ancient cult of the ancestor, and mostly the love predominated …  The sad thing was that the earliest Christian teachers in the Gilbert Islands gave no honour to the spirit of filial gratitude and fatherly goodness that breathed through the old beliefs … indiscriminate derision poured upon

the old ways of thought … had destroyed almost all veneration for the pagan dead. …  Affection had made its exit with respect, and only superstition remained.  Ancient superstitions are not rooted out as easily as ancient loves.  The ghosts of the dead still haunted the villages; the difference was that they had become wholly vindictive now in the belief of everyone but a handful of the dying generation.”

Here Sir Arthur is dealing with the fundamental thinking of many millions upon millions of villagers not only in the Pacific area but all over the world, and his analysis is very penetrating and should not be skipped over lightly.  To deny what we do not understand ourselves, to dismiss as nonsense or superstition other people’s beliefs just because in our society no comparable beliefs of this nature may still exist, is both foolish and unkind.  I remember a conversation with the President of Rwanda, who said he had studied Western philosophy as well as African philosophy and that the difference was that to the Westerner the sequence of birth, childhood, puberty, death and the hereafter was interrupted, broken off by death, but to the African it was one uninterrupted process.  This impressed me very deeply because it is also the Bahá’í viewpoint—life and death are seen as one great, unbroken experience of the soul of man journeying towards his Creator.

Bahá’u’lláh teaches that those who have passed beyond the veil of death can be aware of us and our doings in this world; indeed, He makes the truly astonishing statement that pure and sanctified souls who have passed away assist and guide humanity in this world:  “The light which these souls radiate is responsible for the progress of the world and the advancement of its peoples.  They are like unto leaven which leaveneth the world of being, and constitute the animating force through which the arts and wonders of the world are made manifest …  These souls … have provided, and will

continue to provide, the supreme moving impulse in the world of being.”

When we join to this Baha’u’llah’s teachings on the power of intercession, that we may ask those who have gone beyond to intercede with the Almighty on our behalf; when we add the fact that He teaches that our prayers for the dead, and good deeds done in their name, can speed the development of their souls and increase the spiritual outpourings they receive; we see how inextricably interwoven are this world and the next and, if we take the trouble to ponder upon it tolerantly, can acquire at least a little insight into what ancestor worship means in the lives of millions of people.  It may have become fogged with superstition and distorted through ignorance, but there is, evidently, from our own Bahá’í teachings, an indissoluble and primary bond between us who are here and those who have preceded us into eternal life.

Sir Arthur’s analysis of the forces at play in witchcraft is no less worthy of consideration, for obviously he knew whereof he spoke and any pioneer among primitive people would do well to ponder his words:  “A brown man with sixty generations of terror-struck belief whispering in his blood, and no trust any more in the saving love of his ancestors, and not yet any deep hold on the comforts of his new religion, was easy meat for the death-magic.  The sorcerers had little to learn in practice about the murderous force of auto-suggestion … it is my experience that malice and fear are strong infections.  They can taint things and places, just as human love can sweeten them.  Generation upon generation of sorcerers who willed evil, and of people who dreaded their power, had lived out their lives in those islands.  The piled-up horror of their convictions had achieved, down the ages, a weight and a shadow of its own, an imminence that

brooded over everything.  It was man’s thoughts, more potent than ghosts, that haunted the habitations of men.”

With real understanding of the problems involved in the practices of sorcerers and witch doctors, who, though often in the background nowadays, nevertheless still wield a great influence over people living primitive lives, Sir Arthur points out the profound psychological factors involved, and that to merely deny a thing does not remove it.  Beliefs need to be re-channelled, to be explained, to be re-directed and sublimated, not to be made fun of and scorned as ignorant or heathen.  How often has each one of us had a vivid and disturbing dream, or was seized by a great fear on some subject, or was harassed with negative thoughts and doubts, and gone to someone in the hope he would listen to us and comfort us, only to be told:  “Nonsense, don’t be silly, it’s all your imagination!”  This solved nothing and only left us more miserable and lonely, more prone to weave our fears and doubts into an even worse form than before.

Indeed a harsh and intolerant attitude can be both cruel and self-defeating.  Sir Arthur’s wide experience, over a period of many years, led him to the conclusion that in many instances the missionaries’ fanatical approach to those they believed, perhaps quite sincerely, they had been sent to save, had done much damage to the lives of the people and that “Their indiscriminate hate of everything pagan uprooted by the way much that was beautiful and useful in native custom, and in so doing destroyed agencies that could have been enlisted not only to illumine the message of Christianity, but also to ease the difficult passage of the race from unsophistication to knowledge.”

So heavy can be the impact of our infinitely more complicated and artificial civilization upon people still living in a more or less natural environment and traditional ways that it

can, and has, more than once in the present century, led to racial disaster; as Grimble puts it:  “There have been in the past some grim cases of depopulation … due to the premature blotting out of interests that kept people alive in their environment.”

The freedom of an individual to believe as he pleases should always be respected, including the freedom of a pagan to be a pagan and his freedom to pay his witch doctor for charms or treatments.  An old Gilbertese pointed these values out to Grimble and added:  “If the Government or missionaries could give them something to keep their hearts alive … perhaps they could be happy without ‘their sorcerers …’  But if you cannot give them an equal thing in return, you will kill their hearts by robbing them of their loved wizards.”

It is in the nature of most men to crave a spiritual lightning rod, a protection against the caprice of accident, the convulsions of nature, the evil in other men’s hearts.  One of the first things villagers should be taught is the power in the two shortest of all Bahá’í prayers “Alláh-u-Abhá” and “Yá Bahá’u’l-Abhá”; they should be told that in danger, in calamity, in sorrow or illness the repetition of one of these is a mighty protection and assistance; they should teach it to their children, and even the babes learning to talk, for the power in these words is very great.  They should also be taught to memorize that short prayer, known as the “Remover of Difficulties”:  “Is there any Remover of difficulties save God?  Say:  Praised be God!  He is God!  All are His servants, and all abide by His bidding!”  They should know that one of the prayers given us by Bahá’u’lláh is the repetition, ninetyfive times, of “Alláh-u-Abhá”, and that we may use a string of seeds or beads, ninetyfive in number, to aid us to say this prayer the requisite number of times, without having to count as we go along, by merely passing them through our

fingers till we come to the end.  Indeed, a string of prayer beads makes a very beautiful and special gift from one Bahá’í to another.  If the village people can also be given a tangible thing, such as a photograph of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá or a reproduction of the Greatest Name, to put in their homes, this also is very important.  The Master Himself said to put the Greatest Name and His photograph in ones home as a protection.  A Bahá’í ring, however simple, is a thing many new Bahá’ís long to possess and if cheap enough will gladly purchase for themselves.  It is the immemorial human story, to possess some tangible thing associated with the one we love and revere, particularly from our Prophet, our Manifestation of God, the One come from Him in this new Day.

“The Help in Peril”

No relationship could be more direct and dynamic than that which Baha’u’llah in His Writings shows to exist between the soul of man and his Creator.  Unlike so many philosophies, decrepit and disoriented from their original spiritual inspiration, which believe that if there is a source and motive power for the universe it is an entirely impersonal force with no interest in men and their lives the entire concept of God given us by Baha’u’llah is of a wondrous, all-knowing and loving Being Who is intensely aware of each human soul and has established innumerable ways by which He can guide, assist and bless each soul on its journey through life.  Over and over in His prayers He refers to God as “the Help in Peril”.  What could be more immediate than this, a God Who is ever ready to stretch forth a hand to meet ours in moments of danger and crisis?  Not only should the pioneer or teacher draw upon this assured assistance himself but he should,

unless, exceptionally, he finds the person he is teaching is an atheist, and therefore must be approached in another manner, be sure to convey to those he is teaching the concept of the singleness of Almighty God, that, in addition to having created men, He never abandons them but rather guides and helps them at all times and has always done so, down through all the ages of history, through a series of divinely-sent Prophets.  They must be taught that individually God will hear their prayers and watch over them if they turn to Him for help.  The intimacy that Bahá’u’lláh so clearly teaches exists between God and man must be conveyed to them as a source of both joy and protection.  They (and we, too, to a far greater extent than at present exists among the believers) must realize that the word that comes from the Manifestation of God, the word of God Himself, is entirely different from all other words because it is the Creative Word, endowed with the power of spiritual life and the capacity to regenerate the souls of men.
