That is why it is so important for all National Spiritual Assemblies, and those concerned with getting literature into the hands of the new believers, to ensure that they receive some of Bahá’u’lláh’s own Writings, because in this Dispensation they alone constitute the Word of God.  No matter how essential and illuminating the writings of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá and Shoghi Effendi may be, they do not constitute that bread of life which is divine because it comes from God, and the bread of life today is the Words of Bahá’u’lláh.

My own belief is that one of the things most needed today, which will have dynamic repercussions on those we have already taught, and on non-Bahá’ís as well, is to have the long prayer of Bahá’u’lláh for the dead translated into the local language and given to the friends for their funeral service.  This prayer not only constitutes the Bahá’í funeral service

(to which of course other readings, music, talks and so on may be added as desired) but is one of the most deeply moving experiences a person who has lost a loved one can have.  The fact that each of the six special verses is repeated 19 times has a strangely soothing and comforting effect which is no doubt why Bahá’u’lláh in His wisdom revealed it in this form.  Slowly the cramp of grief and distress relaxes as the wonderful words, like rain, fall again and again on the heart.  I quote it in full here as it appears in Prayers and Meditations of Bahá’u’lláh translated by Shoghi Effendi.

O my God!  This is Thy servant and the son of Thy servant who hath believed in Thee and in Thy signs, and set his face towards Thee, wholly detached from all except Thee.  Thou art, verily, of those who show mercy the most merciful.

Deal with him, O Thou Who forgivest the sins of men and concealest their faults, as beseemeth the heaven of Thy bounty and the ocean of Thy grace.  Grant him admission within the precincts of Thy transcendent mercy that was before the foundation of earth and heaven.  There is no God but Thee, the EverForgiving, the Most Generous.

Let him, then, repeat six times the greeting “Alláh-u-Abhá”; and then repeat nineteen times each of the following verses:

We all, verily, worship God.

We all, verily, bow down before God.

We all, verily, are devoted unto God.

We all, verily, give praise unto God.

We all, verily, yield thanks unto God.

We all, verily, are patient in God.

(If the dead be a woman, let him say:  This is Thy handmaiden and the daughter of Thy handmaiden, etc. …)

Prayer for the Dead

All over the world, in all societies outside of those in big cities and among the ultra-sophisticated and spiritually inert, death is the most important ceremony of a man’s entire life.  Neither puberty, marriage, birth or anything else celebrated by people together compares with the importance of the ceremonies and customs associated with death—the supreme event—when a man goes all alone out into an unknown world, a new life.  None of the existing world religions, philosophies or cults have the concepts about death we Bahá’ís have received from Baha’u’llah’s teachings and as much illumination on the nature of the next world as we have in the Bahá’í writings.  To talk about these things—death and the life after death—I have found is a wonderful way to teach people; there is a hunger in men’s hearts, particularly in this troubled, threatened world of today, to know what death means and what they may hope comes after it.  We Bahá’ís have the most assured and detailed answers to these questions that any people so far have received from God; now is the time to share them.

The real oneness

The Bahá’ís must always bear in mind that generally in the world today any idea of oneness—whether advanced by a

political or a religious group—is based on the concept of uniformity:  “You be like me, accept my philosophy, my way of doing things, my standards in life, and then we will have a united, peaceful world.”  The concept of unity advanced by Baha’u’llah is, on the contrary, based on diversity; this is a very profound difference, and as one is the concept of men, and the other the Divine Revelation of God, it behoves us to truly grasp what Bahá’u’lláh means.

Most people think of unity—the act of unifying a group of people—as a process of exclusion.  The members of a religious sect, for instance, condemning all those who do not adhere to it as inferior, misguided or, even damned, create a feeling of unity based on exclusiveness; tribes, races, nations do the same thing, whereas the entire Bahá’í concept of unity is inclusive, not exclusive; we are fundamentally united with others because we believe they are right too, equally right, whether as a religion having its origin in a Manifestation of God, or as a people or a nation whom we view as our human brothers and sisters with the same rights as our own.  The method by which most men measure others in relation to themselves is to denigrate the other person’s religion, or customs, or country, as the case may be, in comparison to their own.  This method of seeking to raise ones self—whether nation, race, religion or individual—by stepping on the back of the other fellow and trying to push him down, never really works, for as he sinks you sink with him.  It seems to me this is one of the most calamitous of all the unsavoury traits we human beings have, this oblique form of self-praise based on belittling, criticizing and exposing the weaknesses of others in order to show what fine fellows we are in comparison, and is in direct opposition to the clear injunction in our teachings to be silent about the faults of others and mention only their virtues.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá told a story of Jesus Christ

Who, walking with His disciples, came upon a dead dog, at which they all turned away, revolted by the stench, but Jesus went and with His foot pushed away the lip of the dog and said:  “See what beautiful teeth it has.”

The pioneer and the administration

The Guardian very kindly used to give me the minutes of various National Assemblies to read; I can remember a day when I rushed to him, very indignant over something I had read, and said:  “They cannot do that!  I am going to write them!”  Shoghi Effendi calmly informed me that I could not interfere at all, the American National Assembly was no longer my National Assembly, I was now living in another country entirely outside its jurisdiction.  This information came as a surprise because I had not really registered the fact that, of course, having moved out of my native land, I was no longer concerned with that National Assembly or it with me!  Wherever a pioneer, or even a travelling teacher goes, he is under the jurisdiction of the Bahá’í body responsible for that country; he owes it as much love, help, obedience and moral support as he is capable of summoning up.

The pioneer cannot have it both ways; often a pioneer is elected to the Local or National Assembly; no use for him to say of his fellow members “these people”.  From now on it has to be “we”.  This identification, for all of us, is extremely hard to make as I well know from personal experience.  It is just one more thing the individual has to pray about and work on in his own character, and it is extremely important for him to do so, because at all times the characters of human beings are in motion, so to speak; either one is getting better or worse, nearer or farther, gentler or harsher, more

tolerant or more prejudiced.  Pioneers must be constantly on their guard lest as time goes by their identification with the people they have come to live among and teach becomes weaker instead of stronger.  It is a purely animal trait for a species to cling to its own kind and exclude a different species; it is really the projection of this protective animal instinct that has caused the sharp and usually prejudiced and bitter sense of distinction that characterizes the relation of tribe to tribe, nation to nation, race to race.  Very often, the foreigner, the pioneer, is not only, obviously, in the minority, but unconsciously has a tendency to cling to his own kind.  This is such a strong natural instinct that one need not be ashamed of it, rather one should analyze it and struggle against it.  I have seen, East, West, North and South, in various Bahá’í communities, how all the Americans tend to mostly associate with each other; all the Persians do the same; the same language, the same habits and background not only draw them together and make them feel more at home with each other, but it is the line of least resistance, the line that conforms to millions of years of animal behaviour.  This does not mean a pioneer, like other people, should not be free to form his personal relationships; life would be intolerable if he could not do this; it does mean he should be conscious of such psychological pitfalls and try, aside from the natural personal friendships he makes, to establish real relationships with both the people of the country and other pioneers.  Social relationships, such as going and coming to each other’s homes, eating together, baby-sitting for each other, going on picnics together, will help to create the feeling of oneness so essential to a harmoniously functioning Bahá’í community.  This kind of social intercourse is the cement that binds people together and one of the ways pioneers can create active and united Bahá’í communities.

There is another question that should certainly be borne in mind by the pioneer (who will often find that in no time he has become an administrator as well) and that is deprivation of voting rights.  Although the Guardian introduced to us the sanction of removing people’s voting rights—their administrative rights, in other words—he made it quite clear that this was the heaviest administrative sanction we can wield at this stage in the unfoldment of the Faith and that it should under no circumstances be used lightly.  At the World Centre of the Faith we are able to obtain a purview of the Bahá’í world, and over and over again it comes to our attention how lightly and unjustifiably Assemblies apply this heavy sanction.  Not only are they willing to chastise a believer for some minor departure from principle but, for inexperienced and immature Assemblies, it is sometimes a convenient method of getting rid of one of its members who may be considered a thorn in its flesh.  At the present stage of our Bahá’í development, although Assemblies must deal with their affairs on a basis of fairness and justice, this does not mean that love, tolerance, mercy and understanding should not animate them in all their dealings with the Bahá’ís who compose their communities.  The Universal House of Justice, at the same time that it guides and guards the laws and institutions of the Faith, is showering the Bahá’ís of the world with a protective love and comprehension of their weaknesses, their immaturity and their needs, without which no progress in the present stage of their development could be made.  If this Body, whose supreme function is justice, can hold us all in its arms until we are strong and old enough to be more disciplined and walk in the ways that we should, how much more then should National and Local Assemblies feel humble before their tasks and protect, love and help their immature communities and the inexperienced believers under their jurisdiction?

The process of mass conversion is many times a shock to the pioneer and to the travelling teacher.  He cannot understand why a person is accepted as a Bahá’í, so quickly, with so little preparation and no previous “deepening”.  Yet ‘Abdu’l-Bahá Himself—the greatest example we have in every single way of life—has made it quite clear:  “Those souls who have the capacity and ability to receive the outpourings of the Kingdom and the confirmation of the Holy Spirit, they become attracted through one word. …  No sooner is the oil touched by fire than it is ignited, but the heat of fire will not  any effect upon black stone.”  In so few words He makes it clear that no amount of teaching and effort, however prolonged, can enable some people to accept Bahá’u’lláh, and that others cannot be prevented from an instantaneous acceptance in their hearts of Him as the Messenger of God for this Day.

Many times I am asked, by journalists in particular, how a person becomes a Bahá’í, and I explain by one of two doors, the head or the heart.  Each is valid, but the person who enters by one might never be able to enter by the other, and we must accept this fact.  Often in the West people read, ask questions, study for years and maybe in the end become Bahá’ís.  But for others something tells them this is right, they are overwhelmed in an instant by an inner assurance and conviction.  After all, it was this mysterious inner process of acceptance that characterized the conversion of the Báb’s Letters of the Living, who, Shoghi Effendi wrote, “… spontaneously, some in sleep, others while awake, some … through dreams and visions … were enlisted under the banner of a new-born Faith.”  In the course of my travels I have come across many villagers, and others, who have accepted the Faith because of a dream.

Bahá’u’lláh says, addressing God in one of His prayers:

“Thou summonest them to that which will recreate their hearts and immortalize their souls.”  He does not say that which will change their minds and recast their living standards and customs; the emphasis is on the spiritual spark that will be the means of regenerating their inmost beings; once this faith in Bahá’u’lláh’s Message is planted in the heart, from it can grow all the other qualities and convictions necessary if one is to mature into an exemplary believer.

There is no time to lose; wherever there are Bahá’ís, however many they are, it is insignificant in relationship to the world’s population.  The system of Baha’u’llah, His Administrative Order which must flower into a Divinely ordained and guided World Order, is applicable in its entirety only to masses of human beings; society is formed by dealing with vast masses of people; social and economic changes, an enlightened system of education, a healthful and constructive application of science to men’s needs, all presuppose large numbers living according to new values and laws.  Of Bahá’u’lláh’s Dispensation, which must endure for at least a thousand years, over 150 have already passed.  It seems to me it is up to His followers to go out and carry, quickly, His Message to all the peoples of the world, particularly to those remote regions where the hearts of men are still uncontaminated by the virus of our present-day, increasingly corrupt, materialism.  This means ioneering.  Having arrived somewhere as a pioneer, the next step is to concentrate on bringing people into the Faith, as many as possible, through ones personal teaching efforts and through doing all in ones power to assist and stimulate the administrative bodies in ones new land to concentrate on teaching and constantly adding new Bahá’ís, as well as on deepening and educating those who have been enrolled.

In addition to this there is yet another step beyond this to be taken when teaching the Faith.  Baha’u’llah says:

O ye beloved of God!  Repose not yourselves on your couches, nay bestir yourselves as soon as ye recognize your Lord, the Creator, and hear of the things which have befallen Him, and hasten to His assistance.  Unloose your tongues, and proclaim unceasingly His Cause.  This shall be better for you than all the treasures of the past and of the future, if ye be of them that comprehend this truth.

The new believer, it seems, receives not only the blessing of accepting the Manifestation of God but a great honour and privilege is immediately conferred upon him for he now may hasten to Bahá’u’lláh’s assistance and, unloosing his tongue, proclaim His Cause to other men.  Such an astonishing statement deserves to be deeply pondered by all of us for it seems to clearly imply the new Bahá’í not only may, but should immediately begin to teach the Message himself.  If this were not so it is hard to see how we are ever going to bring humanity under the shadow of Bahá’u’lláh.

Hitherto we have had the concept that an individual should be first educated in the teachings and prepared and then enrolled; but it seems this is not the way in mass conversion; the individual, if he wishes to become a Bahá’í—and he should appreciate the salient fact that by doing so he is accepting the Divine Messenger of God for this Day—should be accepted, should be encouraged to teach; and then all the combined forces of the pioneers, the travelling teachers, and the administrative bodies of the Faith, should ensure that his knowledge and understanding of the Faith and its teachings are steadily expanded.

The sequence of events in our Bahá’í teaching activity has

always seemed to me most mysterious, indeed at times puzzling; as already pointed out, we are supposed to teach, and if the hearer wishes to become a Bahá’í we have no right—except for a valid and exceptional reason—to deny him and say that he must wait, he must do this and that first, that now is not the time for him to be accepted as a believer.  There are two reasons why we cannot do this, first, there is no time, and second, we are not the judges of other people’s hearts and their relationship to God’s Messenger for this Day, sent to them just as much as to us.  Bahá’u’lláh is the Baha’u’llah of anyone who believes in Him.  Having been obliged to accept new believers we are informed in our teachings that whenever there are nine or more adult Bahá’ís they must form or elect a Local Assembly on April.  The result of this is that we get a great many uninformed Bahá’ís and a large number of never-functioning or poorly-functioning Local Assemblies.  There is evidently no alternative method of doing it.  If this, then, is our method, given us by those guided by God, then obviously instead of complaining about it we had better get busy and see how we can make it work better:  the teacher, at the initial contact, should teach with more concentrated wisdom and information in his words and then return, as often as possible, to impart more information to the new believers and assist not only in the election of new Local Assemblies but in their organization and functioning.  If he cannot do this himself then he should make every effort to see that some other Bahá’í does.  This is the essence of deepening.

For a long time I have believed that although summer and winter schools and institutes are essential and in many ways of great value to the friends, we will never succeed in what we mean by deepening and consolidation unless we carry some programme of Bahá’í education to the villages.  There

are many reasons for this:  the people who are invited to attend some course somewhere else are invariably very few and must be selected for some reason or other—because they are outstanding, already travelling teachers, or are headmen, or Assembly members, and so on.  Among those who cannot come in to take an advanced course and thus increase their knowledge are usually the women.  My firm conviction is if there are strong Bahá’í women in a village there will be a strong Bahá’í community there.  Often I advise the friends to concentrate on teaching the grandmothers.  The women, in many tribal societies, and particularly the older women, exert a tremendous influence, even though it may be invisible; if they are against something it will be very hard for it to take root, and if they are for something it will be almost impossible to uproot it.  Another group that is reached by taking the course, or institute, or whatever one wants to call it, to the village is the children; they are not only open-minded and intelligent, but usually those adults who are not Bahá’ís, and even have a resistance towards the Faith, will tolerate the children attending the meetings or special classes held just for them.  This type of village teaching, which a pioneer, unless he speaks the language, cannot carry on alone, requires the individual, or the team, to stay some days in the homes of the villagers, or make some other provision for accommodation in a car or a tent, and then hold classes convenient to the villagers.  This might mean during a season when there is no agricultural activity, at night when the men come home from their work or in the middle of a broiling hot afternoon when the mothers are relatively free before they start getting the evening meal.  The teacher should be at the disposal of the villagers and not vice versa, and the local people and Assembly if one exists should be fully consulted and if they come up with any remotely reasonable suggestions the

pioneer should follow them and not start all over with that interminable “We are used to doing it this way …” in America Persia Australia or wherever he hails from!  Until we do this at village level, all over the world, we will never be able to reinforce the grass roots of the Administration.  Needless to say, native teachers, with no outside help, can carry on such courses to excellent advantage.  But the presence of a foreigner, a pioneer, has great value not only in demonstrating the oneness of mankind but in adding an attraction to the course which one of themselves—the native teacher—does not provide.  However, my own observation has been that a well-informed Bahá’í of his own country can teach the Faith and answer questions often not only as well as but better than we foreigners can.  All people who still live closely in contact with nature have to have their wits about them to survive; the wilderness or forest is no place to be vague or absent-minded!  The concise thinking, clarity and wisdom of villagers’ minds is a never-ending joy to me.

Although “visual aids” may be of use they are far from being a universal cure.  Many typically American and European visual aids are becoming available for the teaching work.  Undoubtedly many of them are of real assistance in illustrating a point or enabling people to grasp a concept.  It does seem to me though that increasingly National.  Assemblies, and particularly the native Bahá’í teachers of a country, should choose what they feel is suitable and useful for their own people.  One cannot go into such a big subject in detail here but certain facts may be of help:

Childish games and gadgets to illustrate a theme can be well-received by some people and insulting to others, such as the people in an African village we heard about who, after being treated by the visiting Bahá’í to a sample of

western visual aids, angrily asked, “Are we children?  Let them talk to us!”

Many of the examples we use in countries with temperate climates—such as the “Divine Springtime”, the barren “winter” at the end of a religious Dispensation before a new “springtime” appears—are useless in areas of the world around the equator because there are no seasons, no one has ever witnessed a spring, so the comparison means nothing.

To play a game with Bahá’í cards may be under some circumstances educational but to throw dice with it is, to me, not only very undignified, but also creates a very bad impression indeed, for in many villages, not to mention cities, gambling games are played with dice; in fact in our own western civilization it is really impossible to disassociate dice from the element of chance and gambling.

Far too many slide shows and films and coverage of Bahá’í events, both in what one sees and what one hears, are far too sophisticated and geared to a mentality that is exposed to not only cinemas but also to television.  A villager who has never heard of television and never seen a film in his life simply cannot understand or follow pictures that convey innuendos or oblique references to a thing symbolically or that jump about from scene to scene.  On the contrary, the deep joy and wonder and satisfaction one sees in villagers’ faces who are given a slide show about the Faith, with every little thing carefully pointed out and explained, is most touching.  They can neither understand nor want to hear a long rigmarole about the history of the Faith and this reference and that to it; it is too far away from their experience and understanding.  Above all, they do not want the pictures to jump from subject to subject or change fast; they just want to sit and be told, “See, that is where Bahá’u’lláh lies buried, that is His Tomb, our holiest Shrine, and we go

in by that door, we approach by that path, those are the beautiful gardens around it, that is the building He died in, see that window on the balcony there?  That was His room.”  I must confess, I, too, enjoy that kind of a show!  I am tired of sophistication.

Pioneers, through an intelligent contemplation of the countries they have gone to live in and the people they are called upon to teach, can be a great help in assisting indigenous methods of teaching, ideally adapted to the mentality of the local people, and thus foster the development of unity in diversity, not unity in uniformity.

Stories, to people all over the world, have a unique fascination.  People everywhere, Bahá’ís and non-Bahá’ís, w sit for hours if someone w only recount to them the details of Bahá’u’lláh’s life, the Báb’s martyrdom, the Master’s words and deeds and travels, how the martyrs died, the saga of this or that famous Bahá’í’s activities.  Among so many new Bahá’ís there is a deep hunger for more information, not just to know more administrative rules, or more of the teachings, but to know all those warm, alive, endearing details associated with the lives of the Central Figures of their newly accepted Faith and with its heroes and heroines.  This is as it should be; for 2,000 years people have lived over and over the life of Christ and His apostles; to tell the wondrous story of such things means as much today as it did in the past.  This is history in stories.

There is another kind of story and that is the illustration; one stresses ones point by giving an example; ‘Abdu’l-Bahá Himself used this method a great deal and His stories and examples are fascinating; but one can make up ones own examples and they are often very effective if one uses something before the audience’s very eyes, such as we are all the leaves of one tree and its branches, and one points to the tree

the meeting is being held under and elaborates the theme.  This is one way that the village teachers of the country excel us in teaching, because they know not only how the minds of their hearers work, but also what examples are in their own tradition and therefore strike them more forcibly.  It took me ages to learn to simplify both my language and my thoughts in order to be effective in village teaching.  It has had a wonderful effect on my own mind, for all my philosophical, involved, abstract, big-word meanderings had to give place to understanding and expressing something as clearly and concisely as I could, both for the sake of the interpreter and the sake of my hearers.  How many times, as I listened to my own forcible and lucid exposition of the Faith I reconverted myself!

Public contacts

Under this heading comes the press, television, radio, public lectures, invitations, meetings with officials and our own general attitude.  Obviously all these require much more detailed treatment than can be given in these pages, but here at least are some suggestions and hints.

To proselytize—that is to unduly insist on ones own beliefs and bring pressure on other people to accept them—is forbidden in the Bahá’í Faith.  We have the right, nay the obligation, to let other people know that this is the Day of a new, long-awaited Manifestation of God.  In doing this we should try at all times to be discreet, courteous, amiable and open-minded towards others and their viewpoint.  Nothing is more distasteful than fanaticism.  The beloved Guardian used to say that we Bahá’ís are “orthodox but not fanatical”.

We must not argue with other people.  Occasionally I have

found that during the question period in my lectures someone begins to dispute with me; when I find we are about to have an argument I say, “Argument is forbidden in my religion, I have no desire to argue with you, you are quite free to hold your own views and I to hold mine.”  Bahá’u’lláh Himself has made this very clear in His own words:  “Beware lest ye contend with any one, nay, strive to make him aware of the truth with kindly manner and most convincing exhortation.  If your hearer respond, he will have responded to his own behoof and if not, turn ye away from him, and set your faces towards God’s sacred Court …  Dispute not with any one concerning the things of this world and its affairs, for God hath abandoned them to such as have set their affection upon them.  Out of the whole world He hath chosen for Himself the hearts of men …”  He furthermore exhorts us:  “Sow not the seeds of discord among men, and refrain from contending with your neighbour …  Open … the city of the human heart with the key of your utterance.”

It is not necessary to have a question period at the end of a lecture, and my advice to speakers who may not have had much experience is to avoid a question period until they feel they are skilled enough to handle it properly, or to arrange the programme in such a way that another Bahá’í, if better able to do so, will reply to the questions.  Very often one of the local believers knows the mentality of the people better than a pioneer or visitor does, and can do this much more satisfactorily.  This does not apply to village meetings, where all the friends will be disappointed if they cannot have this more intimate contact and discussion with the visiting teacher.

Over and over Shoghi Effendi advised the Bahá’ís to study the talks of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá and follow His example.  The Master’s talks (which in the West were invariably translated)

were very short, simple and direct and He often underlined His point by telling an anecdote or a story; He seldom dealt with more than one or two subjects at a time.  A great mistake all of us make, in lectures and in encounters with people, is to try and tell them every single thing we can think of about the Bahá’í Faith all at once!  In some lectures, to some audiences, a comprehensive survey of the teachings may be indicated, but generally speaking it is better—particularly where at least half of ones time is taken up by the translator—to stick to a very few sentences about the history of the Faith, and a short outline of what we call its basic principles, and then place special emphasis on the principle of progressive revelation because it is this concept that places the Bahá’í Faith on the stage of history and helps the hearers to grasp the grand—and usually to them unheard of—concept of the over-all scheme of God:  one God, educating one humanity, through what is fundamentally one religion, all down the ages.

The Guardian, in a small pamphlet entitled The Faith of Baha’u’llah, a World Religion, which was a statement he prepared as Head of the Faith for the United Nations Special Committee on Palestine, at its invitation, in 1947, has given the most perfect and complete short outline of what this Faith represents that we will ever have.  I quote it herewith in full so that it may be available to the friends for easy reference in making their own outlines of their talks, which is what I myself often use it for.  In addition to this, it is an excellent introductory pamphlet to give to scholarly people; the English is too advanced, however, for the average non-English-speaking person to understand unless he has had a very good education in that language.

The Faith established by Bahá’u’lláh was born in Persia about the middle of the nineteenth century and has, as

a result of the successive banishments of its Founder, culminating in His exile to the Turkish penal colony of ‘Akká, and His subsequent death and burial in its vicinity, fixed its permanent spiritual center in the Holy Land, and is now in the process of laying the foundations of its world administrative center in the city of Haifa.

Alike in the claims unequivocally asserted by its Author and the general character of the growth of the Bahá’í community in every continent of the globe, it can be regarded in no other light than a world religion, destined to evolve in the course of time into a world-embracing commonwealth, whose advent must signalize the Golden Age of mankind, the age in which the unity of the human race will have been unassailably established, its maturity attained, and its glorious destiny unfolded through the birth and efflorescence of a world-encompassing civilization.

Though sprung from Sh‘h Islám, and regarded, in the early stages of its development, by the followers of both the Muslim and Christian Faiths, as an obscure sect, an Asiatic cult or an off-shoot of the Muhammadan religion, this Faith is now increasingly demonstrating its right to be recognized, not as one more religious system superimposed on the conflicting creeds which for so many generations have divided mankind and darkened its fortunes, but rather as a restatement of the eternal verities underlying all the religions of the past, as a unifying force instilling into the adherents of these religions a new spiritual vigor, infusing them with a new hope and love for mankind, firing them with a new vision of the fundamental unity of their religious doctrines, and unfolding to their eyes the glorious destiny that awaits the human race.

The fundamental principle enunciated by Bahá’u’lláh,

the followers of His Faith firmly believe, is that religious truth is not absolute but relative, that Divine Revelation is a continuous and progressive process, that all the great religions of the world are divine in origin, that their basic principles are in complete harmony, that their aims and purposes are one and the same, that their teachings are but facets of one truth, that their functions are complementary, that they differ only in the non-essential aspects of their doctrines and that their missions represent successive stages in the spiritual evolution of human society.

The aim of Bahá’u’lláh, the Prophet of this new and great age which humanity has entered upon—He whose advent fulfils the prophecies of the Old and New Testaments as well as those of the Qur’án regarding the coming of the Promised One in the end of time, on the Day of judgment—is not to destroy but to fulfill the Revelations of the past, to reconcile rather than accentuate the divergences of the conflicting creeds which disrupt present-day society.

His purpose, far from belittling the station of the Prophets gone before Him or of whittling down their teachings, is to restate the basic truths which these teachings enshrine in a manner that would conform to the needs, and be in consonance with the capacity, and be applicable to the problems, the ills and perplexities, of the age in which we live.  His mission is to proclaim that the ages of the infancy and of the childhood of the human race are past, that the convulsions associated with the present stage of its adolescence are slowly and painfully preparing it to attain the stage of manhood, and are heralding the approach of that Age of Ages when swords will be beaten into plowshares, when the Kingdom promised by Jesus Christ will have been established, and the peace

of the planet definitely and permanently ensured.  Nor does Baha’u’llah claim finality for His own Revelation, but rather stipulates that a fuller measure of the truth He has been commissioned by the Almighty to vouchsafe to humanity, at so critical a juncture in its fortunes, must needs be disclosed at future stages in the constant and limitless evolution of mankind.

The Bahá’í Faith upholds the unity of God, recognizes the unity of His Prophets, and inculcates the principle of the oneness and wholeness of the entire human race.  It proclaims the necessity and the inevitability of the unification of mankind, asserts that it is gradually approaching, and claims that nothing short of the transmuting spirit of God, working through His chosen Mouthpiece in this day, can ultimately succeed in bringing it about.  It, moreover, enjoins upon its followers the primary duty of an unfettered search after truth, condemns all manner of prejudice and superstition, declares the purpose of religion to be the promotion of amity and concord, proclaims its essential harmony with science, and recognizes it as the foremost agency for the pacification and the orderly progress of human society.  It unequivocally maintains the principle of equal rights, opportunities and privileges for men and women, insists on compulsory education, eliminates extremes of poverty and wealth, abolishes the institution of priesthood, prohibits slavery, asceticism, mendicancy and monasticism, prescribes monogamy, discourages divorce, emphasizes the necessity of strict obedience to one’s government, exalts any work performed in the spirit of service to the level of worship, urges either the creation or selection of an auxiliary international language, and delineates the outlines of those institutions that must establish and perpetuate the general peace of mankind.

The Bahá’í Faith revolves around three central Figures, the first of whom was a youth, a native of Shíráz, named Mírzá ‘Alí-Muhammad, known as the Báb (Gate), Who in May, 1844, at the age of twentyfive, advanced the claim of being the Herald Who, according to the sacred Scriptures of previous Dispensations, must needs announce and prepare the way for the advent of One greater than Himself, Whose mission would be according to those same Scriptures, to inaugurate an era of righteousness and peace, an era that would be hailed as the consummation of all previous Dispensations, and initiate a new cycle in the religious history of mankind.  Swift and severe persecution, launched by the organized forces of Church and State in His native land, precipitated successively His arrest, His exile to the mountains of Ádhirbáyján, His imprisonment in the fortresses of Máh-Kú and Chihríq, and His execution, in July, 1850, by a firing squad in the public square of Tabríz.  No less than twenty thousand of His followers were put to death with such barbarous cruelty as to evoke the warm sympathy and the unqualified admiration of a number of Western writers, diplomats, travelers and scholars, some of whom were witnesses of these abominable outrages, and were moved to record them in their books and diaries.

Mírzá Husayn-‘Alí, surnamed Bahá’u’lláh (the Glory of God), a native of Mázindarán, Whose advent the Báb had foretold, was assailed by those same forces of ignorance and fanaticism, was imprisoned in Tihrán, was banished, in 1852, from His native land to Baghdad, and thence to Constantinople and Adrianople, and finally to the prison city of ‘Akká, where He remained incarcerated for no less than twentyfour years, and in whose neighborhood He passed away in 1892.  In the course of His

banishment, and particularly in Adrianople and ‘Akká, He formulated the laws and ordinances of His Dispensation, expounded, in over a hundred volumes, the principles of His Faith, proclaimed His Message to the kings and rulers of both the East and the West, both Christian and Muslim, addressed the Pope, the Caliph of Islám, the Chief Magistrates of the Republics of the American continent, the entire Christian sacerdotal order, the leaders of Sh’h and Sunní Islám, and the high priests of the Zoroastrian religion.  In these writings He proclaimed His Revelation, summoned those whom He addressed to heed His call and espouse His Faith, warned them of the consequences of their refusal, and denounced, in some cases, their arrogance and tyranny.

His eldest son, ‘Abbás Effendi, known as ‘Abdu’l-Bahá (the Servant of Bahá), appointed by Him as His lawful successor and the authorized interpreter of His teachings, Who since early childhood had been closely associated with His Father, and shared His exile and tribulations, remained a prisoner until 1908, when, as a result of the Young Turk Revolution, He was released from His confinement.  Establishing His residence in Haifa, He embarked soon after on His three-year journey to Egypt, Europe and North America, in the course of which He expounded before vast audiences, the teachings of His Father and predicted the approach of that catastrophe that was soon to befall mankind.  He returned to His home on the eve of the first World War, in the course of which He was exposed to constant danger, until the liberation of Palestine by the forces under the command of General Allenby, who extended the utmost consideration to Him and to the small band of His fellow-exiles in ‘Akká and Haifa.  In 1921 He passed away, and was buried in a vault in the

mausoleum erected on Mount Carmel, at the express instruction of Bahá’u’lláh for the remains of the Báb, which had previously been transferred from Tabriz to the Holy Land after having been preserved and concealed for no less than sixty years.

The passing of ‘Abdul-Baba marked the termination of the first and Heroic Age of the Bahá’í Faith and signalized the opening of the Formative Age destined to witness the gradual emergence of its Administrative Order, whose establishment had been foretold by the Báb, whose laws were revealed by Bahá’u’lláh, whose outlines were delineated by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá in His Will and Testament, and whose foundations are now being laid by the national and local councils which are elected by the professed adherents of the Faith, and which are paving the way for the constitution of the World Council, to be designated as the Universal House of Justice, which, in conjunction with me, as its appointed Head and the authorized interpreter of the Bahá’í teachings, must coordinate and direct the affairs of the Bahá’í community, and whose seat will be permanently established in the Holy Land, in close proximity to its world spiritual center, the resting-places of its Founders.

The Administrative Order of the Faith of Bahá’u’lláh, which is destined to evolve into the Bahá’í World Commonwealth, and has already survived the assaults launched against its institutions by such formidable foes as the kings of the Qájár dynasty, the Caliphs of Islám, the ecclesiastical leaders of Egypt, and the Nazi regime in Germany, has already extended its ramifications to every continent of the globe, stretching from Iceland to the extremity of Chile, has been established in no less than eightyeight countries of the world, has gathered within its pale repre-

sentatives of no less than thirtyone races, numbers among its supporters Christians of various denominations, Muslims of both Sunn and Sh‘h sects, Jews, Hindus, Sikhs, Zoroastrians and Buddhists.  It has published and disseminated, through its appointed agencies, Bahá’í literature in fortyeight languages; has already consolidated its structure through the incorporation of five National Assemblies and seventyseven local Assemblies, in lands as far apart as South America, India and the Antipodes—incorporations that legally empower its elected representatives to hold property as trustees of the Bahá’í community.  It disposes of international, national and local endowments, estimated at several million pounds, and spread over every continent of the globe, enjoys in several countries the privilege of official recognition by the civil authorities, enabling it to secure exemption from taxation for its endowments and to solemnize Bahá’í marriage, and numbers among its stately edifices, two temples, the one erected in Russian Turkistan and the other on the shore of Lake Michigan at Wilmette, on the outskirts of Chicago.

This Administrative Order, unlike the systems evolved after the death of the Founders of the various religions, is divine in origin, rests securely on the laws, the precepts, the ordinances and institutions which the Founder of the Faith has Himself specifically laid down and unequivocally established, and functions in strict accordance with the interpretations of the authorized Interpreters of its holy scriptures.  Though fiercely assailed, ever since its inception, it has, by virtue of its character, unique in the annals of the world’s religious history, succeeded in maintaining the unity of the diversified and far-flung body of its supporters, and enabled them to launch, unitedly and systematically, enterprises in both Hemispheres, designed

to extend its limits and consolidate its administrative institutions.

The Faith which this order serves, safeguards and promotes, is, it should be noted in this connection, essentially supernatural, supranational, entirely non-political, non-partisan, and diametrically opposed to any policy or school of thought that seeks to exalt any particular race, class or nation.  It is free from any form of ecclesiasticism, has neither priesthood nor rituals, and is supported exclusively by voluntary contributions made by its avowed adherents.  Though loyal to their respective governments, though imbued with the love of their own country, and anxious to promote, at all times, its best interests, the followers of the Bahá’í Faith, nevertheless, viewing mankind as one entity, and profoundly attached to its vital interests, will not hesitate to subordinate every particular interest, be it personal, regional or national, to the overriding interests of the generality of mankind, knowing full well that in a world of interdependent peoples and nations the advantage of the part is best to be reached by the advantage of the whole, and that no lasting result can be achieved by any of the component parts if the general interests of the entity itself are neglected.

Nor should the fact be overlooked that the Faith has already asserted and demonstrated its independent religious character, has been emancipated from the fetters of orthodoxy in certain Islamic countries, has obtained in one of them an unsolicited testimony to its independent religious status, and succeeded in winning the allegiance of royalty to its cause.

“It is like a wide embrace,” is Queen Marie of Rumania’s own tribute, “gathering together all those who have searched for words of hope.  It accepts all great Prophets

gone before, it destroys no other creeds and leaves all doors open. …  The Bahá’í teaching brings peace to the soul and hope to the heart.  To those in search of assurance, the words of the Father are as a fountain in the desert after long wandering. …  It is a wondrous message that Bahá’u’lláh and His son ‘Abdu’l-Bahá have given us.  They have not set it up aggressively, knowing that the germ of eternal truth which lies at its core cannot but take root and spread. …  It is Christ’s Message taken up anew, in the same words almost, but adapted to the thousand years and more difference that lies between the year one and today …  If ever the name of Bahá’u’lláh or ‘Abdu’l-Bahá comes to your attention, do not put their writings from you.  Search out their books, and let their glorious, peace-bringing, love-creating words and lessons sink into your hearts as they have into mine.”

“The teachings of the Bábís,” wrote Leo Tolstoy, “… have a great future before them ...  I therefore sympathize with Bábism with all my heart, inasmuch as it teaches people brotherhood and equality and sacrifice of material life for service to God …  The teachings of the Bábís which come to us out of Islám have through Bahá’u’lláh’s teachings been gradually developed, and now present us with the highest and purest form of religious teaching.”

“Take these principles to the diplomats,” is the late President Masaryk’s advice, “to the universities and colleges and other schools, and also write about them.  It is the people who will bring the universal peace.”  “The Bahá’í teaching,” is President Eduard Benes’ testimony, “is one of the great instruments for the final victory of the spirit and of humanity …  The Bahá’í Cause is one of the great moral and social forces in all the world today.  I

am more convinced than ever, with the increasing moral and political crises in the world, we must have greater international coordination.  Such a movement as the Bahá’í Cause which paves the way for universal organization of peace is necessary.”

“If there has been any Prophet in recent times,” asserts the Rev. T. K. Cheyne in his The Reconciliation of Races and Religions, “it is to Baha’u’llah that we must go.  Character is the final judge.  Baha’u’llah was a man of the highest class—that of Prophets.”  “It is possible indeed,” declares Viscount Samuel of Carmel, “to pick out points of all creeds.  That is the essential purpose of the Bahá’í religion, the foundation and growth of which is one of the most striking movements that have proceeded from the East in recent generations.”

“Palestine,” is Professor Norman Bentwich’s written testimony, “may indeed be now regarded as the land not of three but of four faiths, because the Bahá’í creed, which has its center of faith and pilgrimage in ‘Akká and Haifa, is attaining to the character of a world religion.  So far as its influence goes in the land, it is a factor making for international and inter-religious understanding.”

And, finally, is the judgment passed by no less outstanding a figure than the late Master of Balliol, Professor Benjamin Jowett:  “The Bábí movement may not impossibly turn out to have the promise of the future.”  Professor Lewis Campbell, an eminent pupil of Dr. Jowett, has confirmed this statement by quoting him as saying:  “This Bahá’í Movement is the greatest light that has come into the world since the time of Jesus Christ.  You must watch it and never let it out of your sight.  It is too great and too near for this generation to comprehend.  The future alone can reveal its import.”

The statistical information in this statement (made over half a century ago) needs to be updated as follows:  the literature of the Faith has been published in over 802 languages; its National Spiritual Assemblies now number 182, of which 148 are legally incorporated or registered; the races and tribes represented within it exceed 2,112 and it has spread to 236 independent countries, territories and major islands of the globe.  When asked how many Bahá’ís there are in the worldthe correct reply is the one Shoghi Effendi himself always said we should give, namely, the number of centres where Bahá’ís reside which, at the present time, is approximately 128,000.  In mentioning Bahá’í Templesthe ones in Frankfurt, Germany; Sydney, Australia; Kampala, Uganda; Panama City, Panama; Apia, Western Samoa; and New Delhi, India should be added to the one in Wilmette, in the United States; if any question is asked about the first Bahá’í House of Worship, erected in Russia at the beginning of the twentieth century, the reply should be that it was destroyed some years ago by a severe earthquake in ‘Ishqábád.  The above figures can be brought up to date annually as new statistics are released by the Universal House of Justice.

I myself often quote that brilliant sentence that the Faith is “essentially supernatural, supranational, entirely non-political, non-partisan, and diametrically opposed to any policy or school of thought that seeks to exalt any particular race, class or nation”, and as most people know what “supernatural” means but have never heard of “supranational”, I give the illustration that it is like the sky, which covers everything, the whole earth.  People are delighted to add this new and interesting word to their vocabulary and it drives home the concept of our Teachings in relation to the whole world.  In places outside Europe and North America it has been my experience that in meetings and receptions given by

Bahá’ís the public is not anxious to leave.  To decide that one is going to give a thirty or forty minute talk, with a ten minute question period, or to decide that an hour and a half should take care of the reception and make another engagement for after that time is up, does not work out at all.  Sometimes one feels impelled to speak for an hour or even longer, sometimes there are no questions, and sometimes one cannot get the audience to stop asking them and let the tired, often non-Bahá’í chairman go home, not to mention ones self!  People can come over an hour late to the reception in ones honour—or not at all!—and remain, having a lovely time and eating everything in sight, long after one had calculated the whole thing would be over.  The way to meet these things is to be relaxed, resigned to ones fate and as friendly amiable and patient as one can manage to be often at the point of utter exhaustion!  Probably, in more than one moment of zeal, the pioneer has prayed, “May my life be a sacrifice”; occasions such as these are the pay-off—one of the pay-offs!

Most people are conservative and in Africa and Asia and many other places there is a definite formality on all public occasions; they dress formally, the men often wearing ties and jackets with their suit, the women their best.  For the foreign Bahá’í to slouch in in slacks or jeans or a sweat shirt is definitely a test to those of the country, and surely a wholly unnecessary one, as courtesy, and to dress properly, are enjoined in the teachings; if the travelling Bahá’í does not possess anything formal in the line of clothing, then he can at least make an effort to appear as neat and clean and well-groomed as possible.  In all societies, everywhere in the world, to honour ones guests is customary and also to honour ones host.

In many village as well as town meetings a careful pro-

gramme has been arranged with every detail planned and often this is written or even typed, and the visitor gets his copy.  I remember one on which I was item seven and invited to speak fifteen minutes.  I waited my turn, needless to say, but I spoke a lot longer than fifteen minutes—having at that point travelled well over 20,000 miles to get there!

If a gift is presented, it should be graciously accepted and thanks given.  I recall an occasion on which a pioneer who was with us refused to allow villagers to present a gift of food to take away with us, saying we did not need it but they did.  The intention was good but the act was insulting to our hosts; the best thing is to accept gracefully.  Among some groups of people to present this gift of food is a time-honoured tribal custom and to refuse it is humiliating to them and their ways.  In this connection it is well to remember that if the pioneer or travelling teacher is going to stay with a Bahá’í family, or in someones home, to take a gift of food, such as sugar or tea or some canned goods or something else, is both courteous and acceptable.  One has to have enough perception and fine feeling to sense what is the right thing and tactful thing to do and one should also inquire a little into local customs.  But under all circumstances the most important thing to do is not to offend or hurt others.

In all such matters one must seek to find the proper balance.  Baha’u’llah tells us:  “o man should enter the house of his friend save at his friend’s pleasure, nor lay hands upon his treasures nor prefer his own will to his friend’s, and in no wise seek an advantage over him.”  This is a very strong statement and it certainly protects the rights of the individual not to be imposed upon by unwanted guests or guests he cannot afford to even feed.  In many places of this world the rural people well know that to take a traveller in—any traveller—can mean the difference between life and

death, particularly in areas where water is very scarce and where their kindness and hospitality is literally life-giving.  But there are occasions, and I have seen many of them, when travelling Bahá’ís seem to think that part of the duty and function of other Bahá’ís with homes is to take them in and house and feed them.  This surely is not the right attitude nor according to Bahá’u’lláh’s explicit injunction.

In press interviews and radio and television programmes the focus is usually on one of two things:  the individual as a person—who are you, why are you here, what do you think about our country, where are you going?—or it is directly on the Bahá’í Faith.  It has been my experience that as time on radio and television is usually brief—often about five minutes spot news—the main thing is to get over certain key words, to say at least once, clearly, “Bahá’í” and founded by “Bahá’u’lláh”, in some way mentioning His name, and to state if possible the great central themes of our Faith, namely, progressive revelation, peace, world unity, the oneness of mankind and the abolition of all prejudices; of course if one has time to say more, to give the basic principles and an idea of how widespread the Faith is today, all the better.  I have long since decided that in these public contacts, whether lectures, press interviews, or radio and television talks, the supremely important thing is that people should be left with the impression that “Bahá’í”—one hopes they get at least that word into their memories—is a good thing.  We want to create waves of friendliness, not unfriendliness towards the Faith; we want people to say, “Bahá’í?  Oh yes, I once heard something about that.  It is a good thing.”  More than this, in superficial contacts, we cannot hope for.  Never antagonize, never criticize, never be negative.  Often one starts to say something and is never given a chance to finish, so one should avoid any negative approach, mistakenly believing

one can explain what one means later on.  Sometimes I made a point to praise the work of missionaries, for two reasons:  first, because of the many wonderful services they render mankind and second, because it is very difficult to attack someone who praises you, you find you have been disarmed.

When a pioneer or a local community decides to give a reception and invite important guests to honour a Bahá’í visitor or notable, or on some other special occasion, it should be well planned, with proper invitations either printed or written by hand and sent out well ahead of time to ensure that those invited have had enough notice; if government and municipal officials, ambassadors or consuls are to be invited, as well as other prominent people and leaders, they must know at least a week or more in advance, otherwise they will have other engagements.  I have attended more than one reception to which almost no one except the Bahá’ís came because of the fact that the invitations were not properly issued or sent sufficiently ahead of time.  Another point to remember is that as far as possible the Bahá’í reception should not come at the same time as some other reception or event to which all these officials will automatically be going; if an embassy is giving a party, or there is some municipal or government event taking place, priority will be given to it by all this class of people and they will not turn up at the Bahá’í reception.  I find a great many mistakes Bahá’ís make are just because they do not do what other people do and they do not inquire as to proper usage.  In this connection it is well to remember that one should not invite both ambassadors and consuls; generally speaking higher-ranking officials are insulted if lower-ranking ones are also invited and it may be sufficient for them not to come.

For those who may not be familiar with the accepted wording of a formal invitation, here is the general pattern:

The Spiritual Assembly of the Bahá’ís of Chicago

requests the pleasure of the company of

His Excellency the Greek Ambassador and Mme. Peter Appolo

at a reception in honour of

Mr. Horatio Smith

to be held at the Masonic Hall, 119 West Portfolia Street

on Thursday, January the tenth

at 5 p.m.

r.s.v.p.

This is only an example; the top line could be the name of the pioneer, if he were giving the reception as an individual, or the name of the National Assembly if it were giving the reception.  In any case the set-up is the same; the line where the guest’s name comes is left open and the name written in by hand.  In big cities the hour of the reception should also be carefully chosen to make it convenient for the maximum number of people to attend; a reception can, in principle, be held at any hour; at whatever hour is chosen some refreshments must be served; these can be expensive catering, or bottles of pop and sweet biscuits, depending on where, when and who!  It is customary to put r.s.v.p. in the corner (an abbreviation of the French expression to please answer this invitation and say if you are or are not coming) but, alas, nowadays people seldom respect this and one has to calculate about how many people there may be.  In almost every town of any size one can find a little print shop to print these invitations and often the printer may have a form to show one which can be adapted and copied.  One can get good

advice in such matters from a consulate or people who are connected with foreign companies and have experience in such formal social occasions.

In planning a public lecture one should likewise be careful to choose the hour day and place which will ensure the greatest success, and as far as possible suitable advance publicity should be given and people invited at a time some other competitive event is not taking place, otherwise the effort, and often the expense of renting a hall, is wasted and through lack of forethought the pioneers and local believers are disappointed that their big event fell flat.

In meeting with officials one should be extremely careful what one says.  Higher officials, the President, Ministers and Members of Parliament, should not be contacted without advice from the National Assembly of that country or, failing the possibility of this if no meeting is scheduled or some other real hindrance exists, then at least asking the advice of one of the Counsellors or a Board member.  There are often delicate and important relationships being established by the Bahá’í administrative bodies with officials, often under the direct guidance of the Universal House of Justice itself, and all Bahá’ís, including pioneers and travelling teachers, must remember that they are under the jurisdiction of the National Assembly of the country they are in and have no right to seek interviews with such high officials without its permission.

My own experience is that officials and reporters invariably want to know how many Bahá’ís there are in the world.  The Guardian said that we should not give any numbers—indeed we do not know them to give—but rather state the widespread nature of the Faith.  In other words the answer is that because in some countries Bahá’ís are still suppressed, and we cannot take a census, and in many others are living in rural areas and are illiterate, or are nomads, we have no

exact lists, but what we do know is that we now have Bahá’ís everywhere in the world, in about 128,000 centres; we should inform people that we are accredited to the United Nations as a non-governmental organization with consultative status, pointing out that this is a privilege which we take full advantage of by attending and taking part in conferences, mentioning the latest ones if we happen to know them.  There are also likely to be very pointed questions as to “How many Bahá’ís are there in this city?  In this country?”  The best, and strictly truthful answer to this is, “I do not know.  I am a visitor here, but I understand there are many Bahá’ís (or a few)”, depending on which statement is more accurate.  My personal policy is that if I find my questioner is antagonistic—and he may well be so as a devout Christian or Muslim—I am doubly careful not to be provocative.

If the person one is talking to is a reporter, remember that no matter what one says he is likely to print things the way he wishes or sees them, and if the person is an official he may, through ones indiscretion or carelessness, get an entirely prejudiced idea of the Faith which can do it a lot of harm and undo the painstaking efforts of the local Bahá’ís and older pioneers.  If the travelling teacher or pioneer decides to write an article on the Faith for publication, he should remember it must first be submitted to a proper Bahá’í body for approval.  Through precautions such as these we can protect the Cause at the same time as we promote it.

In connection with publicizing the Faith it should be borne in mind that the “Bahá’í Faith” is the correct term to use and not “Bahá’í World Faith”.  The Universal House of Justice, in a letter to a National Spiritual Assembly written in 1967, made this quite clear, stating that:  “The designation ‘The Bahá’í Faith’ is more dignified and is preferable.  Any adjective added to this name tends to a diminution of its

stature and might be taken to mean there are other ‘Bahá’í Faiths’.”

It is extremely important, as already pointed out, for the Bahá’ís to avoid “confrontations” with other people on the Faith.  If a violent attack on it is made, one can rise to defend it in a meeting or to correct a flagrant misstatement of facts in the press.  But to seek out the followers of other religions, or their priests, and attempt to argue it out, and demonstrate that Bahá’u’lláh and His teachings are right, is not proper and can do nothing but harm.  Likewise to frequent mosques and churches in order to argue and debate with others regarding our teachings is certainly wrong; such acts, far from creating goodwill towards the Bahá’ís and the Message of Bahá’u’lláh stir up trouble, alienate people, and prejudice them against us, not only temporarily, but in villages for many years to come.

Another point to be borne in mind is that in this age of abbreviations, when increasingly so much is being reduced to a kind of verbal shorthand, and initials are taking the place of words, we Bahá’ís seem to have fallen into this same bad habit and glibly refer to the nsa, itc, cbc, ntc, rtc, av and Lord knows what!  Although some Bahá’ís may know what all this stands for, some of us do not, and certainly people in other countries do not, for the Spanish French or Swahili for “nsa” would be entirely different and no one could dream it meant “National Spiritual Assembly”, the highest body of a community of Bahá’u’lláh’s followers in any given area.  If it is confusing (not to mention unnecessary and undignified) for Bahá’ís themselves to hear all these abbreviations, they mean nothing at all to non-Bahá’ís and it is both rude and irritating to other people for us to constantly refer to things in a kind of code, more particularly so as we would like them to know what our Bahá’í institutions are

and to admire and respect them.  If the purpose of using abbreviations is to economize on words, I suggest this can be done by going back to a simple, direct use of small English verbs and nouns:  instead of saying, “he proceeded to embark on his transportation at the conveyance terminal”, just say, “he got on the bus”.  Instead of saying, “the rhetorical hyperbole displayed by Professor Smith in his dissertation”, just say, “Professor Smith spoke very well”.  In foreign lands, at any rate, this clarity and simplicity will be much appreciated and make it a great deal easier to teach the Bahá’í Faith.

Patience

Nothing is a better training for the soul than the amount of patience that has to be summoned up when one goes out as a pioneer or travelling teacher.  Strictly speaking, although the merit in being a pioneer is far greater—having abandoned ones home and native land to serve God—the patience required of a travelling teacher exceeds even that required of a pioneer!

Always we must keep before our eyes a fact, not a theory, that over two billion of the almost six billion people in this world are living a life atypical to the minority.  Urban and suburban life—and to a great extent the rural life of Western Europe and North America, as well as the life in the vast metropolises of this world such as Rome, London, Paris, New York, Teheran, Calcutta, Rio de Janeiro, Tokyo, and so on—is not shared in at all by the majority of the world’s population who still live in villages or as nomads.  Strangely enough, during the various plans that have formed part of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s great Divine Plan, it has usually been the city individual, from an advanced-standard-of-living environment, who has arisen to serve in far places and written his

or her name in letters of gold in the annals of the Formative Age of our Faith.

The point is that whereas people living in the highly competitive urban atmosphere of our present devastatingly materialistic civilization of the West are governed by clocks, by a perpetual sense of haste and of fear of not being the first, ahead on time, the vast majority of the human race do not even own a clock or a watch see no reason to hurry, and therefore go through life much more relaxed, sweet-tempered, ulcer-free, and high-blood-pressure-free, than their city counterparts.  Bahá’ís pioneering or travelling in Africa, Asia, Latin America and the Pacific, must realize this fact.  It is, I believe, of supreme importance for them to appreciate it and accept it because constantly they are going to be subjected to what is for them, at least in the beginning, a great strain, and the strain is far from being confined to clock problems!  Take some of the things that happen (chosen at random) as one travels or lives in these places so different from the places some of us come from, yet so fascinating, interesting, and warmly human.

You call the hotel room boy to come clean your room while you are still in it because you have so many papers and bits and pieces lying about that you prefer to keep your key with you when you go out.  Although the hotel you are staying in is one of the best in that big Latin American city, the boy has no bucket to wash the bathroom floor, so he uses the wastepaper basket; he also has no cloth, so you see him take the used face towel he is going to change anyway and clean the toilet and floor with that, knowing the “clean” one he carefully places on the towel rack was used for the same purpose yesterday!  He has no dust pan, but neatly sweeps the debris across the wall-to-wall carpeting and out into the corridor.  Usually such a servant is tidy and clean according

to the possibilities given him, very courteous and, if you treat him decently, probably honest.  Why, you ask yourself, does the hotel give him no bucket, dust pan, floor cloth or vacuum cleaner?  The answer is because it will be mislaid or pilfered in no time, or the employee has no sense whatever of how to treat a machine, and an expensive electrical appliance will soon be ruined.  You steam with impatience while the servant thoroughly, slowly, accomplishes his task by methods you heartily disapprove of.

You have an appointment to speak at a public or Bahá’í gathering, yet even in a place like Latin America’s capital cities, your chairman may inform you that he will drive you there half to three-quarters of an hour after the time the meeting is scheduled to begin so that you will not be kept waiting while the audience straggles in.

You, a travelling teacher, limited in time, having perhaps given your vacation to render this service, are handed a schedule of meetings arranged for you in villages and towns; with some, or a lot of difficulty, often by local bus or taxi service, often even entirely alone in a strange country, you arrive at your destination to find either no Bahá’ís gathered, the only person whose name you had to contact has unexpectedly had to go out or go away, there was a death and all the villagers are at the funeral, there is a football game or a political rally—or, or, or!

So many personal (and in retrospect!) very endearing memories come back to me:  such as the meeting where we sat in an open courtyard, after driving 80 kilometres to get there, and waited for over an hour for the Bahá’ís to turn up; a mother hen and her chicks, one pullet and two ducks were the only living things that came!  Finally, after asking a housewife who was cooking her supper in a corner of the yard if she spoke any French, and ascertaining she did, I

asked her to tell anyone who might eventually come, that we had been forced to return to the city as night was falling.  We heard later that forty people came—but when!  This incident was far from unusual but in this case part of the blame fell on the arrangements made as the meeting was scheduled to start when most of the Bahá’ís were still at their employment and could not get away.

Aside from such contingencies there is the ever-present one of the road and the meeting.  I recall an extreme example of this when a National Assembly (who love me very much and whom I love) arranged a meeting ahead of time; it had rained during the night and I asked the member with me, who had already been there a few days before, if the road was passable.  Oh, yes! quite passable, and there were over a hundred believers there.  It was a eight mile trek, in and out, in mud-filled ruts over the axles of the Land Rover.  Without the Land Rover I would never had made it, and my arms and shoulders ached for days afterwards from the effort of holding the heavy car to the slippery road and forcing her over it in low gear, four wheel drive.  We finally arrived to find four people gathered under six trees on a hillside.  I was really irritated that time and added my own steam to the already steaming atmosphere of the countryside!  But an old man, a new Bahá’í, chastened my spirit by thanking me most courteously for coming, and saying:  “We new believers are like children, we need care and to be visited and taught more.”  I have never forgotten him or his words and spirit, and would drive to that rendezvous gladly again to see him.  Often in our disappointments are hidden precious rewards.  What reward could be greater than for a Bahá’í pioneer to hear an old man, an African villager, say:  “I lost my heart.  I had no heart and the Bahá’ís came to my village and gave me back my heart; they gave me back my heart—thank you.”

Another experience, by no means uncommon in some places, was our arrival, dead tired after driving in the intense heat for hours, at a farm where many friends had gathered for the meeting.  Our hostess, after we had driven into the back yard and she was sure we were there, counted noses and went off to cook the lunch for everyone (boiled plantains), which was then served three hours later when we were faint with exhaustion and hunger.  It was a very happy occasion, and the hardship has singled it out in my mind as such.

Experiences like these teach us patience.  Without it one had better stay at home, for anger, impatience and irritation will do no good but only ruin ones own nerves and digestion, and hurt, alienate and puzzle the native people of the country one is a guest in.

One not only needs patience with the climate, the roads, the food and often the living conditions, as well as the people who live in a totally different time world from our own—usually the villagers’—but the pioneer or travelling Bahá’í must muster his patience and fortify himself with it and with prayers when he has any work to do in a foreign country with either government officials or in government offices.  This is really a point of great importance, for usually, somehow, he is recognized as a Bahá’í; Bahá’ís are better known than they think and often observed covertly with intense interest and curiosity—and sometimes animosity herefore it is absolutely essential to be polite, patient and share a warm word or smile with the people one is dealing with.  One of the most wonderful things about Africa—where a white person can sometimes find he is being paid back for a long history of the way his race emphasized it was white to the detriment of the black race—is that a warm smile almost invariably will be met by a smile in response.  It is one reason I miss Africa so much.

My personal experience is that in almost no case does a public servant consider he is there to serve you as a member of the public; he is much more likely to treat you as someone over whom he temporarily has power and is determined to make you feel this.  Because of this almost universal attitude of petty officials it is much better, if at all feasible, to try to see the person at the top.  This is particularly true of things that concern important Bahá’í business and not merely ones own personal affairs.

“Culture shock”

This term—one being bandied about at a great rate these days inside Bahá’í circles—irritates me extremely.  It is typical of the new quasi-scientific jargon being increasingly used in our modern society, as if what it describes had been recently invented, and no one ever—under another name—had the same thing a million years ago!  It means, as far as I can discover from listening to its silly application, that a person leaves his own place, goes to another, different place, and, as the place he left was something he was used to and had its own characteristics—in other words constituted his “culture”—and the new place he has arrived at is different, he has a “shock”, a “culture shock”.  So what?  Why make it sound so important?  The young stork that was hatched in Scandinavia, when he arrives in Egypt with his parents to spend the winter, must also have a frightful culture shock, and the salmon spawned up in a cold northern river must get a jolt when he gets down to the warmer salt waters of the sea, but both species do it annually—and hundreds of others—and tremendous differences of environment are experienced by each new generation of such animals.  It is all,

evidently, perfectly natural and no one, before this new term came along, ever made much fuss about it.  I never heard that my ancestors who migrated to North America from Scotland and England suffered “culture shock”, nor do we read it in the diaries of the pioneers who crossed America in covered wagons and were shot at by Indians.  It was different, but it was not a shock; to adapt to things that are different is innate in man, the most highly adaptable creature on the face of the earth!

The pioneer, before he starts out, should make up his mind that quite naturally things are going to be different and that this is one of the great charms of and incitements to go pioneering.  He need have no “shocks”; he undoubtedly will have many surprises, and sometimes things that happen will, indeed, be shocking, but shocking things already happened to him in the course of his life at home and will happen to him again if he goes back.  Whenever people use this term “culture shock” it always seems to be applied to and arouse all ones sympathy for the person who arrives at his pioneer post.  I have never heard anyone talk about the formidable “culture shock” we foreigners must constantly present to the people of the countries we are visiting!  Is it because we are so over-civilized, so spoiled by our materialism, so arrogant about our accomplishments and so conceited that only we are shockable?  When I think about our manners, the way we dress and what we insist on eating and the insulting criticisms we make openly of the people different from ourselves whom we meet, I am horrified at what they must think of us.

One day, when visiting a tiny village of Bushman families in the Kalahari Desert, a young college student who was with us, eager to buy an ostrich egg, went around and gathered up a number and reappeared with them all in his arms, asking me which was the best?  I was horrified and said “You
