didn’t go into their houses and take those out?”  It seemed he had.  When I was his age I would probably have done exactly the same thing, but I asked him how he would feel if someone went into his room in his home and took out his belongings without so much as a “by-your-leave”?  It would be a rare tribesman who would offer any of us a similar inconsideration and discourtesy.  On that occasion, who received the greater “culture shock”, we or the Bushmen?

The more one comes to know different men in different places the more one grows to appreciate the fact that we are all the same, all the leaves of the one tree—humanity.  I love people’s differences, of colour, dress, hair, houses, customs, music, art, language food, but I never have been able to feel that any human being was different from me inside; the likenesses between all men are so deep and uniform that the superficial differences fade into insignificance and only remain to add charm and distinction  never to divide us from each other.

Let us analyse this and prove it is true.  Suppose someone is asked what is your sister Jane like?  Would one reply she is a pair of brown canvas shoes, a blue wool skirt, a cotton blouse, three daisy hair clips, one cheap wrist watch, an imitation gold ring and a necklace of artificial pearls?  Is that really Jane or are these her outward trappings?  One would have to at least say she has two hands and two feet, is five feet tall, has two brown eyes and wavy hair, a nice nose and thick lips, and so on.  But still this would not be Jane.  One would have to say she has a loving nature is very musical has quite a little temper of her own her memory is good she likes sports she is a good cook, but a little heavy with the salt and is inclined to be a bit lazy by nature.  The deeper one goes into the description of a human being, the more it can be a picture of anyone in the world.  We must see each others

likenesses so as to feel no strangeness between us and then see each others superficial differences as part of the unending beauty of God’s creation.  Such an understanding of our fellowmen is based on the teachings of Bahá’u’lláh; the oneness He has pointed out as being absolutely fundamental; the differences are not only part of life and its charm but constitute the very shaping of life’s forces so as to make each individual, each group, each tribe and race different and therefore capable of making unique and precious contributions to the whole, to the one world of the future.

How to speak

As language is a means of conveying thought, the first step is to have a clear idea of what one means before trying to express it.  In other words, to focus ones mind on ones thought and try not to have it all blurred around the edges.  Practise clarity.  From the standpoint of teaching the Faith, it seems to me that this involves a study of the Teachings.  But supposing a person has neither the time nor means to do this properly; then at least he should try to order his thoughts and disentangle different, seemingly contradictory teachings, getting their outlines clearly in mind.  To give an example of what I mean:  on one hand are the many teachings about justice Bahá’u’lláh says justice is loved above all things and teaches us that society rests on two pillars, reward and retribution; yet on the other hand He exhorts us to love and forgive in our relationship to each other.  These two things, justice and love, seem equally stressed in His Writings and equally important  et, if our thinking is blurred, we find we are advocating love and mercy towards criminals convicted of heinous crimes, who well deserve the rigour of the

cold justice of the law  supposedly dealing “justly” with our fellowmen in our personal contacts with them, judging and condemning them and often sanctimoniously setting ourselves up above them as superior  hat we are enjoined to do in the Teachings is to show them kindness, tolerance, love, and to look upon them with a sin-covering eye!

The second step is to use, at least most of the time in our daily contacts and in our talks to people whose native language is not English, plain and simple words and simple verbs.  The pompous, puffed up, verbose pedantic misuse of words today is rapidly becoming a contagious disease.  For example, the weather bureau over the radio wants to tell us it may “rain” later in the day, but what it actually announces is that there will be “precipitation activity”.  If one wants to say “I am sorry I am late” or “I am early”—short, clear, easily understood sentences—do not change them into “behind schedule” or “before schedule”, which are terms informative and useful for an air terminal a railway or bus station but not for villagers and ordinary people who, if they know any English, know straight, simple, classic English—in fact in many cases Biblical English—but not modern American or the journalese jargon that passes for English in too many publications.

The over two billion m of the human race are for the most part extremely polite people, exemplifying Bahá’u’lláh’s injunction to be courteous; aside from this, people have their own pride and will seldom admit they do not understand what someone is saying to them.  If asked, they may politely say “yes”, because in a great many societies, in both Africa and Asia, to say “no” is considered extremely discourteous if a person is addressing someone higher in rank than himself, such as a guest, a teacher, a foreigner and so on.  Through heedlessly and inconsiderately

using his own “jargon”, whether it be mixed with slang or just the modern, high-brow, long-and-complicated-word usage now so much in vogue, the Bahá’í who has gone out to teach the Message of Bahá’u’lláh defeats his own ends, his primary object, which is to convey to other people what Bahá’u’lláh has said and what His Revelation means.  What is the use of going to a village and giving a talk if the translator, and the other few people who may know some English, cannot understand more than 20 to 50 per cent of what the speaker says?  It should be one of the pioneer’s primary objectives to be understood by his hearers, and he should work at it so as to not only use simple words but learn to have a clear and direct delivery of his thoughts.

The third step is to learn to speak relatively slowly, pronouncing all the syllables of a word clearly, and neither dropping ones voice at the end of the word or swallowing it, that is mumbling it.  One hears people these days all too often say “go’n” for “going”, “thinkin” for “thinking” (not to mention “hi’yer” for “how do you do”), and other unintelligible contractions.  This clear enunciation makes a tremendous difference to ones hearers, not only in giving talks but if one speaks on the radio, is being translated, or is just conversing with another individual.  The foreigner should always remember that if he finds it difficult to understand a person who is talking to him in his own language (which I frequently do!), that other person may be having even more difficulty in understanding him.  Sometimes I tell people quite frankly, “I am sorry, I did not understand what you said”; in the question period of public talks I often ask my native, or at least local chairman to re-state to me the question, saying I did not catch it clearly; naturally I try always to give the impression it is my fault I did not hear or understand clearly so as not to offend the other person.  What is the use of going

to the end of the world to teach if the pioneer or teacher does not ensure that he can be understood by someone who at least speaks his own language when he gets there?  Strangely enough, I have noticed many times that in foreign countries the natives understand more easily a Persian who is speaking English than a person whose native language is English.  I think this is because that Persian, or other person whose language is not English, and who may not speak it grammatically or well, is speaking more simply and clearly just because it is not his own language.  In India and other Asian countries one often finds a perfect English spoken, where the real value of the words has been perpetuated in the schools and in general usage  onsequently although the accent may be very poor the language is beautiful and puts the way many young people speak in America, who have received a higher education, to shame.  In Africa, thanks to the education mostly received in mission schools, the people often have a wide knowledge of English based on the Bible, and because of this have little difficulty in reading Shoghi Effendi’s beautiful translations of Bahá’u’lláh’s Writings.  But by and large the best thing for any Bahá’í to do when he goes to another country is to make an intelligent effort to be sure what he says is clear, well-pronounced, and simple.

‘Abdu’l-Bahá, in His Tablets of the Divine Plan written over eighty years ago, urged the Bahá’ís not only to go forth all over the world to pioneer but to first learn the language of the people among whom they planned to teach.  Very few people have ever been able to achieve this degree of preparation; most have gone forth knowing only their mother tongue; as it is so extremely urgent for the Bahá’ís to arise disperse and teach the Message of Bahá’u’lláh uring the five Plans  have already implemented the Master’s Divine Plan, emphasis has not been placed so much on learn-

ing the languages as getting up and going.  However, a far greater effort should be made by pioneers on arrival to learn the language of the country they settle in, or at least the regional language of the area they are teaching in; it makes all the difference, in every way, when a pioneer can speak directly to the people.  I remember years ago in a conference in Panama one of the very devoted South American Indian Bahá’ís said, wistfully, that their languages must be very difficult because no pioneer ever learned them.

If the pioneers cannot comply with the advice of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá that the “teachers travelling in different directions must know the language of the country in which they will enter”, then at least let them make a serious effort when they get there to learn them.  There are in most countries concentrated courses available where one can quickly master the native language  it is astonishing the degree to which pioneers—and the committees sponsoring them—ignore these facilities, along with ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s own instructions, and either make no attempt to learn the native language or dawdle along trying to “pick it up”.  Not everyone can master a new language but the people who cannot do so are the exception and not the rule  odern methods one  learn much faster than ever before.  At least the pioneer or travelling teacher should make an effort to learn the common phrases of “how do you do”, “good-bye”, “thank you” and so on.

Meetings

Meetings all too often seem to consist of a steady procession of “comings” and “goings”, late arrivals, early departures, someone taken by an afterthought or an urgent necessity to get the child out.  One must just learn to suffer all this pa-

tiently and amiably.  As I find that delivering a talk requires intense concentration on my part, especially when I am speaking in French or being translated into another language, this constant state of flux in an audience disturbs me very much.  Incidentally, it is characteristic of almost all village audiences to easily have their attention removed from the speaker; a car passing by can draw all eyes as if it were the latest jetliner coming into an airport, and even a pedestrian walking along has the same effect.  One way to handle this is to just stop talking and quietly wait until the disturbance is over or to turn ones own head and goggle at the bus, along with ones hearers, in a relaxed and human way, until the diversion has passed.  I am sorry to say many times the ones who were most disturbing to the audience were the pioneers themselves  would fidget, whisper, and bounce up in response evidently to some urgent brain wave that impelled them to do something other than sit still and show a good example of paying attention.  If someone takes a photograph or fiddles with a tape recorder, the entire audience gives him its undivided attention, usually when one is about to drive home ones most important point!  The audiences I have found most capable of sustained attention are those in India, South East Asia, and the youth in North America.  But in spite of all this shifting about, I have found that many villagers will listen and ask intelligent questions for hours on end until one is ready to drop with fatigue  ften their questions will revert to something that was said at the beginning of the meeting, indicating that in spite of the various digressions that occurred, they were not only listening to but retaining what the speaker said.  As they are mostly illiterate, their memories are excellent and they can carry abstract thoughts without the help of visual aids—very clearly in their imagination.

Laziness and customs and clocks

In many relatively primitive societies, particularly in Africa, the men seem to do almost no work while the women labour hard in the fields, gather and carry heavy loads of firewood, fetch water, often from long distances, to the village in containers on their heads, do all the cooking and housework, and not only sell in the markets but are often regular merchants.  There is, at least in Africa, a good explanation for this:  for thousands of years there has been constant tribal fighting; no outsider realizes to what an extent the African people have wandered over their continent and migrated from place to place; stronger tribes pushed out weaker ones or conquered them, even enslaving them; many large and powerful kingdoms emerged and dominated an area as big as one of the present African republics; indeed, even in these republics today one can find an Ashanti King and an Emperor of the Moshis and other very powerful traditional Chiefs.  In these societies the men were the warriors, constantly on the alert to defend their villages, crops, women and children and cattle.  There is no longer, in modern states, any real need for them to do this, but the division of occupations between men and women—the old pattern of “women’s work”—has remained for the most part intact.

In connection with the historic background of Africa, it does no harm to remember that it was the Africans themselves who were the great slave catchers, selling what became known as the precious “black ivory” to both the Arab and European slavers who exported this human merchandise East and West.  When I found the attacks, usually in question periods in universities, on the white race and colonialism getting too violent and out of proportion, I would remind the students that Africans as well as whites battened on the slave

trade, and that evil and criminal as has been in many ways the history of the white man’s relationship to Africa, he nevertheless was responsible for Africa’s blooming and important position in the present-day world; without the white man, the Europeans in particular, it is hard to see how such a vast and inaccessible continent could have been developed so fast.  A dispassionate exposé of facts goes far to dispel the red and steamy clouds of prejudice—no matter whose prejudice it may be!

In societies where the economy is of the simplest nature, where clocks and watches are non-existent or a rarity and soon get out of order in the intense humidity or extreme dryness of the climate, as the case may be, the entire tempo of life is different from our own hagridden sense of the passing of not only hours but minutes, the time sense which dominates the lives of our over-civilized urban populations and creates the deluxe diseases of ulcers, high blood pressure, heart attacks and mental and nervous breakdowns.  So drastically different from ours is the rhythm of this other almost timeless way of life that it takes real imagination and patience on the part of the foreigner to adjust to it.  This effort, however, must be made, otherwise the pioneer or teacher becomes exasperated, critical, antagonistic and impatient, and completely defeats his own ends with the very people he has left his home to serve.

The pioneer must remember that he not only can get used to these things, he must get used to them!  One day I remember a very nice Bahá’í friend came to our hotel to take us on an errand, as we had arranged; we were not quite ready so he said he would be back in ten minutes; he came back ten hours later—said he had had a flat tire!  I heard from a friend, whose husband had been an ambassador to a South-east Asian country some years ago, that when they formally invited peo-

ple to dinner they would often come not only some hours late but even some days later!  This complete indifference to clocks and to appointments strictly bound to clocks in our own part of the world demands a truly erculean adjustment on the part of some of us.  In some places there seems to be—where clocks do exist—a positive antipathy to punctuality.  Many is the time in Latin America (and other places!) when I have waited an hour or so for the audience to arrive at my scheduled lecture.  Strangely enough I found African audiences more punctual than Latin American ones.

Another thing that cannot be emphasized enough and which is not only common in societies where the time sense is different from ours but also throughout the Near and Far East, is that people simply do not say “No” or “No, I cannot come” or “No, I will not do it”.  “No” is extremely impolite and will just not be said.  It will not only not be said but in its place one is likely to get a “yes” as “yes” is polite!  Years ago, talking to an oriental believer who constantly kept up a polite “Yes, yes”, I began to suspect something was wrong and so asked her point blank “Do you understand what I am saying?”  She was so taken aback by this sudden attack that she inadvertently burst out with “No, madam!”  One may not care for this kind of politeness but one has to be tolerant of it because it is other people’s way of doing things and their traditional courtesy.  In Latin America—and other places—one may meet a fellow Bahá’í and say, “Will I see you at the Feast tonight?” or “Will you come to the gathering in my home on Friday?” or even invite him to a meal and fix the time and the answer will be an assurance that he will come even though he may already know he cannot or will not be there!  This is aggravating but does no real damage except to ones own feelings and convenience.  What does do damage is if, while lecturing and teaching the Faith, the hearers do

not understand but will not admit it.  The best way around this is to either have a little “review” of some of the points, to ask some of the friends to explain what was the point, in their own words, so as to be sure they did understand or to ask one of the native believers to tell you frankly—when you get him alone—if you were really speaking clearly enough so as to be understood.

Problems of giving

For those who arise and leave the so-called “affluent society”—poverty stricken though it be in spiritual and moral values—and go forth to spread the Message of Bahá’u’lláh in “backward” or “deprived” areas—usually rich in spiritual and moral values the contrast between what they were used to at home and what they see about them is often unbearably painful.  Here are people who often have nothing at all except what they can grow or make themselves.  They will often ask for help, individually and collectively, from requesting that the Bahá’ís open a school for their children to a tentative hand placed on your dress or your earring and a laughing demand, “Give it to me?”.  Some villagers are shameless in their importunity and some extremely proud and reserved.  In other words they are human beings, for all of us know shameless people at home and also very reserved and proud people who would never ask for anything under any circumstances.  There are no minute rules to follow in giving or not giving, but much observation and experience has led me to be convinced of certain things which I believe are fundamental.

The only remedy to the manifold ills of this world, in the smallest village or the biggest city, is the Teachings of Bahá’u’lláh in their entirety; His system, in other words.

When a river is in flood its destructive power attacks left and right and people do what they can to save themselves, but it is no solution at all, the solution is to remove the flood waters at their source, to restore things to normal.  The only solution for the problems of the human race is that it should accept its Lord for this Day and apply His laws.  We Bahá’ís neither have the manpower, the financial resources nor any other material powers sufficient to carry out such social programmes as opening schools, giving economic aid, medical services and so on—we not only cannot provide such things but the pioneer or travelling teacher must be very careful not to promise them.  I remember a large meeting, in a remote area, for which a number of villages had combined, raising an elaborate canopy of tree pillars and branches to shade the audience and generally going to a great deal of trouble; after my talk, when questions were asked, they wanted to know when we would start the school they had been promised by a very prominent Bahá’í traveller who had been there some months before.  They were waiting for it, and the land was set aside for it.  Knowing perfectly well that neither the National Assembly of that country nor any one else was either contemplating such a project or had the funds to carry it out, I had to try and explain to them the difficulties involved; they were angry and sullen, considering a promise had been broken.  Such events are extremely bad for the Cause; they are a great test to the local believers, in this case a newly opened zone, and can lead to their not only abandoning the Faith but becoming antagonistic.  One cannot blame them, one must blame the folly of any Bahá’í who goes around making rash promises, either because he wants to attract people through them or appear a jolly good fellow in their eyes.

In a religion, one of whose cardinal principles is universal education, the pioneer or visiting teacher must be par-

ticularly careful what he says on this subject.  Again, I feel it is very important for each one of us to think things through clearly before talking about them because misunderstandings can easily arise, particularly on a subject that means so much to people thirsting for an education, as this principle does.  A great many of both the laws and principles of Bahá’u’lláh cannot be put into effect yet because they presuppose a changed society in which there are sufficient Bahá’ís to shape its conduct according to His Revelation; we also believe in universal suffrage, a universal language, an international currency, a parliament of nations and so on.  The point is that the teacher or speaker at a village meeting must be very careful not to give the impression that this principle of universal education means the Bahá’ís will open a school there.

At all times the travelling teacher or pioneer must remember he is under the jurisdiction of the National Spiritual Assembly of that country and has no right to promise any material benefits, such as a school, a local centre, that a well will be dug, that a child will be sent out to school or a sick person to hospital, unless he has been informed by the National Assembly that such an undertaking will indeed take place and his promise be honoured.

The friends must realize that such problems as these are one of the reasons for the creation of the Bahá’í administration, so that resources and work can be coordinated and the pitfalls of individual decisions on matters that do not concern the individual alone can be avoided.

The best way to handle the personal demand for something of ones own is, I find, to be perfectly frank.  Sometimes the demand may be based on a real need, but very often it is just “trying it on”, having a little fun and experimenting to see what can be got out of the traveller free, and showing off in front of any one of their friends that may be

watching.  Villagers, it must be remembered, are shrewd and parsimonious themselves; they invariably bargain for anything they get, expect to do their best on the one hand to beat the other fellow down and gain as much as possible by the transaction, and on the other not to be fooled or imposed upon—in other words, they have the same mentality that is required, in other circles, to make a famous financier.  Therefore either ignore the matter completely by silence, or say, “I am sorry, I need it myself”; if they say, “Give it to me, I like it”, say, “So do I, that is why I bought it”; or, as in the case of the ladies who ask for ones dress or jewellery, try putting your hand on their things and saying, “I want that!”  They usually get the point amidst riotous peals of laughter.

Whatever one has will be wholly inadequate to meet the material needs of almost any poor village.  The best general solution is to give the Teachings of Baha’u’llah and the news of His advent, this is the most priceless gift of all, it is God’s Own gift to every one of His creatures today and they must be made to understand that they are being offered it by right and not as an act of charity or condescension on the traveller’s or pioneer’s part, that it has no strings attached, no material benefits to be obtained from it, that through accepting it their people’s, our own people’s, all humanity’s problems will be ultimately solved by a God-imposed world solution.

I heard the following true story from a Bahá’í who had pioneered in Latin America, and requested him to write it down for this book because it so truly and tragically illustrates the points I am trying to make:

The red sweater

After an extensive trip through Mexico a Bahá’í couple, with the purest of motives, decided that action was

needed—not words—to help alleviate to some degree the suffering of humanity as they saw it.

Therefore, with a well-organized slide show they toured some southern states and collected clothing to give to the needy ones of Yucatan, Mexico.

Their trip was successful, as one can imagine; when the tenderhearted Bahá’ís saw the pictures of naked and half-naked children and adults they loaded up a truck as full as could be managed, and took it to Yucatan.  It was with great difficulty that they got through customs.

It has been several years since Operation Clothes took place, yet the heartache and unhappiness even today still lingers on.

For example, one person says, “Those people from Texas didn’t love me because I wasn’t home and I didn’t get anything.”

From having had to work over the same area after this gift-giving, I can only say that unless we have a red sweater to give to every single soul in Mexico we had better not give anything except the Greatest Gift of all, the Cause of Baha’u’llah, because this we can indiscriminately offer to not only every human soul in Mexico but to every soul on the planet; and it never leaves in the recipient, heartache and unhappiness.

Children

Throughout Africa, and in many places in the Western Hemisphere and elsewhere, it is the custom to carry the latest baby on the mother’s back, sitting astride and supported by a shawl that goes around the woman’s shoulders or under her arms and is securely tied in front.  This goes on until the baby

is well over a year old or even more.  To see a woman in Africa without a baby on her back is almost the exception rather than the rule.  Even in the big capital cities one will see a Europeanized African mama in modern clothes shopping in a supermarket with baby on her back in a smart lace shawl!  If mother is not available for the moment, a tiny girl—sometimes only twice the size of the baby—or even a little boy, is carrying baby instead.  This is a very common sight in the villages and one marvels at how these children can carry such a heavy load when they are so small themselves.  It seems to do them no harm for they all grow up strong and straight and crazy about babies.

This seems to other people of other lands a strange, tiring, entirely unnecessary custom, but after years of travel among people who do this I have come very seriously to believe that one reason neither the African nor the American Indian is a nervous, high-strung, irritable individual—characteristics he lacks to a marked degree—is because the children have this bodily warmth, this complete sense of “security” in their infancy.  “Insecure” has become a common psychological term these days; in many hospitals in the West volunteers come in daily to take babies in their arms and give them this muchneeded sense of warmth and nearness and “security” for a few hours, because they have found it makes a big difference to the baby’s health.  Other primates, mostly the monkey and ape population of the world, carry their babies on the mother’s body until they are past infancy.  So at least let us look with interest and respect at this custom, so different from our own of shoving the baby off in a crib or play pen or nursery so mother can be free to work or to play or assert her own personality unencumbered by the nuisance of the baby.  Who knows, science may yet come around full cycle to believing in the million-year-old wisdom of nature.

One often hears the lament, mainly from those pioneers living in cities, that there is no one to teach and no one is interested or comes to the meetings.  There are always children to teach and children are always interested.  I remember one pioneer who made a point of getting into conversation with little street urchins, of which there are many in towns and cities where the children have nowhere else to go; from six years up she would stop and converse with some bright-eyed and curious imp, telling about the new Day, telling about Bahá’u’lláh as if the child were an equal and had a right to hear it.  It was enough for her to appear in the street to get her amiable and friendly little audience about her.  She claimed that not only did the children have a right to hear it but something at least would stay in their eager and enquiring young minds and that anyway, the children were the future!

Another pioneer’s experience touched me very much.  He had been actively teaching in an Alaskan village and was walking down the street when a little seven-year-old Indian girl came up to him and said:  “Can I please be a Bahá’í?  Can I help build a new world?”  She was given a book of Bahá’í prayers and told to memorize them and pray; she said she would and pointed to her three- or four-year-old brother whom she was leading by the hand and said:  “And I am going to teach him too!”

In many poor districts, where there is much crowding and the children are at a loose end in the streets on week-ends, Bahá’í Sunday classes can be started.  The mothers, once they see it keeps the children quiet a few hours, that they learn songs and prayers and good teachings, approve, even though they may be rigorous followers of their own church; many times such children’s classes have led the parents to investigate what it is all about, this thing the child says it has heard in the class, not the least of which is the teaching of

progressive revelation.  A sports club for boys, a sewing class for girls—all are ways of serving and reaching the people.

Many pioneers make a special point of taking their children when they go out teaching, even though the children may be very young and the villages very dirty.  I will never forget being met in a village by a pioneer and her one-week-old baby; it was the first trip outside for baby; when she was placed in the arms of an African grandmother to admire and hold, one should have seen the woman’s face!  This is one way of spreading the message of the oneness of mankind and a way that, not being confined to words, is not easily forgotten.  Many pioneers who have brought up their children in the pioneer field have made a special point of taking them—even when very young—on their teaching trips in the villages and elsewhere.  All villagers, everywhere, seem to love children, and all people, everywhere, appreciate the fact that if you will bring your child—your dearest, most intimate possession—into their midst, then you really trust them.  The white man has shared lots of things with the foreign races he went to live among:  religion, education, his concepts of economy, sociology, political science and so on, but he almost never shared his children.  His children were sacrosanct.  They had their own way of life, their own nursery, their own special schools.  When the Bahá’ís show this is not their attitude, which it most certainly cannot be, the effect on other people can be decisive.

The Bahá’í child should be encouraged to not only teach this Faith (at any age!) but to commit to memory prayers and recite them in public, in meetings, and at Feasts, and to look forward to the thrill of going out in the villages to teach other children the Faith.

It is sometimes heartbreaking to hear the laments of devoted Bahá’ís whose children are not interested in the Faith

at all or do not even wish to be considered Bahá’ís.  Almost invariably it is the fault of the parents and not the child.  We all know that old joke about a parent telling a child, “Don’t do as I do, do as I say!”  As children are extremely perceptive and easily influenced, all the “saying” in the world has no effect upon them when the example differs from the words.  The complaints of children are usually the echoes of their parents’ complaints, and as they generally manage to hear everything, and understand at a much earlier age than they are given credit for, the attitudes of the parents become reflected in the children.  If they hear the parents complain that their children cannot celebrate a Christmas or a Muslim or Jewish holiday, they are going to feel aggrieved.  If, on the other hand, they are told, quite matter-of-factly, that everyone has his own Holy Days and you have nine very special ones all your own; that although your schoolmates or friends are celebrating their Feast with a party—which of course you can go to—you will have your party on the day of your own Feast like Naw-Rúz, your New Year’s day, and they will come and all have just as good a time as you had at their Christmas party.  This approach cuts down the youthful complaints and can lead to a lot of pride and anticipation in how the children are going to entertain their friends on their own Holy Day.

My own observation is that although Bahá’ís may be willing to give their own lives to the Cause they are not willing to give their children’s.  Whether psychologically this comes from a mistaken sense of virtue or a martyr complex, “Well, it is all right for me, but I have no right to impose it on my poor children”, or from the strange misapprehension that a child chooses at 15 to be or not be a Bahá’í, I do not know.  The fact, however, remains that children are one of the main reasons for pioneers leaving their posts or people who long to go pioneering not doing so.  No doubt if the children, or

the oldest child, is already a teen-ager, a real resentment in some cases can develop because of having to leave the environment the child is used to or go to a foreign school.  A lot depends on the individual child and how much it understands and is enthusiastic about serving the Faith.  Younger children, depending on how happy and exciting the parents can make the whole experience, should be able to be taken pioneering without the risk of resentment building up or much adjustment being needed for the new life.

There is often a grave misunderstanding of the significance of the age of 15 in the Bahá’í teachings:  Baha’u’llah calls it the age of maturity; according to His Laws at 15 an individual can marry, and at 15 the obligation to fast and say one of the obligatory prayers comes into force.  There is no mention in the Writings about accepting the Faith or declaring ones self to be a Bahá’í at the age of 15.  Indeed a little reflection should show us how utterly ridiculous and impracticable such a concept is because it implies that at any time during the Bahá’í Dispensation, each generation, over and over again, would have to “accept” Bahá’u’lláh as the One sent by God under Whose guidance it was living and Whose Laws were divinely ordained and Whose Order was the Kingdom of God an earth.  To carry it to its ultimate point of ridiculousness it would mean that millions of human beings every day were being asked whether they really believed the whole society they lived in was based on a message from God or some kind of accidental phenomena or false!

A long time ago, in my own youth, in the United States the National Spiritual Assembly decided to register Bahá’í children at 15 so that a record would be available to prove they were Bahá’ís when they later applied, on religious grounds, for non-combatant military service; this registration also helped in keeping track of the age of the children so they could be

appointed as Bahá’í youth to committees.  It was a purely functional, administrative ruling with no spiritual overtones; somehow the misapprehension grew up that as Bahá’u’lláh had enjoined the independent investigation of truth, one brought up Bahá’í children in a sort of spiritual fog in which they were taught all religions were good and they could make up their own minds which one—if any—they would like to join at the age of 15!  As Bahá’u’lláh strictly enjoins His followers to rear their children in the Cause of God, this unfortunate misunderstanding of the significance of the age of 15 must be rooted out once and for all.

To me one of the most criminal mistakes pioneers—or any Bahá’í parents anywhere—can make is to complain about their work, lament their sacrifices and air their grievances in front of their children.  Even very young children listen much more than one would think, and if they do not catch all the words or understand them they are extremely keen on catching the tone!  If mother bemoans the hardships of a pioneer’s life and stresses her “sacrifice”, if father reminisces about the good things and the better paid job he has “given up for God”, if the parents are depressed, discontented and critical, what on earth do they expect the minds and hearts of their children to be filled with—devotion, and willingness to sacrifice, and joy in service’?

I had a conversation with a Mormon some years ago that shamed me for us Bahá’ís and left a profound and unforgettable impression:  I asked him about the two years’ missionary service so many of them render their church, and he told me it was entirely voluntary, either deputized or paid for by themselves, but not by the church.  That in itself was an eye-opener.  He had already done his own two years’ service and was married, with small children.  He told me they had a little bank into which they put their pennies against the day

when they, too, would go out and do their two years of voluntary service to their church, and he described with what joy his children would count their money at the end of each month and think of their future plans!  I wondered what the Bahá’í world would be like in one generation if we brought up our children with this sense of dedication, of thrill that one day they, too, could go out and teach the Cause of God!

One of the first things we inform people is that our religion has no priests, clergy or missionaries, that every Bahá’í is free to teach and share this message with others.  This includes the children.  Children love being useful and active and should be encouraged to serve in every way, whether teaching or praying or passing the cookies.  They should be taken to the meetings on Holy Days and the Nineteen Day Feasts (providing they will either not disturb the meetings or there is a place they can be put separate so they will not do so).  In this way they will feel Bahá’í activities are a part of their own lives, something to look forward to, maybe a special sit-up-late occasion.

‘Abdu’l-Bahá over and over stressed the importance of teaching children to memorize the Prayers and Writings.  There is a mistaken idea running about that to commit things to memory is unnecessary and even undesirable as then the child repeats it “parrot-like”.  Undoubtedly a young child does not ponder very much over what the meaning is, he concentrates on becoming word-perfect; but the words are there, engraved on his mind, precious words of the Message of God, to protect him and to come back to him later on, maybe in a time of need or while travelling when reading may not be possible and the memory becomes the book.  To be able to recite something from memory I find is very much enjoyed by audiences in meetings, it is particularly appreciated in villages where many of the people are illiterate.

I remember a meeting in Africa of about 100 people, half of whom were children; at the end I asked the African chairman if one of the children could not say a prayer, and his ten-year-old son did so.  Then the seven-year-old son of a Persian pioneer family came forward and recited a prayer from memory and his five-year-old sister, not to be outdone, said she would say one too!  I then lifted up onto the table in front of me the three-year-old sister and told her not to be afraid, but to say her prayer, as I had heard her do the night before in a very small meeting.  She bravely folded her little arms, closed her eyes and in a barely audible peep, recited her short prayer.  I am sure this example of the children had a much more profound effect on the audience than my long lecture!

Not many years ago a nine-year-old child of pioneers in South America, who could write, went out with an illiterate adult Indian Bahá’í teacher and helped elect four Local Spiritual Assemblies on April 21st.  Pioneers should make their children feel the thrill of such things, that they, too, are needed by God and can help Him.  Another nine-year-old child asked her teacher in school if they could have a Bahá’í prayer too.  This led to so much discussion that the child was eventually summoned by the principal who asked her a lot of questions which she answered so well that he became interested and phoned her father and asked him to come and address the school about the Bahá’í Faith.

A delicate question for many pioneers is where to send their children to school, and in many cases they decide on the special, and often exclusive, school for Americans or other foreigners.  Although this is certainly a matter in which individual families must be absolutely free to make their own choice, it seems to me that such a choice is justified when there is absolutely no native school to send them to that has even a remotely decent standard of education.  But all too

often there tends to be an attitude that although there is this native African or Latin American or Indian or some other country’s school, high school or university, which has to be good enough for the children of the elite of that country, it is not good enough for the pioneer’s child, who has to go, eventually and if at all possible, to one of the big-name universities, like Oxford, Cambridge, the Sorbonne, Harvard, or some other “Ivy League” university.  What it all really amounts to is that seldom does a pioneer ever go to a country to make it permanently his home and the home of his children, the place he will love, serve and ultimately bless by laying down his bones in it.

Many of us who are people of the New World, the Western Hemisphere, are very proud that our ancestors left their homelands to live in a bigger, freer, often much harder and more challenging environment, believing that the creative effort of the individual would be more rewarding.  Surely the present-day equivalent of this challenge to conquer in new fields is to pioneer and spread the Cause of God.

Husbands

Not unusually it is the man who first goes out to pioneer, and having gotten settled then brings his family to join him or, if he is not yet married, he goes home to look for a wife.  My own observation in this field, fraught with possible complications, is that it is not so much a question of what to do as what not to do.  One friend who had previously lived some years in Africa persuaded his European wife to join him there with their two children.  In his anxiety to make her feel at home and happy in a strange environment he moved her into a super deluxe house in the best residential area, a house that even had wall-to-wall carpeting (wholly unnecessary in

the tropics anyway and merely a nuisance and status symbol!).  It was located in the wealthiest quarter; it was also located in the place that had the most armed robbery; it also was so new that the water system of the city most of the time did not supply any water to it.  For some months the young wife sat amidst tubs of water, as her sole supply to be drawn upon for all purposes, and lived in terror of some armed man breaking in on her and the children, as was happening fairly often in other houses near her.  The result was, after several months, scared and disillusioned, she went back to Europe with the children—a decision with which he agreed—and left him there, both accepting this sacrifice in an exemplary spirit in order for him to continue his pioneer service.  It is possible that this wife could not have adjusted to life in the tropics of Africa, but I think this very unlikely.  She just had too many strains placed on her in the beginning, when everything was already new and different anyway.  If she had even been forced to live where water was hauled by hand from a well it would not have been half as wearing as to look at a perfect modem bathroom and kitchen in which every tap was dry, week after week, because the city had not got the water pressure working yet in this newest and most fashionable district!  If they had gone to live in a less pretentious place it would not have been a regular target for thieves.

There is a period in pioneering which to myself I always visualize as comparable to that period when the honeymoon is over and a couple have to learn to live together and give and take and make concessions and adjust their egos to the other person’s rights.  It is the let-down, the time when one thinks one has made a terrible mistake, one never was cut out to be a pioneer in the first place.  In fact the idea may even cross ones despairing mind, “Why did I become a Bahá’í!”  This is the point when much prayer and patience with ones own self

is needed, when one must realize it is like living through a bad storm which will surely pass and the sun come out again and the sky be washed clear and bluer than ever.  One must just sit it out, not funk and run away.

When a man is wholly devoted to his pioneer service, loves the unique rewards it holds for anyone who arises in this field to teach the Faith, he should, I believe, think twice about whom he marries.  I have seen many a devoted pioneer marry an equally devoted-to-the-Cause woman, who said she was dying to pioneer, only to discover that this young lady of his choice—whether from a huge metropolis or a village in Persia—could not adjust, or more often, would not adjust to living in the mountains, the savannas, the deserts or the jungles where pioneer service is at once most needed and most rewarding.  This is not a criticism of young Bahá’í women; far from it, because a host of most dedicated and wonderful women are serving in the pioneer field all over the world with unique distinction.  In fact a veritable miracle has taken place in the last ten years because now one sees young Persian and other oriental girls out serving as pioneers with great courage and success, and many of these girls present a challenge to any man to equal them in self-sacrifice and service!

The point is that if a man is really consecrated to his pioneer service, and wants a wife to share it with him, he should not believe that just because a woman says she, too, wants to pioneer she is fitted for it mentally and can go through with it.  I say mentally because except in unusual circumstances the adjustments to be made are in the mind; the body, unless one suffers from a serious ailment, will almost invariably adjust if the mind has adjusted first.  Many very successful marriages are taking place these days between pioneers, often of different national and racial backgrounds, because both parties really love pioneering.

Tribal and local customs

When I visited in the home of some American Negro friends in Africa I was surprised and even a little hurt to discover that their adopted grown-up African son, a very fine young Bahá’í, always ate his meals in the kitchen and never with us; I simply could not understand how such lovely people could treat him this way.  Finally someone explained to me this was the old, time-honoured custom of the boy’s own tribe by which he could not sit and eat with his seniors lest he be spoiled before his character was formed and his quality as a human being proved and he had learned the necessary respect for his elders.  This American family had bowed to the adopted son’s tribal relatives and customs and were, incidentally, doing a wonderful job of bringing him up.  Considering the behaviour of so much of the youth influenced by western ideas, and what excellent characters and behaviour so many young people have who are brought up in various tribal traditions, we might well more often study their methods and their concept of how to raise children.

In many, many places all over the world, places that have never heard of Islám, it is the local custom for the men and women to sit separately; one sees it among the Indians of Latin America, many tribes of Africa, in the villages of Asia and so on.  It has nothing to do with equality.  I recall the excited comments of an American visitor attending an Indian conference high in the Andes in Bolivia; when she saw all the women sitting on one side she cried “This is all wrong!  We believe in the equality of men and women, tell them to sit together!”  I stopped her, for after all what right had she, a foreign visitor, to tell people where and how to sit in their own meeting in their own country?  And who says sitting next to a man in a meeting constitutes equality and not sitting

next to him inequality?  In lots of European countries men and women sit together, eat together, vote together, but the woman has no real equality at all, and in lots of African tribes the women are certainly equal to the men, indeed, in some tribes, where matriarchy prevails, the women hold the power, but still they may not sit together at meals.

A pioneer or a travelling teacher does not go out to another country to make people over in his own image and teach them his personal opinions and the customs of his nation, he goes out to tell them Baha’u’llah has come and that they too are welcome to drink from the life-giving waters of His Revelation and be recreated by them as we hope we ourselves have been.  We must constantly bear in mind that there is not yet a Bahá’í world order or Bahá’í culture or civilization; the tiny embryo of these is developing in the Faith through the spread of its teachings and the establishment of its Administrative Order all over the world.  No one knows how much or how little of any one race’s, nation’s or tribal group’s customs and outlook may eventually become part of that glorious future World Order.  So how can any pioneer—be he American, Persian or from some other place—tell the people he is teaching: “Be like me!”  Who says his way of life is the pattern, or even better than the ways of the people he is teaching?  And yet this is what many of the pioneers do, from sometimes trying to get the villagers to say “Khoda-ha-fez”, which stands for nothing more nor less than “good-bye” in Persian, to trying to get them to brush their teeth with the pioneer’s favourite toothpaste!

In Sri Lanka we went to visit a Government project to settle some Veddas—the aborigines of that island and a stone-age people—in an agricultural settlement with cement houses; the diminishing virgin forest, in which they have always lived a nomadic existence, subsisting by hunting and gathering

what wild things they could find, is now not sufficient to feed them and they are faced with extinction if they do not adopt another way of life.  For thousands of years they had lived in hovels made from the branches of trees, where, in the steaming hot jungle the air could circulate freely.  As I looked at the neat little houses, each with a door and small windows, I wondered how they would ever survive and what diseases would soon be ravaging them, shut into those roasting cement boxes, and why their own more beautiful type of housing could not be adapted to the new way of life.

Every year of my life it is borne in upon me more forcibly that we human beings, more particularly in the countries with an advanced system of education and high standard of living, live by wholly undigested ideas rather than by realities.  It is the “modern way of life” to build with cement, and a tin roof is cheap and enduring; small windows may be seen in building catalogues for Europe and North America so, of course, in the raging tropical heat, under the blazing sun of savannas, in the steaming, debilitating, suffocating humidity of the equatorial forests, the “modern”, “educated”, “advanced” civil planning engineer—or whatever other benighted term he flourishes under—goes ahead and builds his nice new cement boxes.  The fact that the houses built by entirely local methods are better ventilated, often 100 per cent cooler, and a lot cheaper, does not matter.

Boredom and curiosity

If you find that on stopping in a garage to see why your horn does not work, immediately ten children—as well as two mechanics—put their noses into your engine, do not get upset or be surprised.  People are naturally curious and you are

a stranger.  Also, they are bored and this is an event, something a little different to break the routine and monotony of life.  Do not be indignant and short tempered; if there are too many just say, “That’s enough, so many people are not necessary”, and gently send some away, and if no one understands or will budge, just be patient and agreeable.  In some circumstances, however, too much crowding up can lead to pilfering of your things lying about, so close and lock the car or stay close to or in it.  It is unjust to assume everyone is a thief, and stupid not to realize some person might be one again the golden mean of wisdom.  Be careful not to walk off, ever, and leave your bag on the seat or anywhere else.  Crime is much rarer in the villages—in fact in villages off the beaten track it is almost unheard of—but in towns and cities all over the world it is spreading.  Bag snatching in Africa in the cities is very prevalent in some places as is also expert car theft—the whole car, not just pieces of it, though they can go, too.  It is very sad because sixty or seventy years ago, even in cities like Kampala, Uganda, the white people never locked their houses as the Africans were strictly honest and it was unnecessary.  Now, through the corruptive influence of our civilization, all this has changed and their moral fibre (just like our own) is steadily deteriorating.

Curiosity is a universal human (and animal) characteristic.  It is not in itself an evil or an unpleasant thing.  When I went to live in the Near East, after growing up in Canada, I was very surprised to be asked by any casual Arab acquaintance all sorts of highly personal questions:  How old was I?  Was I married?  Did I have any children?  If not, why not!  This is not what to us is unpardonable nosing into ones private affairs, it is a form of courtesy, and strangely enough it is common in many countries throughout the world.  It is showing a friendly interest in the other person.  Even if there

is an element of curiosity in it, what difference does it make?  I have come to adopt this myself and find it often makes a bridge between myself and a total stranger; except for our own civilization people like being asked—usually, not invariably!—about themselves.  I am interested in them, and they are warmed and flattered by this interest and respond to it in a friendly manner, and it often makes a good introduction for me to tell them where I came from, that I am a widow, with no near relatives (this always produces head shakes of sympathy) and am making this long trip because I am a Bahá’í.  Did they ever hear of the Bahá’í Faith?  Then one can give a little explanation or a pamphlet.  In Africa if one gives one pamphlet, one is literally stormed on all sides by hands demanding one each.  It is impossible to ever keep or to have enough free literature.  All too often we feel that only a contrived occasion, a planned event, is when we should mention the Faith, but in a natural and spontaneous way it can be mentioned almost any time, anywhere.  Nothing is more tiresome than a fanatic, a bore who pours forth, unsolicited, a stream of information his listener does not want to hear at all.  But between this and dead silence there is a happy medium; cast out, if the opportunity is there, a worm on a hook, in other words casually bring in the word Bahá’í, and if you get a bite, if some interest is shown, say a little about the Teachings and more only if demanded to do so.

Reflections on behaviour

People’s customs, particularly in tribal Africa, are very marked and often very regional; in fact, as one travels the world, the degree to which traits and customs are particularized is truly extraordinary.  Near Mysore in India we once

had a large meeting of jungle people, representing, we were told, three distinct tribes, the woodcutters, the honey collectors and the basket weavers and there was no overlapping of occupation.  Motoring through Africa one passes through a tribe that is famous for its wood carving, suddenly, along the same road, with a gap of a few kilometres between villages, one enters a new tribal area where the people, except for rare exceptions, do no carving at all but are skilled in leather and bead work.  In the unspoiled, typical bazaars of the Middle East each trade has its own section, the pearl sellers and jewellery workers, the embroiderers, the makers of sweet cakes, the butchers, the coppersmiths whose deafening din rises from only one quarter—hundreds of particularized, fascinating trades, each keeping to itself.  It is late in human history that the emporium, where all kinds of goods can be found for sale in one place, has come into existence—the most devastating example being the new supermarkets.  The reason for this specialization whether in what a man produces or the style of his house or the food he eats, is, I believe, a reflection of our animal heritage; where and how a bird or animal lives and what he eats are highly specific things in nature, and death results from trying to get the creature to live like another different one.  Men too have developed in tight little groups over hundreds and even thousands of years and hence these marked differences between tribes and races and various societies.  It is part of the beauty, the wonder and fascination of our world and it also means that each group of men—in this new age of the oneness of mankind—can and will bring its own gifts to add to the richness of the whole, its particular talent, its exceptional skill, its different outlook, its unique charm and abilities.

This is yet another reason (if politeness and consideration for others’ feelings were not enough) for us to respect and

take an interest in the customs, traditions and beliefs of those we go among to teach.  By and large the white man, in his power and conceit, has succeeded in impressing all other races with a sense of his superiority and their inferiority.  With the exception of those people of Asia of Chinese and Indian stock, who, because of their long, unbroken cultural memories and experience, which exceeds by thousands of years that of the white man in the West, this seems to be universally true.  It is too long a subject to be gone into here but the fact remains that a vast number of people who were living in the nineteenth century in primitive societies, and a great many who still are, have been given an inferiority complex by the white man.  By making a man look down on himself one persuades him his qualities, his way of life, even his thoughts are not worth while or at best poor and shabby compared to ones own.  This is little short of criminal.  It has had the effect of making a vast number of human beings, in addition to beginning to despise their own values, begin to worship and covet those of western civilization, a white man’s product, which Shoghi Effendi has assured us is, at least in its present form, a “cancerous materialism”.

People require encouragement and praise, the way plants require sunlight and water.  This is one reason why we Bahá’ís are admonished to mention the good points and not the bad in others.  So often I have seen, as if a cloud lifted, a change in the faces and the spirits of villagers—and plenty of non-villagers too!—when they, their history, a custom, an artfact they had made or a thought they expressed was praised.  Many times villagers, responding to this sincere encouragement, particularly the older ones, would tell me a tradition or piece of folklore or produce some native thing that even their own children, let alone the pioneer or visitor from outside, had never known of before.  God, we are taught, created and

loves each man for himself.  Then let us give back to many people their self-respect, because it helps a man to not only be himself but to grow and develop and that alone can lead him to a deeper relationship with his Maker, and to establish a deeper relationship with ones Maker is the main object of our life on this earth.

The more people are still living a tribal or traditional life the more strict and formal are their customs, a thing foreigners do not usually either suspect or respect.  Whereas all too often the city American’s greeting has become “hi!” as he slouches into a room and throws himself in a semi-recumbent position into a chair, in Africa and Asia, Latin America and the islands of the world and, to be fair, also in many villages of North America, not to mention Europe, there are set and polite forms of greeting which can often take some time to exchange.  As courtesy is a command of Baha’u’llah, we may well rejoice that it is already practically a universal characteristic of most races, with the exception of a great many members of the white, urban population of the world and those who imitate their ways.

When the father and mother of ones host come into their son’s compound, in some places in Africa, and refuse to either eat with him or their daughter-in-law or enter their home, one is taken aback at what appears a senseless lack of cordiality.  Yet when one learns that this is a custom based on the observation of human nature and frailties, designed to solve the mother-in-law problem at village level and maintain family unity and peace, one ceases to be shocked and controls the words of disapproval that were about to be heedlessly poured forth into the lives of other people.  Again, an excellent principle to adopt is to mind ones own business and to remember that neither God nor the pioneer committee has sent one out to mind other people’s.

As we passed the Nigerian frontier I read and copied the following, issued by the Ministry of Information, Lagos, Nigeria, and titled:

Guides on human relations

Have self-control

Understand it from other people’s point of view

Make others’ interests your own

Admit it when you are wrong

Never criticize publicly

Reason, don’t argue

Explain thoroughly

Lead, don’t drive

Avoid snap judgements

Take care of little things

Inform people of matters affecting them

Offer helpful suggestions

Never forget to give compliments for a job well done

Stress the positive

I thought it one of the best pieces of advice I had ever read.  Particularly “stress the positive”.  Bahá’u’lláh’s words we believe are the words of no less than God Himself.  When He says, “Breathe not the sins of others so long as thou art thyself a sinner.  Shouldst thou transgress this command, accursed wouldst thou be, and to this I bear witness”; when we are enjoined in His teachings to mention the good points of others—to such an extent as to be silent about all the faults and mention only the one virtue—we are not dealing with a

nice set of spiritual platitudes but rather with far-reaching spiritual laws that will enable us as individuals to live in harmony with each other and assist us to bring out and strengthen the good qualities in each other.

It is strange, how much psychology we think we know in this modern age and yet never even practise its rudiments.  The powers of the mind are as strong as, and sometimes even stronger than the powers of the body.  If one is convinced one cannot do a thing one seldom can; if one is equally convinced one can do a thing seeming miracles take place.  To give an example, if one wanted to light a room the addition of one candle would add one more unit, to add 1,000 candles would be to add 1,000 units—a brilliant light; with the addition of every candle the light would be increased.  But supposing one starts taking the candles away, one by one?  With each subtraction the light diminishes.  Constant criticism, dwelling on the negative aspects of a person’s character, or a community’s joint character, is like taking away one candle after another until only darkness or a gloomy twilight prevails and a misery of despair or hopelessness envelops the individual or even the entire community.

I have observed that unless pioneers are on their guard they seem prone to becoming negative in their outlook; it seems at times almost an occupational hazard!  In a strange land, amidst a strange people, after the exhilaration of the spiritual honeymoon of leaving ones country and arriving in the new one, the pioneer has to begin, very much like a newly-wed, to adjust to a new way of life, a different and maybe difficult climate, different living standards and foods; he may pick up a germ and have intestinal troubles or the flu—germs and insects I find are very fond of foreigners in their midst—in any case there may be a real let-down, both physically and psychologically; the answer is prayer, and for

the pioneer to realize that such things are a normal part of anyones experience who leaves his home and goes far afield to serve this wonderful Faith, and that if he is patient, body and mind will adjust to the new environment and his spirits will rise again and enthusiasm flow back.

I have long since come to the conclusion that we must do what we do, in our effort to serve this Cause of God, for His sake alone, seeking only His good pleasure, neither expecting nor demanding appreciation and praise from others.  I recommend this attitude because it has made much of my own work in life endurable.  Although people may appreciate what we do much more than we realize, we often get crushed and hurt by what we feel is lack of any encouragement or appreciation for our services and efforts.  To expect reward and recognition only from Baha’u’llah frees one from these tangled feelings.

Climate and environment

It was Shoghi Effendi who called my attention to the fact that the temperate zones of this planet are few and far between, whereas the badlands, the arid lands, are vaster and more numerous.  For someone from Northern and Western Europe, North America, New Zealand and a few more areas, the green, the flourishing agriculture, the bracing climate with marked seasons, the snow-capped mountains, the abundance of water, are all things taken for granted.  The unending steppes, tundra, savannas, and deserts of the world are an abstraction, something in books.  Do people from the temperate climates realize there is no spring, summer, autumn and winter throughout practically the entire African Continent?  There is only a rainy season, maybe two rainy seasons—the

little rain and the big rain—and otherwise it is always the same.  I have learned in my travels that the greatest single blessing on the planet is water, yet how often do we ever think of it as such, living in countries where there is always rain and water?  So when we people from such privileged countries, from a geographical standpoint, travel in other less favoured lands, it often takes us a long time, a stupidly long time, to get it into our heads that one reason others are not like us is because neither they nor their ancestors for thousands of years lived in the same kind of an environment we and our ancestors did, that they still do not, and therefore their approach to living and its possibilities are fundamentally different from ours.

It is my own experiences—dearly won and highly prized—that have opened my eyes to these things in the course of my travels in now 109 countries.  In the south of India, at the end of the dry season before the monsoon broke, we motored every day to villages in Orissa; the sweat poured out of our bodies at such a rate that it was acrid and irritating to the skin and quite unbearable if one could not wash it off.  When we drove at the end of the day back to our hotel, I looked at the village pond, shrunk down to a mere large puddle of green slime, in which men, women, children, cattle, and dogs all bathed and mostly drank, and I realized that if I could not go back to my comfortable room, with its bath and cool drinking water, but were forced to stay in that village, I, too, at the end of a day of that heat, sweating, and intense thirst, would have taken my place in that slimy green pond, gratefully, like man and beast.

On another occasion, in a remote Indian area in the interior of Argentina, in a barren wilderness, the river was in

spate, but so filled with mud it was like a gruel and we could not drink it, though the Indians could.  We fetched our water from a swamp, every drop an elixir to our parched bodies, “disinfecting” it—in theory at least—with Halazone tablets.

In the Andes, way up above the altiplano in Bolivia, I remember sleeping in a stone hut while attending a conference of the Bahá’ís of that area.  There are no trees, almost no shrubs; a bitter wind blows over the barren heights, adding to the intense cold in the thin air of that altitude of almost 16,000 feet; there is no fuel for heating; the buildings usually have no other opening than one door, to preserve the inhabitants as much as possible from the elements; at night, even though it was going on to summer, we were so cold I stuffed newspapers around the crack of the door to keep out the icy draft.  I came to understand then why the Indians never bathed; in what? how? and decided that I, too, under no circumstances whatsoever would take a bath (nor, indeed, could take one!) if I lived in their environment, where, aside from the climate, water is very hard to come by.

When travellers from the sophisticated environment of our over-civilized cities, with all their comforts and facilities, arrive in countries and towns so extremely different from their own in every respect, one of the first things they do (along with harbouring a lot of other prejudiced judgements) is to condemn the local people, especially villagers, as “lazy”.  For a long time I think I more or less shared this judgement.  Then gradually, as I drove and drove through the wastelands of this world, past one sun-baked mud village after another, or one cluster of huts in the deep jungle after another, an idea began to shape up in my mind:  what would you do if you lived there?  Well, being an artist, I would do something of some kind with my hands.  But most people, my mind answered, are not artists, so what would you do?

Then, I thought, I would repair the walls of my mud home and certainly make it look neater and nicer.  But then I remembered how one has to do this—when the rainy season comes, because otherwise the large quantities of water needed to work the mud are not available.  Well, I argued, I would repair the roof—but I remembered that can be seasonal too, in some places depending on the grass.  Well, then I would paint the frames around the door and paint the little windows; really, I said to myself, with what?  Paint costs money and the nearest place to buy it is 30, 50, 100 or more kilometres away—besides, what would I use for money?  I have not even got money for matches or oil or salt and sugar …  Then I thought why don’t they at least cultivate something?  Again my mind answered me that this was usually seasonal too and very short in drought areas or, in the truly tropical belts, all one did was to clear a patch of jungle (hard work) and plant plantains and cassavas or maybe a little maize and it all grows so fast there is almost nothing one can do to it except eat it when it is ripe!  Then, why don’t the housewives take more pride in their culinary skills and make more effort?  I ask myself.  Don’t be silly, I answer me, people here eat to live, eating is not a luxury or a social rallying point as it is with us, it is like a bird or an animal that takes gratefully and humbly its daily gift from the gods of sustenance to sustain life.  So in the end, as I travel the long dirt road, I see again that given their circumstances I would live as they do.  Then, believing that many economic advancements and social changes are the will of God for this Day, I realize, once more, that it is because of this belief, that Baha’u’llah’s teachings offer the sole remedy to the problems facing the world, I am travelling on this dusty road, in the suffocating heat, to assist in creating the Kingdom of God on earth by the only way it can be done

through mankind’s acceptance in their entirety of the teachings of God for this Day, not a piecemeal remedy through this or that nation’s plans and schemes, which so often conflict with those of other nations, but a universal remedy for all men.

Morals

The light of a good character surpasseth

the light of the sun.

Bahá’u’lláh

Bahá’ís should make a sharp distinction between what is the state of the so-called civilized world at present, what is expected of them as followers of Bahá’u’lláh, and what is the framework in which a tribe or a given group of people functions.

Shoghi Effendi, in 1938, in The Advent of Divine Justice, said the society of North America was suffering from “an excessive and binding materialism”, “an excessive and enervating materialism”, that it was “notorious” for its “laxity in moral standards” and that this “moral laxity and licentiousness” defiled “the character of a not inconsiderable proportion of its citizens .  ..”  It is, alas, the standards of this society of North America—so prevalent in Europe as well—which have ever-increasingly spread to the rest of the world, until now in the capitals and chief cities of the nations of Asia, Africa, the Pacific and Latin America, their evil effects are increasing by leaps and bounds.  The world, Shoghi Effendi wrote, was “enervated by a rampant and brutal materialism”.  It is very illuminating that more than once he associates the word “enervating” with materialism for to enervate means

to deprive of vigour, to sap the strength; he seems to depict materialism as a virus that so weakens the resistance of human society that all other ills prey on and devour it.  Indeed he refers in his writings to a “cancerous materialism”—cancer being the dread killer that transforms normal cells into rapidly proliferating and destroying, diseased cells.  No words were ever idly used by the Guardian and those who go out to teach the Faith in developing countries should ponder what are the implications of the civilization from which they themselves may have come.  “The civilization,” Bahá’u’lláh says, “so often vaunted by the learned exponents of arts and sciences, will, if allowed to overleap the bounds of moderation, bring great evil upon men. …  If carried to excess, civilization will prove as prolific a source of evil as it had been of goodness when kept within the restraints of moderation.”  “The day is approaching when its (civilization’s) flame will devour the cities …”

One of the things which this pernicious civilization is most rapidly destroying is the moral standards of humanity.  The present so-called “permissive” society is the exact opposite of the society envisaged by Bahá’u’lláh, which is anything but permissive in its nature and is governed by law, order and strong moral principles.  We should make this quite clear to officials, educators and leaders of public opinion with whom we come in contact.  The moral standard to which Bahá’ís should adhere is set forth very clearly by Shoghi Effendi in that same great epistle:

A chaste and holy life must be made the controlling principle in the behavior and conduct of all Bahá’ís, both in their social relations with the members of their own community, and in their contact with the world at large.  It must adorn and reinforce the ceaseless labors and merito-

rious exertions of those whose enviable position is to propagate the Message, and to administer the affairs, of the Faith of Baha’u’llah.  It must be upheld, in all its integrity and implications, in every phase of the life of those who fill the ranks of that Faith, whether in their homes, their travels, their clubs, their societies, their entertainments, their schools, and their universities.  It must be accorded special consideration in the conduct of the social activities of every Bahá’í summer school and any other occasions on which Bahá’í community life is organized and fostered.  It must be closely and continually identified with the mission of the Bahá’í youth, both as an element in the life of the Bahá’í community, and as a factor in the future progress and orientation of the youth of their own country.

Such a chaste and holy life, with its implications of modesty, purity, temperance, decency, and clean-mindedness, involves no less than the exercise of moderation in all that pertains to dress, language, amusements, and all artistic and literary avocations.  It demands daily vigilance in the control of ones carnal desires and corrupt inclinations.  It calls for the abandonment of a frivolous conduct, with its excessive attachment to trivial and often misdirected pleasures.  It requires total abstinence from all alcoholic drinks, from opium, and from similar habit-forming drugs.  It condemns the prostitution of art and of literature, the practices of nudism and of companionate marriage, infidelity in marital relationships, and all manner of promiscuity, of easy familiarity, and of sexual vices.  It can tolerate no compromise with the theories, the standards, the habits, and the excesses of a decadent age.  Nay rather it seeks to demonstrate, through the dynamic force of its example, the pernicious character of such theories, the falsity of such standards, the hollowness of such

claims, the perversity of such habits, and the sacrilegious character of such excesses.
