Leaving no room for ambiguity the Guardian quotes from the pen of Bahá’u’lláh Himself, showing that chastity is enjoined upon the Bahá’ís of both sexes until they marry.  In one quotation it is clearly stated by Him that His words refer to men, for He says:  “… And if he met the fairest and most comely of women, he would not feel his heart seduced by the least shadow of desire for her beauty.  Such an one, indeed, is the creation of spotless chastity. …” and in another He directly refers to women:  “Purity and chastity have been, and still are, the most great ornaments for the handmaidens of God.”  Although these passages refer to the relationship of men to women and women to men, in addition it must be clearly understood that homosexual relationships are forbidden by the Bahá’í teachings, and those so afflicted must make every effort to overcome such tendencies through determination to obey the law of Bahá’u’lláh, through prayer and through medical and psychological assistance if required.

All this is really in the nature of a preamble to the subject of morals and pioneering.  Those who leave the more “advanced” civilizations and go among the more “backward” ones—both obviously very arbitrary terms—may discover that whereas immoral conduct is, or at least was, more covert in the former places, it is much more overt in the latter ones.  It seems to me that in our judgement of other people’s moral behaviour we must always take this into consideration and realize it is certainly not more virtuous to gild wrongdoing with hypocrisy than to do what is wrong openly.  Perhaps an example of this will make it clearer than words:  on more than one occasion, when I was in the home of Bahá’ís,

I commented on a lovely baby, the child of the daughter of the house, only to find, when I asked to be introduced to the husband, that he did not exist.  Though the family may have deplored this fact, the baby—certainly the most blameless party—was adored.  Remembering the intense hypocrisy surrounding immoral behaviour in my part of the world when I was young, and the secret disposal of the unwanted illegitimate child, I could not but feel this attitude was much better, and certainly much fairer to the child, who grew up in a home surrounded by the love it was entitled to.  As openly acknowledged illegitimate children are very common in many parts of the world, Bahá’ís from other places, with other standards, must be extremely careful to in no way hurt or insult other people as regards their moral mores or customs; our duty is to be better Bahá’ís ourselves and through our example help others to change the pattern of their lives to conform to the laws of God for this Day.  As Bahá’u’lláh so aptly puts it in His luminous Hidden Words:  “How couldst thou forget thine own faults and busy thyself with the faults of others?  Whoso doeth this is accursed of me.”

According to the standards given us in this Dispensation by Bahá’u’lláh, to find moral behaviour in conformity with His teachings anywhere in the world today is extremely difficult.  The best person, therefore, to begin reforming is ones own self.

I firmly believe, however, that Bahá’í Assemblies, national or local, as well as their committees, should be much more vigilant in supervising the arrangements made for travelling teachers and the accommodation provided for them as regards sleeping arrangements; the same applies to schools and institutes.  An unmarried couple or young men and women should not be accommodated on Bahá’í property with no chaperon.  Whether their moral behaviour is above reproach

is not the point; firstly, it is not in conformity with the above ideas quoted from Shoghi Effendi and secondly, it is impossible to persuade those who are not Bahá’ís that the moral conduct of our young people is above reproach; in conformity with human nature they are going to think the worst so that in either case we defeat our own objective, which is to show the world we have a very high moral standard and that we not only preach it and practise it but give the appearance of it as well.

In many places throughout different continents of the world it is very bad for a young woman, unaccompanied by a husband or older woman, to go on a teaching tour; villagers, especially, consider it not only very peculiar but are convinced she is immoral.  I recently heard of a case, in southern Asia, where a young, very devoted, charming and gifted Board member went on a tour of villages; she was not only constantly shadowed by the secret police of that country but her tour was unfavourably commented on by non-Bahá’í villagers who simply could not conceive of a young woman doing such a thing.  When I called the attention of the Counsellors to the unwisdom of such an act they said they had no one else to send.  The answer—unless there is a truly extraordinary emergency of some kind—is to not send anyone.  It is far better for the Cause to do nothing than to do the wrong thing.  There is an oriental proverb that says it is better to deal with a wise enemy than a foolish friend!  If people have no attitude towards the Faith, because they never even heard of it, that opens the door for us to teach, but if their first impression of it is unfavourable it takes a long time to eradicate their prejudice, a very long time in villages, particularly.

On the other hand, there are places all over the world where a young and unmarried Bahá’í woman can go out and teach on her own; she will be well received; there is no gos-

sip about her and she can do infinite good.  As I said, it is up to the bodies responsible to be wise, well-informed and vigilant; this certainly applies to the individual Bahá’í as well.

In Latin America in particular, young Bahá’í women—and older women too—cannot be too careful in the way they conduct themselves and particularly, if they are young, in where they live.  A girl living unchaperoned, not, in other words, under the wing of an older woman or a family, is likely to find herself almost automatically considered at least a loose woman if not a prostitute and she may find herself courted or shunned as such, as the case may be.  It is no use to say she comes from Europe or North America, and that such an attitude is all old-fashioned nonsense!  The purpose of her pioneering is to attract souls to the Cause of God, to present it in such a light that people will turn to it for their and the world’s salvation, not to arouse a violent local reaction against it and the one who teaches it!

Not only must single women in Latin countries be very careful where they live but they should never invite a man to their room alone; the same is true for young men; they are much freer in choice of a place to live but they should guard their own good name by not inviting a girl to visit them alone.

Such admonitions and advice, I fully realize, can be very irksome to young people with the excessive freedom they enjoy these days.  Nevertheless, they are based on both the Bahá’í Writings and long experience and observation on my part.  Ignoring these things has often caused much damage to the teaching work and the reputation of the Faith, and personal tragedy to dear and devoted but unwise and inexperienced believers.

Prevalent in Latin America, but not exclusively confined to that part of the world, is the common custom to leave ones wife or husband (often divorce is non-existent by law)

and take up living, on a more or less permanent basis, with another partner; this can go on for years and a whole new family be born.  In many cases, even though any obstacle to getting married has been removed or does not exist, still the parties concerned do not want to get married at all but just to continue as they are.  In some cases this is due to a mental attitude and habit that sees no reason to change, but in others it is based on the laws of the country by which the woman has almost no rights and could, if she married the man, lose all her rights over her own money and her children.  When people in such a situation desire to become Bahá’ís it is not the business of the pioneer or teacher to tell them what to do about their personal life or to lay down conditions for their acceptance as Bahá’ís.  What people did before they became Bahá’ís is one thing and what they do after they become Bahá’ís is another.  If any action at all is indicated in these cases of common law marriages it concerns National Assemblies, who either have or will secure advice from the Universal House of Justice as to how they should proceed.

We must be very careful to always draw a sharp line between what we, who are Bahá’ís, must do and what laws we must obey, and what other people, who are not Bahá’ís, can do:  drinking is strictly forbidden to us because this is the law given us by Bahá’u’lláh.  But if a Jew, a Christian, or anyone else chooses to drink we have no right to upbraid them with breaking a law because, as they have not believed in and accepted Bahá’u’lláh, they are not bound by His law.  We can point out it is not good for their health and why, but we have no right to tell them not to drink.

The same is true of monogamy:  this is the new law of God for this Dispensation; it does not for a moment imply anyone who practises polygamy is a sinner.  When a well-known African Chief accepted the Faith he had seventy

wives and that was perfectly all right as far as we were concerned for he had acquired them under his tribal laws and customs; but now he is a Bahá’í he cannot add one more.  No blame attaches to his previous acts and certainly it would be grossly unjust to require that he divorce all his wives but one; but having accepted the teachings of Bahá’u’lláh, he cannot add another wife for that would then break Bahá’í law.  It is this wonderful reasonableness and tolerance that is yet another proof of the perfection of this Cause.

An immemorial Chinese custom is for a man to have one or more concubines in addition to his wife; in Tibet a woman often had more than one husband; the point is that for us Bahá’ís there can be no condemnation of people who obeyed the laws of other religions and followed the customs of other cultures.  The most we can say is mankind has now reached a new and higher point in its evolution and therefore, quite naturally, requires new laws.  We must never think that by decrying other people’s customs and ways we are exalting Bahá’u’lláh’s Revelation.  Think of with what love ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, the Perfect Exemplar, surrounded all men of all backgrounds.

In the words of Shoghi Effendi cited above, he points out that “moderation in all that pertains to dress” is also a standard we Bahá’ís should exemplify.  My own mind works best with examples so I will quote two from my own recollections:  after the passing of the beloved Guardian I met for the first time a Hand of the Cause who had been very active in teaching the masses in one of the islands of South East Asia; I asked him about those people and their customs and he said they went naked in their steaming tropical jungles, clothed only in tattoo marks!  I asked him if he had said anything to them about wearing clothes.  He said, “No; why should I, my purpose was to teach them about Bahá’u’lláh.”  To me this

seemed entirely right.  On another occasion a young pioneer to Africa approached me and asked if he could consult me alone; I said yes, and it turned out that when he went to teach in the villages he was often driven there by a very devoted European Bahá’í lady who wore mini-mini-skirts; he said, “When my eyes fall on her sitting there I look away in embarrassment and then my eyes fall on the tribes-women and it is their custom to be naked from the waist up.  Where shall I look!”  To me there is a very subtle point involved in these two stories:  the two different tribes, one Asiatic and one African, were neither immoral nor indecent in their lack of clothing because it was based not only on thousand-year-old custom but also because there was no provocation involved whatsoever.  In my own experience of undressedness in dozens of places I have never seen a villager’s eyes follow a woman because of her nakedness; it just does not exist for him, or, alternatively for her if the men are naked.  But the introduction and propagation of the mini-skirt and plunging necklines has acknowledgedly been a device to attract and excite sexual attention.  Therefore to me it was appropriate to call the European matron aside and ask her not to wear her mini-skirts in the villages or in Bahá’í meetings as it shocked the Africans very much—which it does—but to say nothing to the tribal matrons about putting on more clothes as no one was shocked in their environment and it aroused no feelings.  If foreigners go to the villages, nakedly exposed in modern western dresses, they scandalize and are a test to the local people, who, whatever they are wearing or not wearing, adhere to rigid social customs.  This is particularly true at present in Africa where a very modest costume, with full length skirts, is being increasingly worn as a form of national costume in many countries.  One should also add here, while on this subject, that trousers are a very shocking costume to

many people in Africa, Asia, Latin America and other places because the people associate them with male attire.  It is therefore much better for Bahá’í women to wear skirts in such places, whether they like it or not.  On my own tours I have insisted that any woman accompanying me should not wear trousers in meetings; if she wanted to travel in them that was all right, but for meetings and In villages she must appear in a skirt.  This rule, however, does not apply to countries where women, as their own national costume, wear pants or trousers, such as Pakistan, Chinese countries, Vietnam and so on.  The point is not to offend people and make teaching more difficult, but to please and attract them and make it easier.  Good judgement and good taste are required in approaching all such matters.

Contributions

The beloved Guardian said contributions are the life-blood of our Bahá’í work and pointed out the aim must be universal participation in giving to our various Funds.  We cannot oblige people to give; one of the points I always try to bring out in public talks and interviews with important government officials as well as the press is that this is a religion which accepts for its work no money from non-Bahá’ís and to which contributions are made voluntarily by its members.  These statements are not only impressive but give one the opportunity of explaining why we are not able to do some particular thing at present, such as opening a school, buying a Centre, building a Temple or a hospital and so on.  It makes sense to people who would like us to immediately do such things, to explain why we cannot, and it also is a true and very useful statement in allaying the fears of those who are

jealous of the spread of the Faith and if they saw signs of our becoming stronger locally, might start to actively move against us.  It is also essential sometimes to state these two facts to Bahá’í villagers who can be importunate in their demands for a school or local Hazíratu’l-Quds, pointing out to them that Bahá’í money comes as voluntary contributions from people often as poor as they are or poorer and that they should therefore make an effort to build what they need themselves, with perhaps a little outside help if possible.  We talk about our world-wide Bahá’í community and all being one in it and yet when we go to a village often treat them condescendingly as if they were backward children; they are not; they are often shrewd, business-like adults to whom facts can speak for themselves as clearly as they do to anyone else.  If they are going to be a part of the whole they have to be made to feel a responsibility for the whole, and the way they respond to this can provide, in its turn, moving examples to cite to not only other Bahá’í communities but to non-Bahá’ís as well.

When the Panama Temple was being built, in a remote village in Nigeria the local believers were told about it and an old woman brought three eggs as her contribution toward its construction; another Bahá’í bought these and the small earmarked sum went to the National Spiritual Assembly who forwarded it to the Panama National Spiritual Assembly and in due course the old woman in her village received a receipt in her name.  I recently told this story to a high-ranking government official in India, pointing out the Delhi Bahá’í Temple would be erected by love and contributions as universal as this.

High in the Bolivian Andes a Spiritual Assembly informed me proudly it had contributions in its local Fund and wanted to show them to me.  I was very willing to inspect them but

not when a building was pointed out about 1,000 feet down in a valley!  I said if I got down there I should never get back—which was true; at 16,000 feet altitude I was already out of breath.  However, the Indian Board member went and inspected it for me and made me a list of the potatoes and maize which would be sold in the town and the money sent to the National Fund.  I sent that precious list to the Universal House of Justice.

We must find the key to people’s minds on the subject of giving because to make offerings to God, to statues, to ones ancestors, has been a universal practice by all men.  It seems to me the connection between what they did before and the necessity to now give to the Cause of Bahá’u’lláh must be made.  All over Asia people give to their Temples—flowers, food, incense, votive offerings; why cannot they be made to realize that giving those few pennies to the Bahá’í work is the same act?  Perhaps in some cases the people must be appealed to for something far, not near, like constructing a new Bahá’í House of Worship.  They must form the mental pattern of giving to the Faith and this being established, to give to other Funds will become easier.  Often villagers, sometimes not without reason, mistrust the Local Assembly or treasurer and will therefore not give; or they are afraid their precious money (for money is very scarce in villages) will never reach the National Spiritual Assembly.  Through better supervision, through promptly issuing receipts in the donor’s name, through letting the friends know where their money went, confidence may be built up.

I believe there are two channels we might try in this connection:  one is to give in the name of the dead; ancestor worship, the little shrine in a corner where joss sticks burn and miniature food offerings are placed, can perhaps be gradually transmuted into the Bahá’í concept of doing good

in the name of the dead one and this should be written into the receipt and the name of the donor and the dead one both appear on it, as well as the sum given and for what purpose.  The other is deputization, to give towards other teachers going out to teach in ones stead, which is explicitly stated by Baha’u’llah Himself:  “Centre your energies in the propagation of the Faith of God.  Whoso is worthy of so high a calling, let him arise and promote it.  Whoso is unable, it is his duty to appoint him who will, in his stead, proclaim this Revelation …”  Is there any reason why a whole village cannot help deputize an individual?  When the hearts of the friends become involved in giving and their pride in service is enlisted, we may find some, at least, of the keys needed to tap the vast reservoir of Bahá’í contributions.

Visas

Although a National Assembly—or its Pioneer Committee if it has one—should be able to give information regarding visas for different countries, the pioneer would do well, in my opinion, to check all the details for himself.  Every place has different regulations:  a transit visa, as the name implies, is for only a few days; a tourist visa is for much longer, often for a three-month period, but if you do not make sure what kind you are getting you may find that although you thought you had one for three months, actually 30 days is written in your passport.  Do not leave things to chance; use your own eyes and your own head, for after all it is you who are going to suffer the maximum inconvenience if something goes wrong and not someone back home, who thought they were right but were wrong!  Residence visas—permits to stay a year or longer—are much harder to obtain.

If the pioneer plans to work or already has a job in the place he is going to, visa regulations become even more important.  Many a devoted servant of the Faith, turning his back on his home and previous pursuits, has gone out to his pioneer post on the wrong visa basis and been put out of the country—sometimes at a moment’s notice or even under arrest—because proper enquiries and proper arrangements had not been made in the beginning.  For instance, you could find that if you entered on a tourist visa you are forced to leave at its expiry, job or no job, because you should have entered on a residence visa or one specifically covering permission to work; this oversight can in many cases mean the government in question refuses you both permission to return to your job and a residence visa because you did not do the right thing in the first place.  In other places the exact opposite may be true, you enter on a tourist visa, find work and then are granted, inside the country, a permanent or residence visa.

There is another very important point and that is that some countries require you to report to the police or immigration authorities after a specified period in the country; I have found that this information is often casually mentioned or hidden away on some form and on a number of occasions have inadvertently ignored it and been caused a lot of unnecessary embarrassment and inconvenience; so be sure you know from the beginning, on entering a country, what is expected of you as regards its regulations.  The same holds true for the date of renewal of your residence visa inside the country or for a work permit that requires periodical renewing or any other special arrangement the government may require.  To let such things overrun the date of their expiry can lead to your expulsion or loss of work or loss of some permit to travel in a special area where you are serving the Faith.

Many countries require that you have a ticket to return to your place of origin, or at least are provided with a ticket to another country beyond the frontiers of the country you are getting the visa for, or produce evidence that you have adequate money to leave their country when your visa expires and thus not be a burden on them.  Remember also that either as a travelling teacher or as a resident pioneer, when you leave the country for even a day and cross its frontier, your visa becomes automatically terminated unless you have one that is good for more than one entry and this is rarely issued; if it is valid for more than one entry, it will be clearly written that this is so in the visa in your passport.  You may require for every time that you leave a country a new visa to get into it again, and you may be given it or you may not, depending entirely on circumstances.  In other words, you might find that by going even on an excursion of one day to a nearby country, you forfeited your permission to be in the country you are pioneering in and could not get back into it.  In any case, be sure to inform yourself from some reliable source before you take any action of this kind.  Shoghi Effendi always advised the Bahá’ís in technical matters to consult experts.  An expert is a man who has experience in a certain field and knows what he is talking about; an expert is not just somebody who tells you he knows what he thinks you should know.

There is another aspect to visas and permits and that is that bureaucracy is such today that an act that should take 15 minutes can take days; you can be flatly told, by a silly clerk in an empty office, that they are so busy they cannot possibly attend to your affairs at once, come back tomorrow.  The wise traveller knows all these kinds of things can (and no doubt will!) happen and is therefore cautious and allows sufficient time to arrange his affairs properly.

Servants

The question of servants is an important one because in Asia and Africa, and other places outside Europe and North America, a vast number of people earn their living in this manner.  To be a servant is no disgrace.  Whatever work a person does in the spirit of service, Bahá’u’lláh tells us, is worship, which surely has abolished once and for all the prudery of “white collar workers” and the prejudices associated today in the West with someone being a servant.  The current concepts of equality and superiority are very confused and misleading:  is a man intrinsically better because he is a chemist instead of a street sweeper?  Surely it is preferable to have an excellent character and sweep streets than to have a bad character and be a highly paid chemist?  In no Holy Scripture has one ever found that the wealthy, the powerful and the educated shall inherit the Kingdom of God!  The standard of God is far different from the standards of men and has nothing to do with external appearances but only with intrinsic values.  Bahá’u’lláh advises every man to learn a trade or profession, whatever else he may know as well.  A trade is usually associated with some form of manual labour; ‘Abdu’l-Bahá Himself knew the trade of weaving mats.

My own recommendation is that if a pioneer can afford a servant in countries where they are available and not too expensive, then by all means engage one; it will give employment to a person who is struggling to earn a living; it will also give the pioneer more time to teach the Faith by being freed from domestic chores, especially if he is himself employed and thus has less time to devote to his Bahá’í activities.  In engaging servants, the pioneer should not be foolish and sentimental, accepting them just because they want the job or have been recommended by a relative or

because he feels sorry for them.  This method seldom works satisfactorily and the pioneer can well be imposed upon by a dishonest person or saddled with someone who is much more of a liability than an asset.  He should choose a servant as he would go to a shop and pick out the best apple or the best flower and not the worst one.  His home will then be run better and a happier atmosphere prevail than if he is constantly worried by or scolding the person he has engaged.

There is another very important point involved in this question of engaging servants; pay the servant what other people in the country pay theirs and no more.  There is a sound reason for this.  The economy of every country is bound up with local wage scales and the purchasing power of its own money in its own way of life and is not based on the standard of the country the pioneer came from which, in all probability, was a developed rather than a developing one and had a quite different economy.  If the pioneer over-pays (doing his mathematics as we all do when we go abroad, namely, in our own currency which we are used to), he is guilty of two errors, to me both unjust:  in the first place he spoils the standard by which other, local people are paying their servants and which is in accord with their income and their nation’s standard of living, and by doing this he creates a lot of ill will against himself on the part of others who must employ servants to do exactly what he is now paying more for his servant to do; in the second place, by doing this the foreigner—in this instance the Bahá’í pioneer—usually ruins the servant he has employed by accustoming him to a wage for his services which he will be unable to get from either his own people or from those more experienced foreigners living in his country who are sufficiently intelligent to pay only the local price.  This may sound far-fetched but it is not.  The servant thus treated vaunts himself as more highly paid than

others and when the day comes that he has to accept a job at the lower market price for domestic labour, he is usually disgruntled and discontented and may even become unemployable.  It all comes back to the same principle:  adapt yourself and your thinking to the ways of the country you are living in and do not try to impose your national standards and customs on other people.

Another wise piece of advice for anyone who finds that for the first time he can afford a servant is not to invite him (or her) to eat with him.  It will not be understood or appreciated, is not necessary and would not be done in the servant’s own village circle.  If the servant is a Bahá’í and is going to a Feast, a meeting or a teaching course, the pioneer, the servant and all the other believers will share together, for we are all Bahá’ís together, but in daily life each of us has his place and things run smoother if we accept this principle and respect it.  A basis of kindness, fairness, dignity and respect on both sides establishes the best relationship between employer and employee.

Above all, the pioneer should avoid forcing his food ways on his employee.  I have come to the conclusion that probably the strongest, most ingrained of all habits of the human race is its food habits.  This is not the place to debate the merits of this, but the fact remains and it is a very pertinent fact.  If a servant should turn out to be an ardent Muslim or a Methodist and spends all his time trying to convert others to his doctrines, the pioneer is not going to like this propaganda and pressure, so let him not try to force his foods and his eating habits, his customs and his beliefs on his servant.  “Do unto others as you would be done by” applies in a great many fields.  We must always respect another man’s right to be himself and always bear in mind that the individual not engulfed in western civilization is likely to be a hundred times more polite than we are and that he will, in his

courtesy, say “yes” when he would like to say “no” and even if one explains to him—and thinks he understands—that he should be quite frank and not say “yes” if he means “no”, still he will do it because it is his ingrained custom.  So leave him free to eat his own diet if he wants to.

The pioneer should neither nag a servant all the time nor allow him to shirk his duties and make a fool of his employer, because neither the one extreme nor the other is good for either his character or the one who employs him.  It seems to me if the pioneer engages a servant he has a clear duty to not spoil his character or future employability by being over-indulgent or unduly over-dominating.  Most domestic servants in the East and Africa are men; they often have wives and families at home in the villages, and this is their way of life.  The pioneer should not interfere in it and insist they should come and join the man in the city or live in the pioneer’s home unless that was included in the terms of employment.  Often the wives are keeping up not only the man’s farm (to which he almost invariably plans to return) in his village but his place in tribal life as well, and neither he nor his wife and their families have any desire to change it.  The golden rule is “mind your own business”.

One last admonition:  the pioneer (or travelling teacher), in his desire to show his great spiritual detachment and his love for his fellowmen, as well as his trust in their higher qualities, should not leave money and valuables lying about openly or easily accessible and thus put undue temptation before others.  A person who, without this flagrant temptation, is normally honest may, for one reason or another, suddenly succumb and who then is more to blame, the one who provoked this dishonesty or the one who was too weak to resist?  Remember that through your folly or carelessness you might be instrumental in helping another person to be dishonest.

Mud huts and stick homes

If I had another lifetime to live I would devote part of it to studying native houses and their style and the materials used to build them.  I have the highest conceivable respect for mud as a building material.  Not long ago I went to the site on Lake Tiberias where some of the Bahá’ís had once lived on lands bought in response to the words of Bahá’u’lláh regarding the vicinity of the River Jordan and there, to my great surprise, I discovered that the houses had been built of sun-baked mud bricks reinforced with straw; in one of these now ruined houses ‘Abdu’l-Bahá used sometimes to spend the night.  The ancestral home of Bahá’u’lláh in Tkur is, I understand, constructed partially of adobe—a polite word for mud.  The tower of Babel was mud and so were the famed walls of Babylon.  The villages of the Africans, the Indians and other South East Asian nations, the homes of many people throughout the Americas and in China, as well as the Middle East, are mostly made of mud.  Mud, being earth, is surely God’s richest gift to men.  Having many times slept and lived in mud huts, I am well aware of their advantages and disadvantages.  The main reason I mention them in this book is not only to remove most “educated” people’s strong prejudice against them but to help the pioneer to counteract the growing feeling of inferiority that is slowly spreading among villagers who live in mud houses because of the scorn and criticism most white men pour upon them.  I have heard of Bahá’ís rebuking people for living in a “mud” house, saying they are Bahá’ís and should know better!  A more unwise and ignorant remark, not to mention unkind, one could scarcely find.  People live in mud houses because they must and have no choice.  But I believe they should be told—and we who come from city houses should know—that all men,

in all continents, have made serviceable and often beautiful houses of mud, which has marked advantages over other building materials.  Mud is found almost anywhere, it is the cheapest building material in the world, it is cool in the heat and warm in the cold and buildings many stories high, in places as far apart as Tibet and Bolivia, are successfully built of it.

Let me give some examples:  houses in Africa are built either round or square, often with very wide over-shadowing eaves that shade the walls and create a sort of porch all around the house where one can sit or work.  This shading of the wall is one of the most “modern” architectural techniques in the desert areas of the United States as it greatly lowers the temperature of the interior, but they have been doing it a long, long time in Africa.  Some houses have a gap of 3 or 4 metres between the top of the wall and the roof which lets hot air and smoke out, a dim light in, and provides ideal air-conditioning.  Roofs are usually of deep grass thatch, the result being a house that in the hottest heat of noon, in the hottest season of the year, is still an oasis of coolness.  A number of times I went from such huts to a school or other building of cement, with plenty of windows and a tin roof; the heat was as intense as a furnace and I wondered how the health of the children would stand up to it in the long run.  On some occasions I have helped a local community to build its Bahá’í Centre—with one stipulation:  that the roof should be the best quality of thatch.  It is impossible, when it rains on a tin roof, to even hear people singing at the tops of their voices, the din of the rain is so deafening, as I have found out to my cost on many occasions when I could not give a talk at a school or other “modern” meeting place.  On the contrary, when it rains on thatch one is not disturbed by the sound.  I have often wondered, as plastic can be

a by-product of other industries, if in the future an imitation plastic grass in strips will not be developed and made cheap enough so that it can fulfil the function and have the advantages of the natural thatching materials (insulation, coolness and pleasing appearance) and be more enduring because one reason villagers are giving up thatching with grass or special branches of trees is that increase of population and agriculture is making them scarcer and they have to be carried greater distances.

One day, when I was visiting the San Blas Islands in Panama, I waited for the Chief to join us in the typical big meeting hall of the village.  Having nothing to do but wait, I studied the building which, according to their local methods, is entirely constructed of straight branches of trees or saplings, about 4 imetres in diameter, stuck in the ground with a space of 2 or 3 imetres between each one.  An immense thatched roof of sloping eaves, set on tree trunk pillars, covered the whole building.  Between these columns were swung the hammocks of the Indian Chiefs and headmen, whose custom it is to lie in them and smoke and consult (and doze!) while lesser fry sit around on benches next to the wall.  (I never got higher than a bench.)  That meeting but had 40 hammocks, was about 8 metres wide by maybe 16 metres long, cool, shady and solidly built.  Being an architect’s daughter, by the time the Chief turned up I had paced it all off, was very enthusiastic over it and proceeded to tell him what I thought of it.  He was an old, fat, wise Indian Chief and after we had finished discussing what I had come to see him about, he said I was the only person who had ever praised a single thing the Indians had and to come again!

This stick construction is widespread in South America; I even slept in a stick house in an Indian village in Argentina.  It is ideally suited to the weather for it gives shelter from the

rain and sun while at the same time allowing a constant free circulation of air.  Contrary to what I had supposed, I found, when I stayed in one, that passers-by, unless they were sufficiently vulgar (which the Indians were not) to go up and glue their eyes to the gaps between the sticks, could not see clearly into these houses but the person inside could see very clearly what went on outside.  Sometimes, depending on the climate, one finds in Africa that the mud house has given way to a similar stick house or one with half-height walls of woven matting; indeed, the latter type of construction occurs in many parts of both Asia and the Pacific Islands.

Many African tribes have special techniques in making their mud floors:  in some places in Uganda they produce a polished surface as smooth and beautiful as wood and resistant to water; in Lesotho I have seen polished floors of exquisite design reminiscent of those produced in Italy with marble marquetry.  In many places all over the world the ingenuity of man has created functional, ornamental, liveable buildings  are economic in every sense of the word and, unlike the vast majority of modern constructions, grace the countryside and blend into the beautiful setting of nature rather than hideously scarring it.

How to furnish a nice place to hold meetings

Where to entertain guests is often a problem for the pioneer.  If he lives in a rented room his landlord may object to his holding meetings in it, and some other place where he can teach people must be found.  If he has an unfurnished home, however modest, of his own, the question is how to seat people for meetings at a minimum cost.  The following suggestion is based on an old Turkish custom.  In the Mansion of

Baha’u’llah at Bahjí, in many of the rooms there is a long seat running the whole length of one wall; the name of this is a mandar” and it is the best and cheapest way to furnish any sitting room.  In the old days it was built as part of the wall and one could probably copy it in constructing a mud hut, but the method suggested here is practical and cheap; the point is the exact dimensions must be followed if it is to be comfortable instead of uncomfortable.  A mandar is much more useful if it runs the entire length of a wall, right up to the corners, but can be any length one likes; a large one can even run along one wall, across the top of the room on the second wall and down the other wall so that the whole room on three sides is lined by this couch; it depends how many people one wants to accommodate; it has the great advantage of seating more people than is possible by any other method.  A mandar is also quite wide enough to be used as a couch to sleep on and this extra space is often of great help to pioneers because it means they can put someone up overnight.

Again I reiterate, the dimensions given here are what make a mandar really comfortable; if they are not followed, and you get the idea your legs are too long or too short for this proportion, or you cannot easily find wood the right size, and it will be all right to change these proportions, you will find that you have an uncomfortable piece of furniture to sit on.  To build a proper mandar boards are required:  loose planks of unfinished raw wood can just be laid side by side, or the pieces joined; their length depends on the length of the mandar but the width must be 75 imetres (30 inches).  This is the width of the bench one sits on.  If you are building a mandar say three metres long against one wall, or one and a half metres long and then joining to make a corner, you have to make your measurements either by what you want or perhaps by the length of the wood planks available.

In other words, do it the most sensible and economic way.  Remember that in making the corner one section must be pushed right up against the wall into the corner and the end of the second section, which is going to go down the other wall, must be pushed up against it so there is no empty corner.  Mandars can also be placed in such a way that the corner is left empty and a small table or lamp put in it, but it is much more practical, as far as seating capacity is concerned, if the mandar fits into the corner.  If you use loose long boards they can rest on trestles on the top of which the boards are laid, or on a series of wooden boxes that happen to be the desired height, but if you build your bench solid, like a table, legs can be attached under the corners and also in the middle for extra support, if the mandar is very long.  The height of your mandar from the floor to the top of the boards should be 33 imetres (13 inches).  More than this will not produce a comfortable seat because on top of this must now be placed a mattress the same width and length as the boards and about 10 to 13 imetres high (4 to 5 inches).  This mattress can be stuffed with cotton, with kapok (a natural vegetable silk-like fibre that grows on trees in many parts of the world), some type of straw or grass, or even excelsior or foam rubber—though the latter will be hot and may be much more expensive.  The final part of your mandar consists of the cushions that form a solid row all along the wall, the same length as the seat.  Again, the proper dimensions of these make all the difference in comfort.  They should be about 35 imetres high (14 inches) and 15 imetres deep (6 inches).  Their length will depend on how many divide into the length of the mandar itself.  In other words if each wood seat is 2 metres long you can have either four cushions 5 metres in length or, if your mandar was only 15 metres long, you could make two cushions 75 metres in

length.  The cushions look much better if they tend to be longer rather than shorter, so study the length of your mandar and divide it into three four or five cushions, if it is a very long one, or two or three if it is a shorter one.  Now you have your benches, mattresses and cushions.  How they are covered is part of their charm for it is very easy to do and can cost a little or a lot depending only on what material you care to buy.  Your material must hang over the front of the mandar so it clears the floor by 2 or 3 imetres (12 inches) then come up over the mattress and be smoothed across it towards the wall, any excess being tucked under the mattress.  This simple process means you need no fancy cover for the mattress, your goods can be either a beautiful handwoven wool blanket or yardage of some inexpensive and cheerful African or Indian print or other dress goods, or a good quality of upholstery goods with a design or plain, as desired.  Remember a plain goods shows dirt much quicker than a patterned goods.  Your cushions can either be in slip covers of the same material or you can take a length of the same goods, cut it in half lengthwise, first tuck it well underneath the row of cushions where they are standing up against the wall on the mandar mattress, then bring the material up over the face of the cushions, across their tops and fold down between the wall and the cushion at the back so that the goods form a tight long band the whole length of the cushions at the back of the mandar.  Be sure and remember that at either end of the mandar you must leave about 4 metres (16 inches) extra goods in order to fold back over the end of the cushions and make a neat joint against the wall.  The same thing applies to the material that you stretch over the seat of the mandar.  Be sure in making your measurements that it goes the full length, not only to the end of the mandar, but that you have enough to turn the corner

and carry the goods up to the wall and tuck in at the back, holding it in position by either safety pins or basting the two together.  In this way people who are looking at the mandar can neither see under it in front nor is there an open and ugly gap at the ends.  Make your measurements very accurately to begin with, drawing them on a piece of paper, and you will have the main furnishing of your living room ready in no time, and at little cost, to seat many guests.

Either make your mandar yourself or, in most countries, you can easily order it from a local carpenter.  Many times people who make sofas have already available standard seats for arm chairs, about 8 by 8 metres or 8 by 6 or 75 metres and 10 or so imetres high, and these can be adapted without having to make something special for the mandar seats.  Your mandar’s length can then be based on the number of these units you find it convenient to buy and put in your room.  Also, sometimes cushions that approximate the dimensions of the cushions that you require for the back of your mandar are already available and it saves the trouble of either making them or ordering them.  Remember that soft cushions are absolutely useless as these cushions are like the back of a chair, to support the back comfortably, and not for sleeping on.  Whatever you do, be sure that the length of the boards, the length of the mattress and the total length of the row of cushions at the back is all going to come out the same size because it will be very ugly if something is too short or too long.  After giving it considerable thought I have come to the conclusion that one reason the proper proportions given above for a mandar are so important is because it is far less tiring to sit when your knees are raised higher than the level of your buttocks.  The Africans discovered this long ago and all their native style chairs and stools are based on this principle.  I have found one can sit a long time on a small, hard,

wooden stool with no back rest and not feel tired because of the position.  Another convenience the mandar offers is that one can draw ones legs up onto it, sit cross-legged or sideways and more or less lounge and relax.  Extra scatter cushions can be placed on the mandar as needed so that people whose legs are shorter can put an extra cushion behind their backs and sit with more comfort and these can also serve as pillows at night.

Throughout many continents and countries, in village and town alike, inexpensive tailors are available, often sitting out on the street in front of a shop, who will, for an extremely small sum of money, sew up the seams of your cushion covers, hem the sides of the cloth you wish to use for your mandar or, for that matter, do any other sewing you require, including making you a suit, a shirt, a nightgown, a dress or whatever you ask them to do.  Be sure however to check the measurements and leave nothing to chance.  If you are wise you will stand and watch it being done, or at least stand long enough to see it is going to turn out exactly as you wish it to.  It also is a very nice opening to chat about the Faith with the man doing the sewing, providing of course he is at all interested.

In nearly every country of the world there are all sorts of cheap and charming things used all the time locally that can help furnish a room.  To use such things fulfils two objectives:  first of all they are, being handmade, more pleasing to the eye than a lot of cheap junk imported from abroad, and second, it makes the local people see that what exists in their own background is not despised and unworthy in comparison with the products of western civilization, that on the contrary, we appreciate, enjoy and prize the things made by them.  On my long African safari I always had one or more wooden stools, usually bought for about $1.00 apiece, and I now use them constantly here in the Home of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá.

As a night table, as a chair when camping, or to sit on and sort things out when they are spread all over the floor prior to packing them, they are charming and useful articles.  In fact, in Bahá’í centres where we visited, my four stools (I actually had four in the back of the Land Rover) helped to seat the friends at more than one large meeting.  The number of baskets I have travelled with in my Land Rover was at one time twentyone—big ones and little ones; some of them beginning the journey in East Africa, crossed the entire continent twice; they have held dishes, stoves, utensils, clothes, the commissary department, papers, cameras anything and everything.  Cheap, strong and often of very fine workmanship, they have proved invaluable, from 3 feet high to 3 inches wide.

Clay cooking pots, water pots and dishes of village workmanship may be found in all parts of the world.  They are useful, extremely inexpensive and often beautiful and decorative.  If you study the way the natives themselves use them, you can use them in the same manner, in other words you can cook in them.  You can also buy them for other purposes such as flower vases, storage jars in the kitchen, or jardinieres to put potted plants in.  Aside from carvings—which are usually made for tourists and can sometimes be decorative and not too expensive—the things people make for themselves for their daily use can have a great deal of ornamental value and charm:  wooden scoops and spoons, gourds and large calabashes, native cups, sometimes made of horn, a local bell for animals made of two rolls of wrought iron joined together by a loop at the top—one pioneer had artfully used a number of these as a wall decoration by the simple expedient of hanging them one above the other on a string.  Straw mats of all kinds, qualities and sizes can be bought cheap all over the world and used on the floor, or hung on the

wall to not only fill in a space or provide a background for other objects, but to cut down echoes in the place where meetings are held, especially if the building is of cement which has a tendency to produce a very loud echo and very metallic tones of voice.  These mats withstand a lot of wear and tear and may be washed in cold water.

I have noticed that in an astonishing number of small local markets in Africa and Asia and other countries real Chinese porcelain dishes can be bought, the heavy kind we associate with Chinese restaurants and which is usually called Canton wear.  They are not only infinitely more beautiful than plastic, but are often very much cheaper than anything else available in the shops—including expensive imported China sets—and you can make up a selection of useful dishes which you and others will enjoy at a very small cost.  A lot of native weaving, wax prints or tie and dye batik are now common all over the world; these may be used for innumerable purposes:  they make delightful table cloths and also bed covers; they can cover a mandar; a decorative one can be hung on the wall.

To live in a room with nothing beautiful or charming to look at is wholly unnecessary.  One weed flower in a Coca Cola bottle is still better than nothing at all, and tin cans, pierced at the bottom, can be little flower pots for strange and charming wild things dug up and brought home; even the spiky top of a pineapple, cut off before peeling the fruit, can be stuck in a glass bottle or a jar and will start to grow roots and create the illusion of a cactus plant or an aloe; a sweet potato placed so one tip is in contact with water will grow a luxuriant green, creeper-like plant; a dish of lentils or wheat wetted and kept in a dark place for a few days will begin to sprout and when brought into the light and kept watered, but not soaked, will produce a thick patch of brilliant green grass in a soup plate or any other flat dish, that

cheers your heart every time you look at it, though it will not last forever as it soon grows tall and falls down—but it has its temporary charm; even a mango seed can be coaxed into a small tree and so can an avocado pit.  There is no end to the charm and beauty you can create in your own little nest when pioneering just by having a little imagination and taking the trouble to do it, and it will certainly impress and encourage other people who come to your room and see with what simple, humble things from their country you have made a place that is so attractive, so full of charm to the eye and so full of love and kindness for the heart.

Eating and other habits

Everyone has different customs and there is no justification for believing ones own are better than those of some other group.  At least onethird of the world’s population eats with chopsticks—in my personal estimation the most civilized way to eat.  Another third eats with its hands.  At first I found it very hard to do this, but once I got used to it I liked it and have done so all over India, many times in Africa—and sometimes at home!  It is the universal custom of those who do so to wash their hands both before and after eating.  The Guardian told me Bahá’u’lláh has instructed not to put ones hand in the common dish; in other words, not to put ones hand into the common dish and then back to ones own mouth; obviously this method of eating is very unsanitary and can convey germs to other people.  In India people usually eat exclusively from a dish of their own but in many other places all eat from the same dish, and one must be very careful not to hurt the feelings of other people unnecessarily by telling them to change their ways and assuring them they

are breaking the instructions of Bahá’u’lláh!  When one is a guest in Africa one is almost invariably given a separate plate for ones self, and served separately as the people are very considerate towards their guests in every way.  Incidentally one should accept this courtesy and not argue about it, insisting one wants to be treated just like everyone else.  Accept to be treated the way your host wishes to treat you, for this may be a unique occasion in his life and he has planned it very carefully.  Indeed, the host may even have borrowed special dishes and cups from neighbours in order to honour his guest.  Sometimes in South America I was served my boiled beans and potatoes in the corner of my poncho, no plate being available.  Occasionally in India I ate off a dried leaf dish or from a banana leaf.  It makes no difference, the main thing is that the pioneer or travelling teacher should be as considerate and polite as those who are entertaining him.

How humanity chooses to eat in the future is not our concern, and Shoghi Effendi, in writing, has made it very clear that those friends who come from the Middle East and speak Persian and Arabic have no right to introduce into other countries any laws or ordinances of Bahá’u’lláh not already given by him to the West and other parts of the world.  Any changes in our manners and customs should come directly from the World Centre, the Universal House of Justice being the sole judge of when the time is ripe to introduce these changes.  In its  of Kitáb-i-Aqdas, it states in its introduction:  “The number of laws binding upon Bahá’ís is not increased by the publication of this .  ”

All those arising to serve the Cause in distant lands, with different historical backgrounds from their own and hence different customs, should bear these words of our Supreme Body in mind and refrain from any interference in the personal lives and customs of others and above all in any criticism of them, for not one of us has any guarantee that it will be our viewpoint, our way of life or our concept of what is befitting, that will prevail in the as-yet-unborn world Bahá’í society of the future.  The Faith is born, the World Order and its nascent institutions are born, but Bahá’í culture and civilization must come later, as a natural outgrowth of the impregnation of men’s lives with the spirit of the Teachings, and that will take a long time to develop.  Who knows how much or how little of the cultures and ways of life of Africa, Asia, Europe, the Americas and the Pacific Islands will be included or discarded in the world of the future?  Meantime there is quite enough for us to teach people without interfering with their local customs.

My own feeling is if you are going to hurt or offend other people, stay at home in your own country.  You are not only free to go out and carry the Message of Bahá’u’lláh to other people, you are strongly urged to do so; you are not free, however, to hurt and insult them and impose your ways on theirs.

To get back to customs.  A pioneer in Africa described her heartbreak over her first entertainment in her own home for the local community:  she was very excited about this occasion and arranged a beautiful buffet table, worthy of Paris, London or New York, with each dish nicer looking than the other; she then invited the friends to help themselves; in the resultant crush and grab she was so disillusioned she broke into tears and fled from the room!  What had happened?  Her intention was excellent, she honoured her guests as she

would have done at home in America and that surely was the right spirit too.  Her mistake was she neither consulted with anybody who knew the country better than she did nor did she study the people she had come to live among.  She just assumed it was like her own background and it was not.  People in Africa, except those who have completely identified themselves with white ways, usually through having studied abroad, eat by taking from the common dish or else each is served a dish with his portion on it.  To serve ones self to a buffet is a completely unheard of situation except to the sophisticated city dweller.  I found myself in a comparable situation at the beginning of my trip in India when my host placed before me a steel tray with a lot of little dishes on it and no cutlery.  I was appalled and did not like to ask for a spoon as I suspected he might not have one so I hastily asked the guest next to me to start eating so I could see what he did and do likewise!

So many situations of this kind, which often shock and embarrass everyone concerned, could be avoided if only we all had enough sense to ask beforehand about local customs and then tried to follow them; “When in Rome, do as the Romans do” is good advice.

On another occasion of cultural mix-ups a pioneer presented her African servant with not just an ordinary wax candle but a very expensive and fancy one and a rarity outside of Europe or the States and told him with great kindness this was a present for his mother, who was a poor woman living in a village; when the servant later presented the candle to another Bahá’í, whom he had previously worked for and was fond of, his new mistress was hurt and surprised.  I pointed out to her that the African had shown much better judgement than she had, giving to some one who could really appreciate it an object  to his own mother would have had no

special value at all and would be not only a waste of money but a wasted gesture of kindness.  A  of sugar would have been a much better gift.  But the American was not really thinking at all, she was only being kind in a completely thoughtless way.

Many times pioneers are puzzled and a little hurt by never being invited to the homes of the people among whom they have come to teach the Faith; no matter how many times a person comes to their home and is entertained and asked to partake of meals, there is no similar invitation extended to them.  The reason for this is not so much (as pioneers often think) that their native friend is ashamed of his home and cannot offer any hospitality commensurate with what he has received, but rather that people call on their friends uninvited, as a matter of course and with no formality at all.  It would never occur to them that one invited a friend to ones home—the friend would come if he wanted and be welcomed, at meal time or any other time!

This unreflecting approach we have towards people of another culture reminds me of an incident in my own life when I was first married and came to live in the oriental home of Shoghi Effendi’s family; in the East, as in almost all parts of the world not highly westernized, the floors are swept by a special grass tied in a bundle and coming to a point, in other words a short whisk broom; by doubling over, and usually keeping the legs stiff, the floor is energetically and quickly swept.  I was horrified and felt the poor maids were ruining their backs and rushed to buy them the long-handled brooms and mops I was used to in my part of the world; they politely used these once or twice and then they never reappeared!  After observing the oriental women in my new environment I noticed they were very supple, could bend over and touch the floor at almost any age and did not have stiff backs or

hips.  I promptly changed my own method of sweeping to theirs so that my back will not stiffen in my old age!

Over and over throughout Africa one sees grass being cut by a machete or sabre, as it is called in some countries—a knife about half a metre long and 6 imetres wide—which is swung in a slashing motion by a man doubled over and, to ones astonishment, seems to do almost as good a job as an electric lawn mower.

Throughout all countries—India, Africa, the West Indies, and so on—where people carry their loads on top of their heads they have straight backs, flat shoulders and walk like kings and queens.  The sense of balance this creates is unbelievable; I once saw a woman gardener in Tanzania sweeping the public paths, easily and rapidly, bent double, with a small empty tin can perched on her head!  Also in those countries of Asia where it is the custom to sit cross-legged people have supple joints and straight backs and their knees do not stiffen up as ours do as we get older.  The principle is live and let live and learn something new and better if you can.

Transportation and equipment

After crossing Africa from east to west and returning via its southern territories I can reiterate what was my conviction when I undertook this great safari:  I would not care to do it except by Land Rover, and 36,000 miles of driving only served to confirm this personal opinion.  This does not mean, however, there are no alternatives.  Many factors are involved in buying a car for serious travel in the interior—whether it is the interior of Africa, Asia or Latin America or some other place with bad roads—but I stipulated three essential points:  the car must have four-wheel drive; it must

have high clearance from the ground to the bottom of the chassis; it must be a car for which I was reasonably sure I could buy parts to replace those broken or worn out.  After driving in deep mud ruts (usually made by huge trucks that plo through the dirt roads in the rain and leave a high hump or bank of mud down the middle), or driving through loose sand a foot or two deep with its terrible drawing power (which I hate much more than mud!), or driving where roads have deteriorated until there are drops of a few feet, or huge boulders spaced all down the road, or gullies where the rain has eroded it into deep crevices, or cutting out over the open country where one was told it was easy, but where more and more bushes, tall grass, fallen trees and ditches appeared in the middle of nowhere—after these experiences I am convinced really nothing will get you where you are going but a four-wheel drive vehicle which ensures both power and control in emergencies.  Land Rover spare parts can be found all across the African Continent.  The second car which is highly considered, but which is not yet as widely distributed, is the Japanese Toyota; the third in this category is the “jeep”, but it is seldom seen so spare parts would be difficult to find; this applies to Africa.  The distribution of four-wheel drive cars in other countries I do not know much about.  What I do know is that one should be very careful, making thorough enquiries from people who have direct experience (in this case not likely to be a pioneer committee) as regards the places one plans to go, road conditions and available transport.

My own experience has been such that I would not, for such a country-to-country tour as the one I made, buy a car fitted up for sleeping.  I have met a number of young men, and a few couples, crossing Africa in this way but I cannot visualize where they put their car at night; a great many

highways—even 1,000milelong ones—are not only dirt but single track and it seems as if only divine protection ever gets one to a place where two cars can pass each other if they meet—fortunately infrequently, for traffic in many developing countries is scarce and most of it heavy transport; the evidence of what happens when two cars meet unwisely lies in decomposed wrecks the length and breadth of the African Continent, for there is almost no means whatsoever of salvaging and repairing them.  The road is either a narrow track with dense jungle right up to the edge of it, barely a place for a pedestrian to draw aside, or it has a deep drainage ditch along it or swamps surround it.  One can easily drive an hour or more just looking for a place to pull over to have lunch.  Therefore where could one park at night to sleep?  I admit in theory one could get permission to stay inside a village but it would take pretty thick-skinned people to eat and sleep with the entire population watching wide-eyed every move; the children in particular never get tired of the performance!  One cannot blame them, there is no entertainment and the arrival of foreigners is the equivalent of the circus coming to town.  Finally, if one should find a place to pull off the road, every motorist will stop and ask you if you need help.  This is a wonderful spirit and one I admire very much but not if I am trying to get a night’s sleep.  What all this amounts to is that if I had known all this in the beginning I would not have bought the large-size Land Rover with a view to sleeping in it.  The smaller model would have done me just as well and Land Rovers are expensive cars to run.  For the cross-continent trip I had an extra sixteen-gallon tank for petrol added underneath at the back, in addition to the ten-gallon tank of the car, and I carried  more gallons in a Jerry can.  As petrol stations can be few and far between and all the petrol sold out when one

does find one—and no more until the next delivery at an uncertain future date—this was an essential precaution and saved us from being stranded on many occasions.  For the same reason it is advisable not to wait till the tank is empty but fill up as one goes along.  Another wise precaution was to have a sheet of iron riveted on the chassis underneath the engine to protect it from damage if one hit a rock or other protuberance, which we did unavoidably many times.  We also carried two spare tires, extra springs, electrical parts, fan belts and two oil filters.

Trips to places with little or no facilities for modern life and machines must be very carefully thought out.  We carried eight gallons of extra water that was safe to drink; five gallons went into the radiator the day the fan belt broke in the middle of a waterless wilderness.  We also carried a well-stocked first-aid kit and various medicines, all of which were essential if one happened to need them.  We had folding army cots, heavy in weight but durable and compact in size, which stood high off the ground; lots of camp beds are lighter but flimsy and low lying perhaps women have a strong objection to being near the floor and things that may be crawling about!  We also carried mosquito nets, small feather pillows (rubber is terribly hot), cotton bed covers, light blankets, thin foam rubber mattresses, and sleeping bags—each person’s bedding rolled tight in its own duffel bag to keep it clean.  We had gas camping stoves, a small one for boiling water and a big one for cooking; it is advisable to carry a supply of refills for in many places they may be unavailable.  Automobile accidents are steadily increasing all over the world.  I pass on my personal rules for driving which have kept me accident-free in not only deserts and jungles but in New York, Chicago, and many of the world’s busiest cities.

1.	Obey traffic regulations.

2.	Never pass a red light, even in the middle of the night on an empty street; you risk accustoming your mind to the possibility of passing on it and may forget and do it the wrong time.

3.	Never pass another car on a curve or a hill where you cannot see the road ahead for a considerable distance to be sure nothing is coming; more people are killed through ignoring these two precautions than any others.

4.	Never believe the other driver either knows or intends to obey the law; take all the precautions for both of you; if you believe he is irresponsible enough to cause your death you may live longer.

5.	Obey the white traffic lines on roads, even if you do not agree with them.

6.	Both in the city and open country I keep my eye on bicycles and children and often slow down when I have to pass near them as both can suddenly swerve into your car or dash across the road in front of it.

7.	I always slow down when I see cattle on the road or approaching it from the side; cows in particular will usually keep right on coming across the road in spite of the blowing of your horn; even if you do not mind killing an animal remember either the jolt to your car or the blood could throw you into a skid and lead to your or your passengers’ death.  It is not right to kill or injure a human being for the sake of avoiding killing a cat or dog or cow.

8.	Do not believe that as you approach another car coming towards you the driver will give way to you or even give you your own half of the road; usually he will not and expects to force you to give way; all over the world wrecked cars testify to the fact that neither side gave way!

9.	If you become sleepy when driving either stop by the road and take a nap, get someone else to drive, or wake yourself up thoroughly; that fraction of a second when the driver dozes off causes many fatal accidents.

10.	Driving, especially at high speeds, is a serious business so pay attention to it; to my horror I have watched temperamental drivers take even both hands off the wheel to wave them about in conversation; to take your eyes off the road to look at the person beside you or even those in the back seat is even more dangerous!

11.	If a person is a really bad and irresponsible driver do not ride in the car with him.  There are good and bad drivers all over the world.  In Africa, however, I found the black man was less reckless than the white.

12.	Do not let other people drive your car.  I realize this rule—on long drives and where there is only one car in a family—cannot be strictly adhered to but if you want to keep your car safe and in good condition do not pass the keys around to oblige other pioneers, travelling Bahá’ís or local people.  Cars, in a great many countries, are often prohibitively expensive and even irreplaceable.  It may seem unkind and selfish to not share yours but by being firm in this respect

it may mean the only “Bahá’í car in a whole district or even a whole country will go on existing to serve the Faith for a while longer instead of being piled up as a wreck by a poor driver or deteriorating rapidly and becoming a total loss.

The mosquito net

Many people nowadays have never heard of a mosquito net and try to cope with mosquitoes by using sprays or repellents.  Although these are helpful they are expensive, sometimes irritate the skin and soon wear off; the same is true of the sprays, which I often found about finished me off but left the mosquitoes in fine shape.  Outdoors (or indoors if one has no effective fly screens in the windows), a mosquito net ensures protection and therefore a restful night’s sleep.  In tropical countries a net can usually be purchased ready-made; it consists of an oblong top the size of a bed, which has four “walls” of net hanging down all around which are tucked under the mattress so nothing can get up the sides; the four corners of the top must be attached to something to keep the net suspended above the bed or sleeping bag.  I always tie strings about 2 metres long or more to each corner so that I can hang it up when I want to and not have to look about for a usually non-existent piece of string.  I also carry with me a supply of nails to drive into the walls and attach the strings to.  One of the most important things for a person going to sleep in villages to take along is a mosquito net.  Although the following type of net can sometimes be purchased (they make them in Japan and elsewhere) I herewith give directions for making one and will call it by its Persian name “Pashiband” out of gratitude for all the services it has

rendered me in my life in the Holy Land and while travelling.

I always make mine sufficiently large for a double bed but a single bed size may be made the same way.  In principle a Pashiband is a small room with a floor, four walls, ceiling and “door”; the top and bottom should be equal in size and for a double bed roughly 2 metres or 220 metres long by 2 to 22 metres wide; the bottom should be of cotton goods—sheeting, unbleached cotton, anything that washes well and is durable; the top may be of the same goods if one wants the “roof’ to be more substantial, so as to catch dust and what-not that might fall on one, otherwise the same mesh or net as the sides will do and be cooler; the sides or “walls” should be at least 15 to 2 metres high in order to make a big airy space inside; to sleep in a mosquito net is always hotter than not having one, and though in cool places this can be an advantage, in great heat it can be suffocating—but safe; these sides are then sewn all around to both the top and bottom pieces, putting small tucks every now and then and specially at the corners so it will hang freely and not stretch the walls tight; in other words for a 2 metres length of the ceiling allow material 215 metres or more in the wall attached to it.  On one side of a wall leave an opening in the middle, vertically (where seams meet will do) about 15 metres high and to each side of this opening sew a piece of net the same length and about 15 metres long, and at the top and bottom sew these pieces together, but leave the end open; if you visualize it you now have a square room and on one side a sleeve or corridor of net extending out about 15 metres; hem this open end and put a 15 metre or longer draw-string through it:  The method is when your mosquito net is erected over the bed or floor or ground you plan to sleep on, and

you have put your pillows, cover and anything else you want inside, go in through the opening and pull the sleeve in after you, draw the string tight and wind it a number of times around the bunched up end so no insect can get through.  If there is danger of lice or fleas, the mesh of the net will have to be much finer to keep them out, like a muslin or gauze, but for mosquitoes and flies, netting will do.  Not only is one protected by this type of mosquito net from all insects but also from scorpions, spiders, lizards, snakes, rats and so on—a psychological if not actually a physical advantage not to be despised!  I remember how comforting it was in a mud but in Zaïre to lie in my net and look at all the big flat spiders on the wall—outside.  Incidentally, in Latin America special mosquito nets are made which fit over hammocks.

Sanitary facilities

The cycles that characterize the intimate bodily functions of people’s lives—what they eat, how often, when and how they sleep, birth customs, their habits of elimination—are deeply ingrained by hundreds if not thousands of years of purely local habits.  In this respect we are very much like birds or animals of different species.  Denying this or getting upset about it and pretending it is all nonsense does not change anything at all.  We are dealing with hard facts.  I recall how, after weeks of travel together, our African companion began to show signs of flagging.  It became apparent that day after day of our relatively “well balanced” western diet, with plenty of protein and fruit, was going to make him ill; his ancestors had been eating, perhaps once or twice a day only, a large quantity of unrefined starch (in his case millet

was the staple, but all over Africa it is the same, the staples being cassava, maize, sorghum, plantain or millet) and his whole body and its proper functioning required this ancestral diet.  I do not say eventually a villager will not get adjusted to another diet if he has to—but why should he?  Throughout Africa most Africans living in cities still eat their traditional food.  People with other habits might not be able, without adding some fruit and vegetables, to live more than a brief period on this African diet without indigestion and constipation.  It is interesting to note in this connection that an article in the Readers Digest for 1971 states that the African’s bowels on his diet move more often than the American’s do, colon cancer being very infrequent, whereas in the United States it is now the second most prevalent form of cancer; investigators consider that the huge increase, during the last eighty years, in the consumption of refined carbohydrates (mostly white flour and white sugar) is responsible for this.

The toilet facilities of the vast majority of the human race (countries following European-North American culture excepted) are based on the natural position of squatting, not sitting; it is better and far more hygenic but can be very difficult to adjust to.  People who may have become accustomed to linking the activities of their intestinal tract to reading would do well to develop a more natural approach as the environment and facilities of the vast majority of toilets all over the world are not conducive to either sitting or reading.  Ignorance of customs associated with what is a daily part of life for everyone can impose hardships that may wreck a pioneer’s or travelling teacher’s much-needed work in rural areas.  Of the complications village life involves for over-civilized people not the least is this question of toilet facilities.
