When touring South East Asia some years ago I was distressed that I never slept in the homes of the village Bahá’ís as I had so often done in Africa, but always had to stay in a hotel or a Government Rest House or Dak bungalow.  At last I got the explanation:  there just are no toilets in the villages and there are usually no trees or bushes to provide cover; it is the custom of the people to get up before dawn and disperse in the dark over the fields around the village.  This is true of conditions in the Andes of South America as well.

Some African village toilets (where they exist) are too small or too flimsy in construction for a large person to enter and white ants may have eaten away the wood supports in the floor, making it even more risky for a heavy person; usually these village toilets are much cleaner and less smelly than the European facilities in towns and cities and certainly a hundred times more pleasant and sanitary than the old-fashioned privy I remember in rural districts of New England in my youth.  One has to learn, in many places, to use nature.  One also has to learn that all bodily functions are taken as a matter of course, treated unself-consciously—but with delicacy and no curiosity—by all rural people, all over the world.  Travellers will do well, in every part of the world, to take their own toilet paper as no paper exists in rural areas; villagers should not be foolishly looked down on for this because often no paper was ever manufactured in their culture, it is unknown as a toilet article, and there would be no money to buy it even if they knew it existed.  All over the East water is used instead of paper, including in the cities.  Also, in most places, people blow their noses into the air—a most revolting and unsanitary habit.  But having neither handkerchief nor Kleenex where would you blow yours?

Adapt and economize

The following general information may assist those who are keeping house in a strange land; although much of it applies to Africa, undoubtedly a great deal can be adapted to other places.

An inexpensive and excellent grater for grating carrots, apples, cheese or anything else, can be easily made from any small tin can by punching holes close together in the bottom, from the inside outwards, with a nail; if a fine grater is desired use a small nail, for a coarser grater, use a larger nail.  Another possibility is to take a shallow aluminium dish or basin and punch holes in it in the same way, always from inside outwards, using it as both a grater and a colander or strainer for washing vegetables, straining cooked rice, and so on.  Any small, round, empty tin can can be used as a cookie, biscuit or doughnut cutter.  Any tin can can be used as a baking dish in the oven; an oblong, square or round biscuit or candy box without the lid will do very well; smaller tins, even empty herring or sardine tins, can make individual baking dishes.  When any of these things get old or rusted they are easily replaced at no extra cost.

Keep aluminium dishes and vessels clean by a soap that does not have a lot of soda in it as this tends to both stain and adversely affect the aluminium.  One can clean all pots and pans with fine sand, or ashes sifted from the fire, as an abrasive; it is not necessary to buy an imported expensive abrasive powder.  In India, when the women went to the well with their beautiful brass water pots, I noticed they sat down and polished them with the wet earth around the well until they shone like pure gold, rinsed them off, then filled them with water and came back to the village with them on their heads, looking very much like the handmaidens of a queen carrying

golden vessels to a feast.  Do not leave iron vessels wet as they will rust very quickly; dry them off at once and rub on a little cooking oil.  Clean rusty iron knives with lime or lemon juice and ashes; brass and copper can also be beautifully cleaned by this method.  Take half a lemon or lime (it may be a left-over half after one has squeezed most of the juice out) and dip it in fine wood ash and then rub it on whatever needs to be cleaned; it immediately removes the tarnish; an orange or grapefruit may also be used but lemons or limes are best.  Leaves from a papaya tree will have the same effect used in the same way.  Never under any circumstances cook acid foods in brass or copper containers or stir with brass spoons.

Wood dishes are widely found in many places in the world and wooden mortars for pounding grains and foods.  They are cheap, useful and charming and if taken care of properly, very endurable.  Wooden mortars are used universally in Africa for pounding foods and grains to prepare them either for cooking, or eating if they are already cooked.  If the starchy grain sticks to the sides of these, soak in cold water to loosen before washing with a rough sponge or the outside husk of a coconut.  The fibre of a coconut is excellent for scouring utensils, wood floors or anything else, but always go in the direction of the grain of the wood, otherwise it will roughen it up.  Do not use steel wool or a metal abrasive on wood under any circumstances.  Generally it is not necessary to use soap on wooden vessels unless they are very greasy and need a good washing.  Whenever I have used any of my wooden bowls (which I do daily), I wash them off quickly, put a little cooking oil on my hands and rub this over the wood while it is still moist; it not only protects the wood from drying out and cracking but gives it a soft and beautiful finish and incidentally puts some much-needed oil on my own hands.  Wooden spoons are immemorial in all parts of the world for cooking.  Unless wooden

spoons and bowls have been well smoothed off with sandpaper when bought, I myself always give them an extra finish, sanding them over until they are satiny soft.  Sometimes one finds that after using them the grain again roughens up and then they need to again be carefully sanded, washed and oiled.  In the end, one has a wooden object that is so lovely to look at and to feel that it becomes a treasure, even if it is used daily.  Wooden spoons, incidentally, can be very ornamental hanging on the wall.

In a great many parts of the world, native—in other words what is used locally—pots and receptacles, are not only cheap but useful and charming to look at.  Throughout southern Africa three-legged iron pots are inexpensive and cannot be excelled if one cooks with them in the way they were designed to be used.  It is a fascinating sight to see an African housewife cooking, with three or four of these tall-legged pots standing around in the fire, from a little tiny pint size for sauces to a big pot large enough to stew an entire sheep in.  She adjusts the temperature by either putting intense flame right under the middle of the pot or pulling the firewood out and allowing the pot to sit in a bed of ashes to stew gently.  This method not only gives a special flavour to food but is ideal for people who are able to cook on a hearth or out of doors; it astonished me to see how rapidly even a large pot of water would come to the boil.  The staple food of South Africa, the mealie meal (maize porridge) is cooked in this way and is a most delicious dish if properly done.  Ragouts, goulashes, soups, anything and everything can be cooked in such a pot.  It is not only ideal when cooking for large numbers of people but also for camping, as I found when I cooked in mine in the Kalahari Desert.  The best way is to learn the technique of how to cook in one of these pots from a village woman.  These three-legged pots, which had their origin in

the inglenooks of Europe, cannot, however, be used on electric or gas stoves as they stand too high for the heat to reach them properly and also they are in danger of toppling over.

Native clay pots anywhere in the world can be used for cooking, Observe how the local people use them and do the same thing.  They are not only very cheap and ornamental but make food taste good.  Many people consider aluminium is not a good metal to cook in from the standpoint of health.  Be this as it may, undoubtedly steel is the best material but it is usually very expensive.  Iron and enamel ware are also excellent for cooking but one has to be very careful of enamel because it chips off easily if it gets a blow and then can be dangerous because the enamel may go on flaking and parts of this sharp, glass-like material get into the food and be eaten unawares.

No matter how dirty the rest of the house may get, try to keep the kitchen clean and all kitchen utensils, cutlery, dishes, towels, and washrags as clean as possible because this is the place where one can most easily be infected through eating contaminated food.  Scrub the kitchen table well after it has been used.  Put vegetables away as clean as possible.  Hygiene is essential in the kitchen for the purpose of keeping the whole family healthy and it is more important fundamentally to have a clean dish towel and a clean washrag for the kitchen than it is to have a clean face towel or body towel for ones self in the bathroom.

One of the greatest causes of chronic ill health, as well as acute intestinal and stomach upsets, is stale cooking fat.  One should be careful when eating in restaurants that the fat being used is not stale; a sensitive nose detects the difference between the odour that comes from a restaurant where the fat is clean and fresh and one where it is stale or they are mixing in stale fat with the fresh supply, which is a very bad

thing to do.  One should use up what has been already used to fry or cook something in as quickly as possible and then start on a clean supply of fat or cooking oil, washing out thoroughly the receptacle in which one keeps ones oil or fat before filling it up with a fresh supply.  Stale fats place an intolerable burden on the liver and cause a great many ailments of a serious nature, including the production in the body of the wrong kind of cholesterol.  There is a great deal of talk today about cholesterol and arteriosclerosis, but our bodies constantly use and need cholesterol; eating the wrong kind of foods, one of which can be stale fats, produces the wrong kind of cholesterol which, among other things, is damaging to the arteries.

In many places calabashes or gourds are available and can be used to great advantage as dishes or containers.  Wash them with strong soap.  If they become dirty and rough inside they can be scoured out with ashes or fine sand and a cut lime or lemon, or one may use as an abrasive finely pounded up egg shells.  Having had a great many of these calabashes, I have found that they are wonderfully useful as a salad dish, a fruit dish, a catch-all for papers, beads or other articles; sometimes they have been carved or decorated, in which case they can be hung on the wall as ornaments.

Baskets are found in infinite variety everywhere in the world.  They are cheap and useful receptacles for anything and everything:  the baby, the dog, ones laundry, onions, potatoes, for travelling, for picnics, for the bread or the fruit to be served on the table.  Personally, any room is not a place fit to live in if it has not got a wastepaper basket, so this is likely to be one of my first purchases anywhere I find myself, even for a few days, providing of course that I am not already travelling with one in my car, which I usually am!  Wastepaper baskets keep the house cleaner, longer.

Backache, in highly civilized countries, is steadily increasing as a national ailment and one reason is because of the way people sit and lie, with the spine curved, the chest caved in, the shoulders humped forward.  Primitive people have beautiful posture and straight backs and I never heard backache was one of their problems.  They sleep flat and hard, either on mats on the ground or bench-like beds.  One of the worst things a pioneer can do is to put up with a bed that has springs so soft he sinks down into it; he would do well to put boards over the frame and then a mattress on top or go buy himself a hard local bed.  One pillow or none is good for the neck.  It is interesting to note that the pillow of the Far East, and also some places in Africa, is a hard support that fits under the neck only and does not shove the head up at an acute angle from the shoulders the way our European method of sleeping does.  Incidentally, hammocks are a widely used form of bed in many countries; I found them hard to get used to but once the technique of sleeping in them is mastered, they are extremely comfortable, practical, cool and healthful.

Whatever people have done to meet the requirements of their own environment is usually highly intelligent, practical and suitable, and to observe these different customs is fascinating; many times what they do we might like to imitate; at least we should not criticize or interfere in their ways.  To give a few instances:  carrying babies on mothers’ backs is ideal for the child, though many western mothers will not accept what seems to them an unnecessary sacrifice and burden; carrying loads on ones head or a back-pack frees the hands and balances the body; the carrying stick of Asia is based on the same principle, putting the weight down the main shaft of the body rather than pulling the arms out of the shoulder sockets or pulling the frame to one side.  I watched

Chinese travellers in Hong Kong, men and women, with their suitcases and bundles all strung along a notched carrying pole across their shoulders; they frequently shifted the pole from one shoulder to the other but were obviously not as burdened as those carrying the same things in their hands; a great longing to try a carrying pole myself came over me!  The Africans, people in Asia and elsewhere can all squat on their heels for an indefinite period of time (my Arab laundress washes all the clothes in this position) yet the average North American or European cannot even get into this position, far less maintain it, but people who do so are less likely to have stiff knees, hips, backs and ankles.  The lesson is:  native ways are very often the best way, so learn where you can and adopt and adapt for yourself.

Your personal environment

A dog, in memory of the days when he was a jackal or a wolf long before he came to dwell by the hearth of man, will often turn around a number of times before he finally lies down.  The reason he does this is that he is following an ancestral urge to flatten the deep grass and make a little nest for himself.  A bird works hard to feather its own nest.  Each one of us, it seems to me, needs an environment of his own, however small or tenuous it may be, that houses his own personality.  Perhaps this is why so many people never pioneer or go forth as travelling teachers, because they cannot, snail-like, leave their shells, and they are afraid of feeling naked and defenceless in a strange place.  They probably do not recognize the feeling as such but subconsciously it affects them.  To me one can pioneer or even travel with at least a token of ones own walls with one.  It is hard to put into words:  some people are

great readers; some people are musical; some people, like me, derive their joy mainly from their eyes; each one of us may be used to something or need something there near him to be his home, so why not try and take it with you?  You cannot take the grand piano or your bass viol very easily, but a less pretentious instrument can go along, or take a little tape recorder and tapes with the music on it that you need and love, or when you get to your new home buy a local instrument and have fun learning to make music on it and new friends to teach you how; take a few favourite books or economize and buy new books for a new home; what is a room without a book?  Or, like me, make a small oasis in your room with a cheerful little table cover of some native print or weaving, a wood bowl or basket on it, a picture of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, in a nice little frame, your travelling clock set on its own little ornamental doily—something, anything all your own, an extension of your own personality.  Did you say you had no table beside your bed?  Why not a native stool, or a plastic stool, or even a carton box with a gay rag over it?  You would literally have to be in a most extraordinary place not to find something to feather your nest with.  An empty soup or bean tin may be your vase for a handful of flowers or wild grasses or even dead leaves; you can paint it or stick contact paper on it to make it look gayer, or even an empty glass jar or a bottle may be used as your vase.  One very ordinary small green bottle was so pleasing to my eye with one flower in it that I carried it over 10,000 miles!  There is no excuse for your not having something in a room to reassure you that your personality is there and this is your home.

Whatever else the pioneer may take into his new life, I would suggest he take his Bahá’í library as it will do more to make him feel at home than anything else, he will need it constantly for comfort and reference and he will usually not

be able to duplicate it where he is going—no matter what anyone assures him to the contrary!

Job openings

The following kinds of work may ensure a pioneer can earn his or her living:  a first-class shorthand typist in English almost always can get a good full- or part-time job; if a woman knows dressmaking fairly well she can usually earn all or some of the money needed to stay at her pioneer post; hairdressing likewise can be a good profession; experience as an accountant for a woman or a man can provide job openings; a skilled automobile mechanic seems to be the male counterpart of the skilled secretary and is rarely unable to find employment; the same is true for someone who can do radio repairs and watch and clock repairs.

Developing countries are faced today with a tremendous flow of their own youth coming out of high schools and colleges and looking for employment—employment which, if the government does not provide it, can lead to unrest and demonstrations.  The range of jobs, therefore, still open to foreigners is getting more and more limited every year.  My own observation is that generally (though every country differs) workers in highly skilled trades, or people in the highest brackets of professions such as medicine, education and the sciences, will still get jobs relatively easily.  This is not a hard and fast rule, however, for pioneers on the spot often get established in all kinds of jobs, professional and otherwise.  Top flight surgeons, particularly in such fields as eye, nerve or brain surgery, I think would be very welcome in most countries and able to render not only a great humanitarian service in the exercise of their profession but

also greatly enhance the prestige of the Faith as such professional men have access to the highest government and diplomatic circles.  The trouble is, alas, that even Bahá’ís, when they reach this level of prestige and efficiency, are unwilling to go to developing countries because they cannot stand the local operating facilities, lack of administrative efficiency and in general an atmosphere very different from what they are used to.  We need some David Livingstones in this Faith.

Many countries are so desperately in need of teachers for their primary schools that they are putting in their own even ungraduated high school students as teachers; they are often willing to employ any foreigner who has a BA, even though this may not be in the field of teaching at all; the pioneer may find he gets this kind of a job relatively easily when he is on the spot—providing he is in a place that accepts outside teachers.  Many universities, colleges and high schools are willing to employ foreign educators but usually demand high qualifications—maybe on the principle if one is paying then one should get the best; whereas they might accept a BA or MA from their own national they would like a PhD from abroad!  Again, in the field of education, the specialist may increasingly stand a better chance of employment abroad.

Skilled laboratory technicians are also a good profession for pioneers to have.  Everything to do with radio and television—except perhaps in the technical field of maintaining and repairing equipment—is usually a tightly closed door to foreigners as jobs that confer prestige (as these do in developing countries) are much coveted and usually handed out as plums; pioneers, no matter how well qualified, will practically never get jobs of this kind.  Detailed information regarding job possibilities should be

sought by prospective pioneers from their National Spiritual Assemblies and Pioneer Committees.

The pioneer who does get a job—as well as the committee which may have sent him out—must bear in mind that not infrequently he will not be paid his salary either on time or even after the lapse of months.  These are hard facts that are little known and may lead to the pioneer’s getting in a position where in disgust or despair he quits his job and leaves; it would be much better if the committee at home tided him over and he persevered in his job, because it has cost a lot of Bahá’í money, and usually sacrifice on his part, to get him there, and given enough time the situation will usually resolve itself; it just takes patience and perseverance.  I know of one pioneer who only got bed and board at her school and was never paid the salary they had promised her!  She remained because of the need for pioneers in that country; without the job she could not have done so.

Although the assistance given pioneers from the Bahá’í Fund constitutes neither a job nor a salary, it had better be mentioned here that committees, National Spiritual Assemblies or individuals who remit the money the pioneer receives should be extremely careful to ensure it reaches him monthly or quarterly, whatever the arrangement is—on time.  I have known cases of pioneers going hungry because of delays in transmitting the sum they needed to subsist on.  Not only are the local believers usually too poor to borrow from but the pioneer, having been sent out specially from another country to teach the Faith, is too ashamed to let anyone know the position he is in.  It is the duty of those concerned to set up a method of payment whereby there will be no delay; the mails, the postal and banking procedures in many countries, are such that there are often long delays.

Bahá’í elections and administrative bodies

In a world becoming daily more political minded, where rural peoples are being educated politically and urged to become politically active, the attitude towards Bahá’í elections within our own administrative order—which is in no way political but has to do exclusively with the management of the internal affairs of a religious community—is of the greatest importance.  It is essential for the pioneer or travelling teacher to impress upon the believers—new or old—the fundamental, and one might say extraordinary difference between our Bahá’í elections and elected bodies and the elections and elected bodies which constitute the various political systems of the world, which are essential for government and the management of state affairs and, one may add, to which we Bahá’ís are obedient, each in his own country.

Whether in the election of delegates to the National Convention or members of a National or Local Spiritual Assembly the basis is the same:  the believer, without previously discussing the merits of any individual with other Bahá’ís, or paying any attention to either foolish and immature or ambitious and scheming people who may seek to electioneer for themselves or their favourites or their relatives, is called upon to vote for only those persons he is conscientiously convinced are the most worthy ones available.  In voting for the members of Spiritual Assemblies he should particularly bear in mind that Shoghi Effendi said they should “be representative of the choicest and most varied and capable elements in every Bahá’í community.”  But no matter whom the Bahá’í voter is electing the attitude and principles involved are the same, the primary one being a prayerful, dispassionate, unprejudiced and guidance-seeking approach:  it is as if

the voter, prior to casting his ballot, makes his heart and mind a mirror turned to Bahá’u’lláh for guidance; he prepares that mirror through his prayers; perhaps he already had some ideas of whom he would vote for, but in his pre-voting prayers he asks for a wisdom greater than his own and seeks only to do what is best for the Cause.  As Shoghi Effendi expressed it, the voter must approach his task with a purity of spirit which alone can ensure he discharges it as ‘Abdu’l-Bahá would wish him to do, in other words in a rarefied atmosphere of “selflessness and detachment”; ‘Abdu’l-Bahá Himself assured us that every Assembly elected in this spirit will be truly inspired and assisted by God.  The Bahá’ís must be taught that Bahá’í elections deal with spiritual power, not temporal power.

We must seek, in those we elect to hold office as Assembly members, certain “prime requisites” laid down by ‘Abdu’l-Bahá:  “purity of motive, radiance of spirit, detachment from all else save God, attraction to His Divine Fragrances, humility and lowliness amongst His loved ones, patience and long-suffering in difficulties and servitude to His exalted Threshold”.  To this the Guardian has added that when voting we should also consider the record of service of the person being voted for (if such people are available), and, irrespective of any material considerations, seek the qualities of unquestioned loyalty, selfless devotion, a well-trained mind, recognized ability and mature experience.  Always realistic, Shoghi Effendi states:

If we but turn our gaze to the high qualifications of the members of Bahá’í Assemblies, as enumerated in ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s Tablets, we are filled with feelings of unworthiness and dismay, and would feel truly disheartened but for the comforting thought that if we rise to play nobly our part

every deficiency in our lives will be more than compensated by the all-conquering spirit of His grace and power.

There is no breath of worldly ambitions or considerations—money, position, prestige, influence, education—in any of the above-mentioned qualifications when prayerfully considering whom to vote for.  It is to me highly significant that the very first qualification mentioned by the Master is purity of motive; over and over again Shoghi Effendi stated how important it was, in anything, that ones motive should be pure.  The other qualifications are equally illuminating:  radiance of spirit—it recalls the first Arabic Hidden Word “My first counsel is this:  Possess a pure, kindly and radiant heart, that thine may be a sovereignty ancient, imperishable and everlasting”; detachment from all save God, attraction to His qualities, servitude to Him and unquestioned loyalty and selfless devotion to His Faith; humility and lowliness among ones fellow believers; patience and endurance in difficulties.

“Ambitions”, ‘Abdul-Baba is reputed to have said, “are an abomination before the Lord!”  None of the ambition rampant in the outside world must be allowed to taint Bahá’í elections.  The friends must realize that to be elected to an Assembly, appointed on a Committee, serve as a delegate or be a Board member are not positions of power and prestige or in any sense a reflection of the political world outside the Faith.  If ‘Abdu’l-Bahá, the son and Successor of Bahá’u’lláh, the Centre of His Covenant, the Perfect Exemplar and Interpreter of the Holy Writ said “My station is the station of servitude”, what right has any ordinary Bahá’í, if he holds any kind of office or performs the functions of a delegate, to consider himself exalted above others?  He should consider he has been given the privilege of serving, not of dominating, and do his best to follow meekly and prayerfully the example

of his Master.  “The keynote of the Cause of God”, Shoghi Effendi wrote, “is not dictatorial authority but humble fellowship, not arbitrary power, but the spirit of frank and loving consultation.”  The Guardian made it clear that the all-important function for a Bahá’í to perform was to spread the Message of Bahá’u’lláh and said that to vote or serve on an administrative body was not more important.

This does not, however, exonerate those who are elected from performing their tasks conscientiously and according to the teachings.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá writes:

In this day, assemblies of consultation are of the greatest importance and a vital necessity.  Obedience unto them is essential and obligatory.  The members thereof must take counsel together in such wise that no occasion for ill feeling or discord may arise.  This can be attained when every member expresseth with absolute freedom his own opinion and setteth forth his argument.  Should any one oppose, he must on no account feel hurt for not until matters are fully discussed can the right way be revealed.  The shining spark of truth cometh forth only after the clash of differing opinions.  If after discussion, a decision be carried unanimously well and good; but if the Lord forbid, differences of opinion should arise, a majority of voices must prevail.

The first condition is absolute love and harmony amongst the members of the assembly.  They must be wholly free from estrangement and must manifest in themselves the Unity of God, for they are the waves of one sea, the drops of one river, the stars of one heaven, the rays of one sun, the trees of one orchard, the flowers of one garden.  Should harmony of thought and absolute unity be non-existent, that gathering shall be dispersed and that assem-

bly be brought to naught.  The second condition …  They must, when coming together, turn their faces to the Kingdom on High and ask aid from the Realm of Glory.  They must then proceed with the utmost devotion, courtesy, dignity, care and moderation to express their views.  They must in every matter search out the truth and not insist upon their own opinion, for stubbornness and persistence in ones views will lead ultimately to discord and wrangling and the truth will remain hidden.  The honored members must with all freedom express their own thoughts, and it is in no wise permissible for one to belittle the thought of another, nay, he must with moderation set forth the truth, and should differences of opinion arise a majority of voices must prevail, and all must obey and submit to the majority.  It is again not permitted that any one of the honored members object to or censure, whether in or out of the meeting, any decision arrived at previously, though that decision be not right, for such criticism would prevent any decision from being enforced.  In short, whatsoever thing is arranged in harmony and with love and purity of motive, its result is light, and should the least trace of estrangement prevail the result shall be darkness upon darkness …

Shoghi Effendi himself also elaborates on the attitude and duties of those elected to Spiritual Assemblies:

The duties of those whom the friends have freely and conscientiously elected as their representatives are no less vital and binding than the obligations of those who have chosen them.  Their function is not to dictate, but to consult, and consult not only among themselves, but as much as possible with the friends whom they represent.  They

must regard themselves in no other light but that of chosen instruments for a more efficient and dignified presentation of the Cause of God.  They should never be led to suppose that they are the central ornaments of the body of the Cause, intrinsically superior to others in capacity or merit, and sole promoters of its teachings and principles.  They should approach their task with extreme humility, and endeavor, by their open-mindedness, their high sense of justice and duty, their candor, their modesty, their entire devotion to the welfare and interests of the friends, the Cause, and humanity, to win, not only the confidence and the genuine support and respect of those whom they serve, but also their esteem and real affection.  They must, at all times, avoid the spirit of exclusiveness, the atmosphere of secrecy, free themselves from a domineering attitude, and banish all forms of prejudice and passion from their deliberations.  They should, within the limits of wise discretion, take the friends into their confidence, acquaint them with their plans, share with them their problems and anxieties, and seek their advice and counsel.  And, when they are called upon to arrive at a certain decision, they should, after dispassionate, anxious and cordial consultation, turn to God in prayer, and with earnestness and conviction and courage record their vote and abide by the voice of the majority, which we are told by our Master to be the voice of truth, never to be challenged, and always to be wholeheartedly enforced.  To this voice the friends must heartily respond, and regard it as the only means that can insure the protection and advancement of the Cause.

The duties of a National Spiritual Assembly, who, Shoghi Effendi says, “constitute in the eyes of the people of their country the supreme body in that land that officially repre-

sents, promotes and safeguards the various interests of the Cause” are obviously more important than those of a Local Spiritual Assembly.  He states:

Let it be made clear to every inquiring reader that among the most outstanding and sacred duties incumbent upon those who have been called upon to initiate, direct and coordinate the affairs of the Cause, are those that require them to win by every means in their power the confidence and affection of those whom it is their privilege to serve.  Theirs is the duty to investigate and acquaint themselves with the considered views, the prevailing sentiments, the personal convictions of those whose welfare it is their solemn obligation to promote.  Theirs is the duty to purge once for all their deliberations and the general conduct of their affairs from that air of self-contained aloofness, from the suspicion of secrecy, the stifling atmosphere of dictatorial assertiveness, in short, from every word and deed that might savor of partiality, self-centeredness and prejudice.  Theirs is the duty, while retaining the sacred and exclusive right of final decision in their hands, to invite discussion, provide information, ventilate grievances, welcome advice from even the most humble and insignificant members of the Bahá’í family, expose their motives, set forth their plans, justify their actions, revise if necessary their verdict, foster the sense of interdependence and co-partnership, of understanding and mutual confidence between them on one hand and all local Assemblies and individual believers on the other.

As to the body of the believers, their role in supporting the Bahá’í Administrative Order is likewise made amply clear in these words of ‘Abdul-Bahá:

It is incumbent upon every one not to take any step without consulting the Spiritual Assembly, and they must assuredly obey with heart and soul its bidding and be submissive unto it, that things maybe properly ordered and well arranged.  Otherwise every person will act independently and after his own judgment, will follow his own desire, and do harm to the Cause.

It is the duty of the pioneers and the teachers to explain painstakingly and patiently these standards and these instructions to the believers so that they may ever-increasingly reflect the Bahá’í attitude towards both elections and their conduct when elected; villagers are usually practical and spiritual and a great many abuses we complain of are entirely the fault of those who do not explain the spiritual nature of Bahá’í elections to them, do not encourage them to pray, individually, before voting or for that matter either give them an opportunity to pray and reflect or create a prayerful attitude in the meeting at election time.  Over and over I have seen how people neither have patience themselves to pray and meditate before voting nor are willing to allow others time to do so; like children who are going to cut open a big cake that has a ring baked in it, they cannot wait to cut up the cake and see who gets the ring!  The atmosphere is very far indeed from turning the mirror of ones heart to God to receive the reflection of His guidance.

One of the greatest difficulties facing pioneers, as well as the National Spiritual Assemblies responsible for the work in those countries where there are thousands of Bahá’ís in rural areas, is to ensure elections, during the period from sunset on April 20th to sunset on April 21st (the Bahá’í day beginning as it does at sunset, not midnight), of Local Spiritual Assemblies, which must take place on this particular

day, according to the teachings.  In many cases new believers are too inexperienced to carry out such elections and careful plans should be made well in advance so that the friends not only know there will be an election on this day but provision is made for someone to go and assist them.  Often a car can go out with a number of people, dropping them off all along the line so they can each go to one or even more villages.  This is an excellent type of service for Bahá’ís living in towns to render and many a housewife would do well to make provision for her home and family and go out to share in this thrilling experience of helping to form a new Assembly.  Often teen-agers take on this job and there is no reason why capable and intelligent children should not help illiterate but excellent native teachers by writing down the ballots.

In addition to ensuring that Local Assemblies are elected it is also essential to make it absolutely clear that only enrolled Bahá’ís 21 years of age and older are eligible.  I well remember the unbelieving expression on the faces of members of an African community when I told them they could not vote for a very active young believer who would be 21 in a few days’ time.  To them it seemed just one more example of the highly arbitrary rules of white men!  But when I pointed to the photograph of the Bahá’í Temple in America and explained that the Assembly owned it legally so that no one could take it away from the Bahá’ís because the Assembly was registered as a legal body, and that if such a body has members elected to it who are not of legal age then the Temple could be stolen from us, they got the point, and were absolutely satisfied; so often we do not explain clearly why we must do things a certain way.

The supreme work of all Bahá’ís, whether acting individually or as members of a Bahá’í body, is to spread the glad

tidings of the appearance of Bahá’u’lláh in our midst.  Shoghi Effendi epitomizes this in these words:

As the administrative work of the Cause steadily expands, as its various branches grow in importance and number, it is absolutely necessary that we bear in mind this fundamental fact that all these administrative activities, however harmoniously and efficiently conducted, are but means to an end, and should be regarded as direct instruments for the propagation of the Bahá’í Faith.  Let us take heed lest in our great concern for the perfection of the administrative machinery of the Cause, we lose sight of the Divine Purpose for which it has been created.  Let us be on our guard lest the growing demand for specialization in the administrative functions of the Cause detain us from joining the ranks of those who in the forefront of battle are gloriously engaged in summoning the multitude to this New Day of God.  This indeed should be our primary concern; this is our sacred obligation, our vital and urgent need.  Let this cardinal principle be ever borne in mind, for it is the mainspring of all future activities, the remover of every embarrassing obstacle, the fulfilment of our Master’s dearest wish.

The following is a prayer of the Master  He revealed for use by a Spiritual Assembly; as many pioneers become members of Assemblies they may like to have it handy:

O God, my God!  We are servants of Thine that have turned with devotion to Thy Holy Face, that have detached ourselves from all beside Thee in this glorious Day.  We have gathered in this spiritual assembly, united in our views and thoughts, with our purposes harmonized to exalt Thy

Word amidst mankind.  O Lord, our God!  Make us the signs of Thy Divine Guidance, the Standards of Thy exalted Faith amongst men, servants to Thy mighty Covenant.  O Thou our Lord Most High!  Manifestations of Thy Divine Unity in Thine Abhá Kingdom, and resplendent stars shining upon all regions.  Lord!  Aid us to become seas surging with the billows of Thy wondrous Grace, streams flowing from Thine all-glorious Heights, goodly fruits upon the Tree of Thy heavenly Cause, trees waving through the breezes of Thy Bounty in Thy celestial Vineyard.  O God!  Make our souls dependent upon the Verses of Thy Divine Unity, our hearts cheered with the outpourings of Thy Grace, that we may unite even as the waves of one sea and become merged together as the rays of Thine Effulgent Light; that our thoughts, our views, our feelings may become as one reality, manifesting the spirit of union throughout the world.  Thou art the Gracious, the Bountiful, the Bestower, the Almighty, the Merciful, the Compassionate.

Warnings section

There are many pitfalls and tests for unwary or uninformed travellers and a few are mentioned here.

In many places in the world freedom of religion, speech and action do not exist; in some few cases the Faith itself is outlawed and no Bahá’í activity or teaching is permissible.  Under all circumstances the Bahá’ís must obey the laws and injunctions of the State they are in and its ruling party; they have no right to argue in such matters; wherever any ambiguity exists, or a delicate situation is not clear, recourse should be had to the guidance of the Universal House of Justice.

At present there are some places where the pioneers and local believers may suffer great inconvenience, and the Bahá’í work itself be placed in jeopardy, through their receiving Bahá’í books and newsletters or papers mentioning the World Centre of the Faith; individual believers should not seek to contact the friends in these places, or visit them unless they are sure they are wanted, or do any teaching if they do visit or pass through such countries.  If an individual is not sure of what is wise in any particular case he should consult his National Spiritual Assembly or a member of a Board of Counsellors as these institutions are kept specially informed by the Universal House of Justice itself.  In the present highly disturbed state of the world ever-greater wisdom is going to be required on the part of individuals serving the Cause in any area that has unusual problems and they should be sure they are working in accord with the wishes of the Universal House of Justice.

In the world today there are many States that follow a one party system and many of these States require by law that their citizens go to the polls.  A Bahá’í pioneer or travelling teacher who comes from a different political background may in such cases give very foolish advice to the local friends, based on his own country’s laws.  He should never, in circumstances such as these, tell other people where their duty lies until he has carefully inquired from the National Spiritual Assembly of the Bahá’ís of that country what the correct answer is.  The general answer is always the same:  all Bahá’ís are required to obey their country’s laws and government.

Currency regulations are very strict in some places:  one can only take in or out a certain amount of the local money; there may be a black market for foreign currency.  The laws of the country should be strictly obeyed by the Bahá’ís.  I

remember a case where a pioneer was put in jail for dealing on the black market—an event which greatly humiliated the local believers and the Faith itself in the eyes of the authorities.

On arrival in a strange city, young men in particular may find that they are approached and offers made to bring them liquor, drugs or girls; loose women abound in towns and cities all over the world and are quite shameless in hunting down their prey; to such approaches and offers the Bahá’ís must be so firm in their refusal that they gain a reputation for being adamant in virtue and obedience to the moral teachings of their Faith.  It will gain, for both themselves and the Cause, a golden reputation and the news will rapidly spread, reaching the ears of officials, missionaries and politicians alike—all of whom are increasingly aware of our existence and watching us.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá said:  “Should any one of you enter a city, he should become a centr of attraction … so that the people of that city may cry out and say:  ‘This man is unquestionably a Bahá’í, for his manners, his behavior, his conduct, his morals, his nature, and disposition reflect the attributes of the Bahá’ís.’”

Bahá’u’lláh has admonished us very clearly as to whom we should associate with and whom we should not:  “The company of the ungodly increaseth sorrow, whilst fellowship with the righteous cleanseth the rust from off the heart.”  “Beware!  Walk not with the ungodly and seek not fellowship with him, for such companionship turneth the radiance of the heart into infernal fire.”  “Infernal fire” is a very strong statement indeed!  He goes on to say:  “Wouldst thou seek the grace of the Holy Spirit, enter into fellowship with the righteous …”  Shoghi Effendi, over seventyfive years ago, wrote that as the “prestige, fame and influence” of the Faith grew the malice and ill-will of strangers and enemies would

correspondingly grow and that it therefore became increasingly important for every individual Bahá’í and Spiritual Assembly “to be on their guard lest they fall innocent victims to the evil designs of the malevolent, the self-seeking and greedy.”  If such warnings and advice are important to us when we are at home, how much more important are they when we go abroad?  The golden mean, the middle of the way, between being loving and kind in our attitude towards all humanity, and, at the same time, suspicious and on guard to protect both the Faith and ourselves from the “ungodly”, “the malevolent, the self-seeking and greedy” must be sought and we must pray for wisdom, guidance and protection.

Be careful not to be robbed; dishonesty is steadily increasing, handbags are snatched in the streets in many cities daily; luggage robbery in airports and stations is common, the thief abstracting the contents of thin plastic or cloth suitcases without even unlocking them by just slitting them open with a knife; pickpockets abound everywhere; one is constantly cheated in paying bills and short-changed in stores.  There are ways of defending ones self against these dangers:  if you are a woman, form the habit of neither laying your handbag down and walking off, whether in a public place, a meeting or the home of strangers, nor giving it to other people to carry—not so much because everyone is going to rob you but because if you form this habit of always having it on your own arm or in your hand, when it is not there you will notice it and this precaution prevents you from losing it.  When travelling, with my money, passport, etc.  in my handbag, I make a point of having one with a number of compartments inside so I can put away my checks and the bulk of my money separately; nothing is more dangerous than to have everything shoved loose into one big container as you risk pulling things out accidentally and also letting other people

see what you have inside; slip the handle of your bag over your shoulder, particularly in crowded places, and hold it tight under your arm; in this way it is almost impossible for anyone to snatch it or open it.  Men should carry their money in an inside pocket and put a safety pin to close the top; it sounds silly but side and hip pockets are most easily picked and inside pockets too.  The method is two pickpockets work together, one jostles you, in a bus or crowd, from the back and profusely apologizes so that you turn your head to look at him while the other then presses against your chest, slips his hand in—and off goes all your money!  In the old days people had a money belt under their clothes and some wise travellers still use them.

On arriving in a country make a point of ascertaining what the equivalent is in local money of your own unit you are accustomed to—an American dollar, a Persian tmn, an Indian rupee, or whatever it is.  Fundamentally this does not solve your problems because you do not yet know what is cheap or expensive judged by the local economy, but it does give you some way of controlling what you spend.  If the local money is 1,000 to 1.00 you may be horrified and think for one dollar you are going bankrupt; if it is 2.30 to 1.00 you may be fooled into thinking it is cheap and spend money like water and go broke in no time!

When paying any bill anywhere add it up and count your change carefully to be sure both are correct; do not be ashamed to take your time; you are only obeying the Báb Who said we should neither cheat nor be cheated.  When paying in foreign money I find this is one of the moments I am most likely to be cheated, for the hotel cashier (or whoever it may be) will try to confuse me on the rate of exchange and short change me.  Many people employed in hotels, restaurants and shops have found the tourist is the easiest person

to cheat as he seldom checks anything or counts anything.  Shoghi Effendi invariably added up all bills himself to be sure they were correct and if he was overcharged pointed it out, and if he was undercharged also pointed it out!

Half of our troubles, inconveniences and accidents in life come to us because we are not concentrating on what we are doing.  Focus your attention on the task at hand, whatever it is.  When you want to be dreamy and full of philosophical contemplation, stop doing anything else!

When travelling, be careful not to hand your belongings about to every hand stretched out to help you; in the general confusion of friends who have come to receive you or see you off things can be forgotten, lost or stolen.  You must be responsible for your own belongings and cannot expect other people to pay as much attention to them as you yourself should.

Be very careful not to make fun of other people to another English- or Persian-speaking person like yourself, as very often, by what seems to be a sixth sense, or telepathy, they understand what you say and are hurt and insulted.  Even if this is not the case, when a person uses a language no one else understands except his compatriots, people always jump to the conclusion they are being discussed; it is better to not exclude and offend people in this manner or else politely translate immediately into their own language what you have said.

Health and pioneering

In view of the amount of devotion, effort, money and self-sacrifice involved in pioneers’ reaching their posts, it seems to me that for them to ignore every rule of health and nutria-

tion when they get there is inexcusable, for by doing this they not only place in jeopardy the success of their own endeavours but the over-all, world-wide pioneering plans of which they form a part, and which are being carried out by various National Assemblies under the guidance of the Universal House of Justice.

It is inevitable that travellers will sometimes pick up infections and fall ill; people from Europe and North America, going to countries where various types of dysentery are endemic, are more prone to getting it because they have not developed a natural resistance to germs they were perhaps never exposed to at home; people from Africa and Asia, on the contrary, who go out to pioneer in such countries have usually much less trouble as their natural immunity has been built up at home.  There are ways of eliminating such hazards or at least cutting them down to a minimum.  From the long experience of a North American in Asia and Africa I have certain general suggestions to make:  one must not be so afraid of germs that one becomes too intimidated to do anything; the body constantly has disease germs in it and is exposed to new ones all the time, yet in spite of this onslaught usually manages to survive; the best protection is to try and keep in as good health as possible because a healthy body defends itself against infections.  The three things that run down ones health the quickest are bad eating habits, chronic disease and continuous exhaustion; when one is exhausted and allows a few days, or even a few hours, of real rest, lying-down-and-sleeping kind of rest, the reservoir of vitality fills up again, but if one persistently pushes ones strength—and this does not mean necessarily bodily strength but can be nervous and mental strength—to its uttermost limit, day after day and week after week, then almost any germ can cause a minor or major illness.

The human body is capable of extraordinary feats of endurance and people everywhere in the world are daily performing what seem like minor miracles.  Bahá’ís, with the power of faith and prayer to reinforce them, will be astonished at the nature and range of the things they do as pioneers which they never in their wildest dreams considered themselves capable of doing.  It is very much like racing, all the effort comes forth and lasts the course till the finishing line is reached and the race won, but then there is a respite.  It is the respite rather than the race that is often the weak point in the activities of pioneers.

Older people, I believe, are in many ways actually tougher than young people.  My mother used to say that they had to be or they would not have reached that age!  Forty years, sixty years of coping with life not only give older people both a physical and psychological stamina often lacking in youth but an accumulated resistance to disease, all of which in many ways make them ideal pioneers.  Young people, however, are often better suited to take the really tough roughing-it required by trips to the wilderness where they seem able to sleep in the corner of any hut, eat anything that comes their way, tramp over mountains and paddle up jungle streams.  To meet the needs of the present stage in the prosecution of ‘Abdu’l-Bahá’s Divine Plan we not only need hundreds of David Livingstones—the great and famous Scottish missionary who was not only an intrepid explorer but an outstanding servant of humanity—but pioneers of all ages and backgrounds to fulfil all kinds of needs in hundreds of pioneer goals.  But whoever they are, whether older or younger people, more wisdom is needed in safeguarding their health; the suggestions and information included here are to help them to do this.

Rest

It is difficult to say what constitutes rest because what is rest for one individual might make another person exceedingly nervous.  One excellent definition of rest is change of occupation, which can be reading a book or a magazine or going to a cinema for one person and playing a set of tennis or swimming or climbing a mountain for another.  I, for instance, find reading restful, cooking restful and going to the market—particularly a big native one—very refreshing, whereas another person might find it an ordeal!  The people, the things they are selling, the pageant of it all, takes me completely out of my thoughts and myself and therefore has a restful effect.

But rest, in the sense of horizontal repose—lying down in other words—can be essential for ones health, either as a daily, brief respite from the wear and tear of activities, or as an aid to curing a more serious condition.  Young people usually sleep long and heavily and if given a chance can quickly recover depleted energy or throw off some ailment just by a good bout of sleeping.  But older people should learn the value to their health of just lying down.  All my life I have resented afternoon naps and hated lying down, but after fifty years passed I was forced to learn their advantages, and they are tremendous.  To a dear doctor friend I owe a precious gift, namely, the knowledge that if one lies down and rests—even if one does not sleep a wink all night—the body recuperates and the store of energy is replenished; of course it is not as good as sleep, but she taught me to not be afraid of sleeplessness, to stop panicking and saying to myself:  “My goodness!  It is 2.30 a.m. and I have not slept a wink, I will be a wreck in the morning!”  When one overcomes this fear of not

sleeping, this idea that one can do nothing because one has lost sleep, a major battle in life has been won.  Especially if, like myself, one refuses to take sleeping pills or tranquillizers; one must learn to lie quietly and accept the sleeplessness, adjust ones mind to it, read or think of pleasant things and try to relax the body; often this changed mental attitude in itself induces sleep.  Before going to bed a dessert-spoonful of honey, plain or dissolved in a cup of hot water, can put one to sleep as it has a very calming effect on the nerves.

A great deal to do with sleep depends on ones attitude towards a bed; a bed should be considered a place to sleep not a place to worry, a blessed spot which God in His mercy has given us to lie down in.  As one grows older, when at last bed is reached at the end of the day, one learns to thank Him most humbly for it.

In many parts of the world, especially the tropics, the custom of taking a siesta, an afternoon nap or rest, is common and is an excellent habit.  Strangely enough, even five minutes of sleep can have a refreshing effect, like passing an eraser over a blackboard it seems to brush away the tensions in both mind and body.  As little as fifteen minutes lying down flat can have a recuperative effect.  If one is ill, or has a bad cold or a bout of dysentery, to go to bed for a day or two, if at all possible, can be very helpful in addition to therapeutic measures.  If one has been walking or standing for a long time, particularly in the heat, to lie down flat on ones back, with the legs stretched out straight but slanting upwards—in other words the feet higher than the head—changes the circulation and is very soothing and well worth the effort of arranging pillows, a coat, a board or even a sack or suitcase under ones legs in order to get in this position.  Swollen legs and ankles in the heat can also

be a symptom of loss of salt in the body through heavy perspiration; salt tablets or taking additional table salt may help this condition.  It has been found that people not used to tropical heat lose, at least until their bodies adapt themselves to the climate, a great deal more salt in their perspiration than native people, even though they may perspire just as heavily as the newcomers.  Aside from these circumstances, however, it is advisable to take as little salt as possible in ones diet.  Reflection on how to handle ones problems, and self-discipline, are essential for a pioneer to learn.

Next to protection against protracted exhaustion as opposed to the normal, logical effort of carrying a difficult undertaking through to the end, however tiring—comes wisdom in what one eats and above all what one drinks.

Water

After years of experience I am convinced that the number one thing for a pioneer to seriously pay attention to is drinking water.  Whenever I go out to villages on a trip I make sure I have a container of either water that has been boiled (which effectively kills all germs) or properly filtered through some apparatus (in many countries people have them in their homes) or comes from a reliable city water supply.  One might suppose that what comes out of the tap in a big city is reliable but it may not be; in some cities the filtering system may be faulty or through other factors the tap water is contaminated.  Halazone tablets are a reliable water disinfectant and may be used if necessary but taste awful.  When we crossed Africa twice by Land Rover we sometimes carried five or more gallons of water

3,000 kilometres—from one safe supply to another—where we washed out our plastic containers and filled up again with new water.  The fact that the water was weeks old never did any harm and we either drank our own or made sure the water we could get was safe; by this I mean that it came from a deep well or had been boiled.  Tea and coffee are usually safe because boiling water is used to make them.  We always accepted such drinks—whether we felt like it or not—so as to not refuse the loving hospitality of the friends.  In India and Africa the tea or coffee, together with the sugar and milk, are often all boiled together to make a sweet hot drink that is safe and pleasant—though it tastes usually quite different from what one is used to!

Because soda water can be bought in a bottle with a proper sealed top does not for a moment mean it is safe to drink; I myself have seen a little “water factory” in Sikkim in the shed behind a very dirty home where bottled water was being put up; how the bottles were washed (if at all) and where the water came from heaven alone knew, yet seeing it for sale in a grocery store or served in a restaurant one might be tempted to think it was hygenic.  I have also seen a sealed bottle of water with a dead fly sealed in.  The best policy is for the pioneer or traveller to supervise his own water supply, carry some in a flask with him to drink and be very firm about the fact that because he is a foreigner he has to be careful not to get intestinal upsets and this water is his main precaution and this is why he cannot share it.  The native people of any place are adjusted to their own water and, whether it is clean and healthful or dirty and contaminated, there is nothing the visitor can do about it and sharing his own glass of special water is not going to solve their problems.  On one occasion in India I remember we went over to an island off the mainland where a big meeting was to be

held and we were to sleep the night; all the sanitary facilities were privies above little irrigation ditches cleaned out by the tide; our precious bottles of water for the trip were laid down somewhere and after my lecture not a drop was left, it had all been drunk up.  The danger of our getting dysentery, through drinking the local water, was very great and as there was little fuel and no Halazone and we were guests in a very humble home, it was difficult to ask for boiled water or even tea so we had an anxious and thirsty time of it—and did not drink the local water.  It must be always borne in mind that sewage in the sea is a dangerous source of disease not only through direct contamination but through eating shell fish from such waters.

I put all this emphasis on water because I have seen so many cases during my travels where pioneers have not only had “upset tummies”, which were usually caused by dysentery germs, but have developed bad cases of either acute or chronic dysentery which ran down their health to such an extent that they had to abandon permanently their pioneer activities as in such cases the slightest touch of any bacterial infection can again make a person seriously ill.  It is not worth it.  As the old saw goes, “An ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure”, so watch your water.

It is only right to state, however, that I have found there are times when it is impossible to refuse a drink of water offered.  I vividly recall one occasion when we arrived in Niger in the raging midday heat at the home of a very isolated but devoted Bahá’í and the first thing offered to each of us was a clean bowl of what appeared to be very pure water.  We drank it gratefully, though I had seen the milling herds of cattle and people clustered about the village well from which it came.  On a similar occasion my host brought me a glass of swamp water to drink which was so grey as to be almost

opaque; I drank it.  I did not know, in those days, that swamp water is not all that dangerous to drink, so I worried quite a bit over the possible consequences.  However, I prayed God to protect me, and He did.  I believe one must act intelligently, take precautions, try to always be on the safe side and then, if the moment comes when one cannot do this—pray and not worry.

Shoghi Effendi used to drink plain water as if it were an elixir, saying it was the best drink in the world.  Not only is water the best drink in the world, it is one of the most precious things on the planet, as one gradually comes to understand through travelling in barren and arid lands.

Imported bottled waters are always expensive, wherever they are found, and drinks like the prepared colas and lemonades—“pop”, in other words—even if they are put up (as many are) by reliable big factories, are not as good for ones health as plain water because of the sweetening, sugar or artificial sweetening, or, what is even more dangerous, analyne dyes as colouring, which are added.  However, it is often not only impossible to avoid taking them when travelling but safer to do so where the ordinary water is in question; one should at least make sure, however, they came from a proper national factory and are not locally made.

There is one other kind of water which is delicious, very good for the health and absolutely safe, and that is coconut water, the juice inside coconuts; before they are ripe there is more water than when all the flesh has grown and the water space has thus become much smaller.  It is remarkable how little they are used by native people or by pioneers even when the trees are full of them!  Oranges and grapefruit, if obtainable, are also a safe drink but one must be sure the juice is obtained after washing and drying the fruit first.

Some common illnesses and hazards

The diseases and illnesses enumerated in this extremely brief list are mentioned not because there is much probability of the pioneer’s getting one of them but rather to set his mind at rest as to what they are, what causes them and how they may be avoided.

Ordinary sore throats and colds:  These are caused by germs in the mucus of the lungs, nose and mouth and transmitted by fine droplets expelled through sneezing and coughing as well as by direct contact; the degree to which people are careless and uninformed on this subject is unbelievable; any of the excretions from the head of someone suffering from a cold or flu are highly contagious so that to kiss or embrace that person, touch his sticky, unwashed hands or inhale his breath exposes one to catching it.  One good sneeze or cough is enough to fill the air of a room with the germs of the sneezer for hours—and yet how generously people share their sneezes with others, raining germs happily right and left!  Cold compounds are available; these commercial remedies put out under different trade names, which, especially if taken when a person feels he is coming down with a cold or has caught it from others, can suppress the infection or curtail its duration; a reliable pharmacist can probably recommend one.  Some people, however, do not tolerate these remedies and may prefer to try heavy doses of Vitamin C instead—3,000 to 5,000 milligrams a day or more—taken at the beginning and continued while the cold lasts; there are differing opinions about whether this is efficacious or not.  Another recommendation is that at the beginning, when a person feels he is coming down with a cold, or senses a change in his condition, a glass of water with a half a teaspoon of salt and a half a teaspoon

of bicarbonate of soda will be helpful.  Undoubtedly the very best way of avoiding colds is to keep away from contact with infected people and not to get too tired and run-down.

Insect bites:  There is no doubt in my own mind that a heavy dose of Vitamin C—1,000 to 2,000 milligrams—taken before one goes to a place where one will be bitten by mosquitoes, not only lessens the itching but helps prevent the bites from getting angry and infected.  It should be noted that Vitamin C has no known effect in preventing malaria; a non-harmful insect repellent, however, usually protects one from being bitten and is a useful thing for every traveller to have at hand.

Malaria:  A miserable disease which can produce serious and recurrent illness; it is caused by a microscopic parasite which completes part of its life cycle in the body of a particular variety of mosquito, the Anopheles, which, when infected, passes on to a human being the malaria germs through its bite; these germs rapidly multiply in the red blood cells and cause them to rupture; icy chills and high fever are usually—but not invariably—part of the symptoms of malaria, which can be readily diagnosed through a microscopic examination of the patient’s blood.  Although the Anopheles is distinguished by its hind legs sweeping upward when it lights, most people are not going to waste time trying to find out what kind of mosquito is biting them!  Malaria is very widespread throughout the world; in places where it exists the traveller should inquire before he goes there from a medical source—either a doctor or health department—what type of anti-malaria medicine he should take and start using it a week or so before he goes to that country; as long as he is in a malarial zone he should never neglect to take his pills; some kinds are taken once or twice a week, some daily;

he should seek the advice of a good doctor in the country as to which type of pill he recommends as people’s reactions differ and the pills available differ from country to country; on leaving the malaria area he should continue taking the pills from two to four weeks so that in case he has malaria germs in his blood they will not develop.  Under no circumstances should any members—young or old—of families living in malarial zones neglect this precaution as malaria is a disease that can be usually suppressed through taking the necessary pills and to catch it through ignoring this fact is sheer folly.

Typhoid fever:  A highly contagious disease caused by microscopic bacteria which affects mainly the intestinal tract and which is transmitted through the contaminated bowel movements or feces of the patient reaching others.  Although it is the feces that contains the germs, the carriers are innumerable, such as flies, a person’s fingers, foods, fluids, any soft cloth or paper to which the excretion of the bowels could adhere, like underwear, diapers, toilet paper, etc.  Flies are one of the most dangerous carriers of disease in the world because they light on filth and then on food, which it is almost impossible to keep them off.  Inoculation against typhoid fever is available now and effective.

Cholera:  A hitherto much-dreaded plague but now no longer so fatal if the patient is immediately given intravenous fluids to offset the acute dehydration the violent diarrhea of this disease brings about and which is mainly the cause of death.  Cholera, like typhoid, is transmitted through the feces and the carriers are the same.  Although vaccination against it is not totally effective, the disease is being brought under control.

Dysentery:  There are many kinds of intestinal upsets loosely classified under this name, most of which are produced by two large families of bacteria called Salmonella and Shigella which can cause mild to violent intestinal upsets with flatulence, cramps, diarrhea and sometimes fever.

Amoebic dysentery:  A serious ailment which may be acute or chronic and hard to get rid of; what are considered recurring attacks may be due to re-infection; in some tropical countries the population is highly infected with it, but treatment for it is now effective.

Hepatitis or epidemic hepatitis (also called epidemic jaundice):  This disease is increasing in many places; it affects the liver, produces anaemia, causes great debility, often takes a long time to recover from and can do permanent damage to the spleen, not to mention weakening the liver itself and making it susceptible to further attacks.  This type of hepatitis is transmitted through both water and milk, as well as sewage contamination, making it all the more essential for pioneers to be sure of what they are drinking and eating and to make every effort to ensure that their food and kitchen are clean.

Serum hepatitis:  Far less common than epidemic hepatitis, serum hepatitis is sometimes transmitted from an infected to a healthy person through injections from hypodermic needles which have not been properly sterilized.

For all forms of intestinal diseases such as those mentioned above there are certain rules of hygiene which, if followed, can protect one from being infected:

1.	Wash ones hands with soap or detergent before handling food in the kitchen and before eating.

2.	Insist that the cook or servant do likewise; in Africa and Asia people have the habit of washing their hands both before and after eating; to get the cook to keep his hands clean while handling food is more difficult.

3.	Do everything possible to keep flies out of the kitchen and off ones food, as they are, next to the cook (who may be yourself or your wife!), the worst food contaminators and carriers of infection.

4.	The general and safest rule, in most places in Africa, Asia, Central and South America and the Pacific—but not necessarily all—is never to eat a raw fruit or vegetable that one cannot peel; this means strawberries and other berries, figs, lettuce, parsley and so on.  If one wishes to eat raw foods (and the craving for them often becomes insufferable, I find), then my own precaution which I realize is by no means 100 per cent sure—is to wash well with soap or detergent the cucumber, tomato, orange, melon, papaw or whatever it may be, rinse it well, dry it thoroughly and then peel off the skin or juice it or cut it up, as the case may be.  In this way one runs less risk of carrying germs into the fruit or vegetable through cutting the skin.

5.	Remember boiled water and milk, well-cooked foods, meat cooked through and not half raw, have had micro-organisms in almost all cases killed and are safe to eat.

6.	In many countries, particularly in Asia, human excrement is often used as a fertilizer and because of this raw fruits and vegetables are very likely to carry dysentery and other germs, as well as worms, which infect and infest the digestive tract.

7.	Food poisoning is of different kinds caused by different families of bacteria but certain general precautions are worth taking:  cakes and pies and desserts that have a custard filling are excellent breeding grounds for bacteria so it is better to avoid these if they have been exposed to handling and to flies, particularly in hot weather; I myself always avoid minced meats and chopped up foods in hot weather because I think they are more easily contaminated; of course if one prepares it in ones own home that is different.

8.	Raw shell fish are a dangerous thing to eat unless one is more or less sure of their source or that the restaurant one is eating in is a well-known one and consequently has its reputation to think of.  In these days sewage often contaminates the shell fish in bays or other places where they are caught and therefore they can be carriers of typhoid, cholera, dysentery and hepatitis.

Typhus (Epidemic Typhus):  A fever disease transmitted by lice and found mainly in Europe, Asia and North Africa; it is not a truly tropical disease.  There is another kind of typhus (New World Typhus) which is caused by rat fleas and also red mites and is endemic in Europe, Asia, Africa and Latin America, as well as in certain places in the United States.  A third type of typhus (called sometimes Mite Typhus and Japanese River Fever) is widespread throughout the Asiatic-

Pacific region.  Without being bitten by the carrier one cannot contract the disease.

Malta or Undulant Fever:  Though not very common, undulant fever can be easily avoided if one takes precautions with milk—whether cows’ or goats’—as this is the carrier of the disease; wherever one is not absolutely sure that fresh milk comes from a safe source and has been pasteurized, one should boil it before using; goats’ milk cheeses, which are often made with unpasteurized milk, can carry undulant fever germs.

African Sleeping Sickness:  An illness confined to certain tropical areas in Africa where its carrier, the tsetse fly, exists; I mention it here because it is often a fatal disease and the traveller in places where the fly occurs should be on his guard as much as possible.  The fly is a large sort of squarish-looking horse-fly and like all its species very persistent; however, just because one has been bitten by a tsetse fly does not mean automatically one is going to get sleeping sickness; the fly may not have the germs in him or one might not contract it.

Elephantiasis (Filariasis):  Transmitted by mosquitoes who carry the larvae of these minute parasitical worms through their bites from an infected to a healthy person, it is found in nearly all the tropics of the world and also occurs, to a lesser extent, in the West Indies and Central and South America.  It is a disease rare among Europeans, even after they have lived years in infested areas; it takes repeated infections to produce its worst form.

Filariasis):  A general term for all diseases (including Elephantiasis) caused by these minute parasitical worms and the

much more prevalent eye worms, easily contracted by people living in tropical Central and West Africa and transmitted by a fly elongated in shape, reddish brown in colour, that stands on high legs and is over a imetre (half an inch) long; for anyone familiar with the Mediterranean fruit fly it looks very much like it.  It is such a large, conspicuous, rather slow-moving fly that if one is on the alert one should be able to spot it and avoid being bitten, or at least make an effort to be exposed to it as little as possible.  Eye worms are also transmitted by gnats.  Antidotes and treatment are available.

Trachoma:  A widespread, horrible and highly destructive eye infection found, along with other forms of chronic infectious conjunctivitis, among poor people in many parts of the world; these infections are propagated by flies, sand flies and midges from eye to eye.  One sees a great many children, as well as adults, with sore, inflamed eyes; they squint, and many of the older people have lost the sight of an eye or both eyes entirely; the eyes look blurred by a whitish, sick-looking film caused by scarred corneas.  The small flies feed on the watery excretion of these sick eyes and carry the infection to healthy eyes.  I well remember when I was a girl in Egypt—where the disease was then very prevalent even in the cities—how these horrible flies would aim like a bullet for the inner corner of my eye and before I could protect myself one would try to settle there and drink.  In the Kalahari Desert, seeing a Bushman mother nursing a baby whose eyelids were covered with flies, I had a hard time shooing them off with my hand, practically having to pick them off, before I could place a thin veil I had been wearing on my hair over the baby’s face.  I tried, through an interpreter, to impress on her that the baby would be blinded if she did not wash its eyes, keep them clean, and keep the flies off, but I
