doubt if it had any effect as the mother had no grasp of why I put the veil there and probably removed it right away, finding it pretty for herself and a nuisance for the child!  In order to help I would have had to live nearby and myself wash and treat the baby’s eyes every day and teach the mother to do the same.  This question of trachoma is one way pioneers can often be of help to villagers through explaining how flies form the habit of collecting at the eyes, and that by fanning ones face and keeping them off—and above all protecting the eyes of babies—the eyes will have a chance to recover; though there will still be plenty of flies about, they will not have this habit of automatically lighting on the eyes and thus carrying disease from one person to another.  Antibiotic ointments and also sulphonamide salves can cure such diseases.  As such eye ailments are highly infectious one must be very careful that ones own eyes do not get infected.

Jigger or Chigoe:  A species of sand flea, the female of which burrows under the skin, usually on the feet, to lay her egg from which very rapidly a larvae develops and creates a sealed pocket, with fluid in it, which almost invariably becomes infected around the minute creature in the middle and which itches very much.  In order to heal the infection this parasite must be carefully removed in its entirety.  It is a common complaint in Africa, South America and other places; a house servant, or anybody’s mother or grandmother, will often be more expert at removing them (but not as hygenic!), and cause less pain, than someone at the hospital.  One can also do it ones self by soaking the foot in hot water for a few minutes, then digging the flea out with a sterilized needle, and putting antiseptic in the wound and bandaging it.  The same treatment can be given to villagers, who are a hardy lot and endure pain much more stoically than city people.  The

hole heals once the parasite is removed.  These jiggers can be so terrible—if not removed—that part of the flesh of the foot or a whole toe may be literally eaten away.  One can pick these up from walking barefooted, or wearing open sandals, on earth, sand or lawns in towns or villages.

Chigger:  Not to be confused with chigoe, is a red mite which is often found in grass and whose bite irritates the skin; some kinds even work their way under the skin where they cause intense itching, redness and irritation for some days.  There are a great many varieties in different parts of the world, some of which carry serious diseases such as scrub typhus.

Blister beetles:  These are insects which produce a secretion that raises blisters on the skin; in many countries where these occur clothes hung out to dry may have these insects light on them, leave their secretion, and when the garment is worn, blisters are produced; this can also happen with baby diapers.  It is well to know such things because strangers to a country, or a foreign doctor, might not be aware of this fact and could be puzzled and worried by blisters unaccountably appearing, not knowing either the cause or that they are not dangerous.

Bilharzia Disease or Schistosomiasis:  Caused by minute worms which live in the veins of the pelvis and produce this serious disease; the eggs of the worms are released through both the urine and feces of an infected person and when this occurs in fresh water, larvae develop which lodge in snails; the life cycle of the worms then undergoes a further metamorphosis inside the snails which release the parasites in the water and in this form they can enter the body of anyone bathing in the snail-infected water.  Because of this peculiar

life cycle people travelling from one place to another have not only spread it widely throughout Africa but it is now spreading in Latin America.  The traveller in Africa, because of its prevalence, and because it is still difficult to cure, should never wash or bathe in ponds, lakes or streams no matter how hot he may feel and how tempting the water may be.  In deep off-shore, or fast-running water it is safe to bathe but in all still water, even if one cannot see snails, they may nevertheless be there, some varieties being very small, and the danger of infection is too great to run the risk; just because the natives are bathing one must not assume the water is safe; on the contrary, both they and the water are more than likely infected.  Unless one is informed by a highly reliable source—a doctor or health department—that the local water is not infected with bilharzia, whether in Latin America or Africa, the rule must be to keep out of it.  Remedies now exist for this disease but every precaution should be taken not to catch it.

Tetanus:  A bacterial infection which is carried in the manure of horses and some other animals; commonly known as lock-jaw, this is a dangerous infection which enters the body through wounds coming in contact with the soil which may be contaminated.

Tuberculosis:  Rife in the tropics but good specific treatment now available.

The following brief summary of immunizations is given for the information of anyone wishing to obtain them:

Typhoid and Paratyphoid:  two separate inoculations a week apart, efficacious for up to three years.

Yellow Fever:  one inoculation supposed to provide permanent immunity.

Smallpox:  vaccination required once every five years.

Poliomyelitis:  one immunization now claimed to be sufficient.

Diphtheria, Tetanus and Pertussis (Whooping Cough):  mainly for protection of children, given in a three-in-one inoculation  should be renewed after a few years.

Mumps, Measles and Rubella (German Measles):  mainly for protection of children, given in a three-in-one inoculation said to be sufficient for a lifetime.

Tetanus (Lock Jaw):  one inoculation required every three to five years.

Food

Pioneers and travelling teachers all too often, feeling the necessity to economize, or being hurried and taking the easiest way out (as we all do when under pressure!), do not pay proper attention to the principles of sound nutrition and live on sandwiches, pastries, cookies and other sweets month after month and through this very unbalanced diet often feel exhausted and below par, weaken their digestion seriously, undermine their health and finally become ill.  Young people are particularly prone to this form of neglect, which is not only unnecessary but stupid.  To acquire a simple knowledge of various foods and their nature and make an effort to apply this to ones self is not difficult and is the purpose of this section.

‘Abdu’l-Bahá said that treatment of disease and ailments will in the future be through foods and waters and that eventually mankind would become vegetarian; Baha’u’llah advocated a simple diet and not to mix many foods at one meal.  What guidance do these things hold for us, and particularly those living in foreign countries where the native food is very different from their own?  Surely the first thing is to not be opinionated regarding what is a proper diet for another man!  Millions of people all over the world either rarely or never eat meat; it is not the business of a Bahá’í from another country to try to change them or to prattle about the necessity of a heavy animal protein diet; either religious convictions, or circumstances over which they have no control, may make it impossible for them to live differently.

Are you a Hindu-background pioneer, or of a vegetarian family?  Then do not feel obliged to eat meat because you are in Scotland or Australia or some other country of heavy meat-eaters.  Are you an Australian or from Scotland living in Africa or India?  Then do not force yourself to become a vegetarian because you may not keep in good health without a certain amount of animal protein.  I remember a Jain sadhu in India—Jains are a particularly fanatical sect who eat no meat and in some cases go to the extreme of covering their mouths and noses by a veil so that they will not even inhale and kill an insect.  This Jain had just become a Bahá’í and looking at me with tears in his eyes, he said he really could not eat meat.  I said whoever told him he should?  It had nothing to do with being a Bahá’í; he could live and die a vegetarian.  But, I added, he must accept that another Bahá’í, who came from a background of meat-eaters, had the same privilege of being free in such matters and was allowed to eat meat if he wanted to.

Many people, particularly when they travel, by a psychological process I do not profess to understand, seem to think it a virtue to pay no attention to what they eat.  Take, for instance, people going on a trip, whether in their own car or some local bus or other conveyance:  instead of taking some food with them or making sure they can get a decent meal on the way, they usually make no provision at all for their journey; to carry some bread, a few boiled eggs, some nuts, a candy bar, an orange or banana or apple, is not difficult, gives a person something safe to eat and prevents exhaustion—which alone brings on many illnesses.  Yet it is remarkable how many people never think of such things or make the slightest preparation for a journey.  Even more forethought should go into being sure they have something safe to drink on the way:  a flask of good water, tea or coffee or a bottled drink from a reliable factory is essential (read reference to this subject in the “Health and Pioneering” section).

In my long African safari we always made a point of eating breakfast before starting out and preparing something to take along for our lunch; usually this was sandwiches of corned beef, or tuna fish, chopped eggs and mayonnaise, or cheese, or even ham purchased from a reliable shop; sometimes we even had—as a special luxury—a roasted chicken if we found one already cooked; we always had a thermos of tea or coffee and always carried water, and when available, fruit or nuts to nibble on.  The best policy is to be prepared and not trust to luck to find safe food—or sometimes any food—on the way, also not to believe that at the end of the journey good food will be found.  There is no guarantee that the travellers’ conveyance will not break down and that they may not be delayed for many hours in some place where there is nothing at all to eat or at least nothing safe for them.  By that I mean the only things available may be exposed to

flies or be so peculiar that the uninitiated do not wish to experiment with them while travelling.  To serve God with a spirit of detachment should not be confused with being foolish and forgetful!

Because travellers should use common sense and be cautious does not mean they should be afraid to eat anything local.  On the contrary, local food is fresh, not canned, cheap and often more suited to the climate.  Many pioneers in Africa and other continents of the world eat local food and enjoy it.  A meal in a native restaurant often costs a quarter or even a tenth of the price of the food in a European restaurant.  One must bear in mind that when food is cooked over strong heat, worms and harmful germs are destroyed and if the restaurant is not too dirty—and one avoids fresh salads and things that might not agree with one—native dishes can be both tasty and inexpensive.  Meat from native butchers can be perfectly safe to eat as long as one is sure it is fresh and cooks it properly.  Most villagers all over the world have no refrigeration whatsoever and therefore insist their meat should be fresh killed; they are well aware of the danger of eating fish or meat that is stale, particularly so when the weather is very hot.

There are many ways of economizing and food is one of them but it requires intelligent thought to do so safely.  This is one reason I have gone into so much detail in this section on food.  The other is to introduce certain valuable foods and stress the need to understand what is best for ones health.  The greatest economy, almost everywhere in the world, is to cook at home and not eat in restaurants; the exception to this can be in Asia where catering (sending to your home from restaurants already prepared food) is a local custom and can be very good and cheap.  Buy inexpensive cuts of meat rather than expensive ones like steak, the vegetable that is in

season and the cheapest, local fruit, rather than imported fruit and the one in season.  Do not economize on quality of food:  by this I mean canned peas may be cheaper than fresh spinach but the fresh vegetable is better for the health; when shopping buy the freshest vegetables as they rapidly lose their vitamins through being stale and wilted though the wilted ones may be cheaper.  For instance, small new carrots and potatoes may cost more, but larger, older ones less; if both, however, are equally fresh, economize by buying the older ones which are usually cheaper although the food value is the same; learn to eat tropical fruits which are delicious and rich in vitamins.

Cooking can be fun and a relaxing occupation.  It is also creative and the good housewife from one country can experiment with local produce and discover new jams and pickles can be put up by old methods used at home; she can also try the often strange vegetables, grains and fruits found in the new country and instead of shunning them welcome them to her table, either cooking them as she did a similar vegetable or learning how they are cooked by the natives.  For instance in African and Asian markets there are many greens—including sometimes leaves of trees and vines—unknown to most of us but which can provide the food values needed for health; learn to use them.  Motoring across Africa we tried to always eat fresh food; if a hard green squash or some okra or tomatoes or carrots or spinach was all we could find, we cooked and ate that; whatever else one eats, one should make a point of trying to get a fresh vegetable.  Green beans are very good for the health so this was the only canned vegetable I carried; canned apple sauce was the best fruit value; when we had nothing else we could fall back on these; the beans, drained and served with French dressing, made a tasty salad.

Here are a few fundamental cooking rules to preserve food values, save time and promote better health:  Never soak vegetables and fruits in water, even though it appear to freshen them, as it leaches out the vitamins; wash and rinse them and cook at once or dry them well and store them.  The fresher things are, the more vitamins they contain; this is true of all fruits and vegetables with the exception of things like bananas, melons, citrus, squash, potatoes, beets, onions, etc., which can be kept much longer.  The best cooking methods to preserve the vitamin value of food are to steam—where the food is placed over, not in, boiling water—or quick-fry; the Chinese people are masters of this system of cooking, which is one reason why their food is so healthful and delicious; things boiled in water or stewed a long time lose their vitamins which is why the quick method of cooking is preferable and to eat raw vegetables and fruit most valuable for ones health.

Meat and fish should be cooked right through, with no pink left; grains and other starches should also be thoroughly cooked as otherwise they are indigestible.

People from Europe and North America, who were rarely exposed to infectious intestinal disorders at home, should strictly avoid eating raw berries, grapes or lettuce because no matter what anyone says they cannot be safely disinfected; only those fruits and vegetables that can be washed, dried, and then peeled are safe.  A nice fresh salad can be made, however, by steaming cabbage until nearly cooked through and serving with a tasty dressing.

Meat:  Nowadays in large towns one can usually find frozen imported meat, but this is prohibitively expensive.  There is no reason why native meat should not be bought in the local markets; cook it thoroughly, never eat it rare or half done.  It

is liable to be tough but this can be overcome by:  rubbing it all over (marinating it) with onion juice or yogurt or the white juice in the skin of papaw fruit and leaving some hours or overnight; putting it in cold salted water and cooking it very slowly until tender; or mincing it to use as hamburger or meat loaf or meat balls, but in this case be sure it is freshly ground as stale pieces mixed into it can cause food poisoning.  (Because of this I personally avoid all minced meat dishes in restaurants in hot climates.)  Beef and goat (a perfectly good meat to eat) should be bright red in colour with no darkening or tainted spots; mutton and lamb are less red and veal and pork are pale meats; fat should be healthy looking and light in colour except in full-grown beef when it can be a deep yellow but perfectly good.  Kidneys, tongue, liver and heart are all excellent food value; brains are often very inexpensive and delicious but must be absolutely fresh as they spoil fast; all meat adhering to bones makes delicious stews and soups.

Chicken:  These are often readily available.  Usually a chicken is purchased in the market alive and ones servant or alternatively ones self—or perhaps a helpful neighbour, if one cannot bring ones self to do it—will kill it and dress it.  The heart, liver and gizzard of poultry are all edible and good.  One can ask a sensible looking person doing her own marketing to help select a good chicken if one does not know how to ones self.

Eggs:  Although eggs are relatively plentiful in most large cities, in villages they are scarce and one has no guarantee they are fresh because the villagers collect the eggs the hens—running around free—lay anywhere the spirit moves them.  One of the ways to test an egg to see if it is fresh is to

put it in a pan of water and if it goes down to the bottom and stays there, it is probably fresh.  Do not be shy, just call for a bowl of water and do it.  I have seen many an African do so in his own market!  Eggs are a valuable and relatively inexpensive food and many dishes—such as fried or scrambled eggs and omelets—can be made with them very quickly, which makes them useful for entertaining unexpected guests.  It is well to remember, however, that contrary to popular belief they are very low in protein compared to meat and fish.

Snails:  In many native markets land snails are sold alive; I have never eaten these, or for that matter ever been able to bring myself to eat them in Europe where they are considered an expensive delicacy, however, snails are a good source of protein and there is no reason why one should not cook and eat them.

Fish:  All over the world fish is found, and, surprisingly, is available throughout Africa, either fresh or dried, including the desert regions and the heart of the jungles.  Fish is an excellent source of protein.  Whenever we travelled near the coast we found sometimes we could buy very tiny fish from the fishermen who were hauling in their nets.  These need very careful washing as the sand gets in the gills and is most unpleasant to chew on; aside from this, they need not be gutted or trimmed at all but are fried in very hot fat until they are crisp like French fried potatoes; by this method one eats the head, tail and all the bones, which provide an excellent source of natural calcium.  Larger fish, however, must be cleaned by slitting their bellies lengthwise and throwing away everything but the roe (eggs) which is very good; they may also need to have the scales removed, which can be

done by scraping them against the grain with the back of a knife, so as not to tear the skin.

Sweets:  One of the main causes of the increase in diabetes, overweight, decayed teeth in children and adults and many other ailments is the tremendous increase in the consumption of white sugar.  Whereas fifty years ago sweets were usually a treat, they are now consumed in one form or another at almost every meal and between meals as well.  Civilization with its new standards, its marketing and advertising techniques and the competition they give rise to, has flooded our urban lives—and would like to flood every village on earth—with more and more sweets in various forms.  To this is added the curse of “pop”, or sweetened bottled drinks of all types now found literally everywhere in the world.  Doctors and nutritionists are well aware of the dangers involved in this modern way of life and have issued many warnings, but people are either ignorant of them or will not change their ways.  Perhaps if they knew there are alternatives they would make a more serious effort to protect their health.

Natural sugar occurs in almost all foods, particularly in fruit and vegetables, but because of heavy consumption of ice cream, candies, sweet bottled drinks, desserts, and too much sugar taken with everything, particularly tea and coffee, people can no longer taste it.  Because of this abuse the liver and pancreas are overburdened and debility, ill health and illness, including diabetes, can result.  Sugar gives quick energy and a vicious circle of more sugar to boost ones energy, greater fatigue produced by this abuse and then still higher consumption of sugar is established.  The way to get back to normal is to steadily cut down on white sugar:  two instead of three spoonfuls of sugar in tea or coffee, then one and if possible none; less sugar in recipes; avoid sweets

and sweetened drinks and ice cream—particularly in hot weather as it may carry typhoid; eat fruit, a handful of nuts, a piece of cheese, bread and honey, or a snack of meat as a pick-me-up because these also produce quick energy without damaging the health.  Starch products, such as bread, rice, potatoes, macaronis, porridges and other grains, all contain considerable sugar as carbohydrates and will to some extent provide the lift that taking sweets does without being as damaging to the health.  Dried raisins and other fruit can easily take the place of candy; try to buy them with an indication on the package that they are unsulphured as in that way they are better for ones health.

This does not mean one should become a food fanatic or feel guilty if one eats a piece of candy!  It does mean—at least to me—that a knowledgeable approach to the subject of nutrition and keeping ones self in as good health as possible is surely intelligent and that to ignore the subject and impair ones health—thus jeopardizing ones service to the Faith in the all-important field of pioneering—is foolish and unnecessary.  There are times when one needs the quick energy that a chocolate bar or other sweet can give, especially when travelling under difficult circumstances.  During my African safari we found that a cup of very sweet, strong, hot tea provided the best and most lasting stimulus when we were exhausted and we took time off to stop and either make it by the wayside or drink what we had already prepared in our thermos bottle.  It is not what one does under exceptional circumstances or occasionally that is so bad for ones health but the forming of an essentially unsound pattern of daily life, and the consumption of too many sugars falls in this category.

Honey:  Though it may be expensive and hard to find, honey is the best and most natural of all sugars.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá both

praised it and prescribed it.  For thousands of years it has been sought out and cherished by men as a unique and precious substance; even the little Bushmen of the Kalahari Desert adore it and have given it a delightful name—“moon’s water”.  The properties and uses of honey are too many to enumerate in detail but here are a few of them:  honey given to small children at night is supposed to help prevent bedwetting; if taken plain or dissolved in milk or water before going to bed it soothes the nerves and can produce sleep; it makes an excellent cough syrup mixed with a little water and sipped; it can replace sugar to sweeten tea, be used in cooking instead of sugar, and half honey and half fruit vinegar (apple cider, grape or wine vinegar) mixed together can be kept in a bottle and one or two tablespoonfuls be added to a glass of water as a refreshing beverage which many claim is excellent for the health in general.  One of the most delicious desserts is yogurt mixed with a spoonful of honey and certainly one of the most healthful for children and adults.  Honey is expensive compared to other sweetenings and may seem a luxury but what one spends on health one often saves on doctors’ bills.  Many honeys are adulterated by being mixed with sugar syrup and one should try to purchase pure honey—usually marked on the jar as such.

Brown sugar:  In Africa, India and most parts of the world where there are sugar factories or where the native people themselves refine sugar from sugar cane, brown sugar sometimes called natural sugar, in other words a dark sugar that is unrefined—can be bought.  It is very much better for the health than white sugar, though not as sweet, and because it has not been refined more of the valuable minerals which are in the cane itself to begin with have been retained.  I remember when I was a child the pick-me-up, or between

meals sweet that I was allowed to beg for in the kitchen was a thick slice of buttered brown bread sprinkled liberally with brown sugar.  It still tastes better to me than cake!  In some countries in the native markets a cone or hunks of very unrefined brown sugar can be bought.  If this is used in cooking it can do no harm because of course the boiling kills any germs that might be in it and the impurities may be skimmed off.  It may also be boiled down, purified, and stored as a sweet syrup for drinks, cooking, on desserts and so on.

Molasses:  A syrup that is a by-product of sugar cane refining, molasses can be sometimes bought from the factory, if one exists in the country, or found in tins for sale in the stores of countries that import from England or the United States.  The least refined, called blackstrap molasses, is very dark and thick and has a strong flavour which to some palates makes it unsuitable for eating raw on bread or desserts; it is the best for ones health, however, as it contains the most vitamins and very valuable minerals; it is excellent in cooking, especially with dried bean dishes and in muffins; the more fluid and lighter-coloured molasses is also excellent for the health and has been more refined so it tastes very good on bread in place of a jam, or mixed with yogurt, which is a delicious way of having it; it is also very useful as a sweetening and to add flavour to cooked breads and other food; both kinds act as a natural laxative which in itself adds a very welcome element to the diet, particularly for children.  Because of these qualities molasses is well worth the trouble and expense of procuring it, if it is at all possible to do so.

Sugar cane:  In almost every continent of the globe one will see in the markets, or in front of a country shop, or in the hand of somebody walking along by the roadside, long stalks

about two metres high of something which appears to be a variety of reed or bamboo.  It belongs to the family of grasses and is the source of most of our sugar.  The natives of Asia, Africa and many places in Central and South America chew it for its juice which is sweet and refreshing.  As it takes a considerable amount of chewing, it is also good for the teeth.  The pulp is spit out.  The hard outer skin of the cane is peeled down and sections of the pith, about an inch long, cut up and chewed.  If one has weak teeth avoid sugar cane!  But for children to munch on and strengthen their teeth it is excellent; also for a thirsty traveller, worth buying and chewing on as he goes along.  Its juice makes a very refreshing beverage.  Put through a juicer, as they do it in India, and flavoured with a little fresh ginger, it is delicious.

Fruits and nuts:  Nature, in every climate, has given men valuable sources of natural sugar and vitamins in fruits, and minerals, protein and oils in nuts; except for what is in season and available locally fruits are not a cheap food; nuts—with the exception of peanuts and coconuts where they are indigenous—are usually a relatively expensive food.  The pioneer should look around and see what is on the market and purchase native rather than imported fruits and buy nuts that are the best price, as well as quality, that can be found.  By this I mean that stale and wormy nuts, even if half the price, are not worth buying instead of fresh ones, but there is no necessity to pay extra for larger nuts, especially if good, and cheaper, smaller ones are available.  Wise shopping means better value for ones health and for ones money.  Fruits (as well as vegetables) should be judged by weight, fullness, colour, smell and freedom from spots as well as insects.  If things are wrinkled, dull looking, flabby or light when they should be heavy, and if they should smell like

themselves (an apple or a melon odour) and do not, they are not really good.  Of course if they are all one can get, that is a different matter.

The subject of food values is a fascinating one but involves a study at least of charts, and there is no place for them here.  But a slight idea of the factors involved is that one egg, for instance, has 6 grams of protein and about 600 i.u. of Vitamin A and only 80 calories (these are the fuel units and, roughly speaking, the part that makes one fat).  A half a cup of bran breakfast cereal, however, also has 6 grams of protein, no Vitamin A, but almost 250 calories.  This means a person who gains weight easily should eat the egg not the bran, while the thin person should be glad of the same amount of protein in the bran plus an extra 170 calories to fatten him up and acquire his valuable Vitamin A through some other food, like a vegetable.  Almonds are about 20 per cent protein and over 50 per cent fat; peanuts 27 per cent protein and almost 50 per cent fat; they also contain valuable minerals—but they are very high in calories.  I have mentioned only a few fruits and nuts here for the benefit of people unfamiliar with what is readily available in the tropics and how they can be used.

Avocados:  A very nourishing fruit now grown, in many varieties, all over the world; the colour can be a bright or a deep green or a purplish brown, with a shiny skin, sometimes smooth and sometimes rough; avocados can be as small as eggs or as large as grapefruit.  There is a stone in the centre which is useless except to grow another avocado tree with—in case you wish to do so, you put the pointed end up and stick the bottom just touching a little bit of water in a glass or a can; if you wait long enough it will eventually sprout and develop into a beautiful sturdy little tree which,

when it is  high, can be planted in earth in a pot as an agreeable house plant or transferred to the garden.  The rather oily flesh surrounding this stone is delicious and many different things can be done with it:  cut in half it can be eaten with a spoon as a fruit; peeled and sliced it can be added to salads or mixed with salad dressing; very ripe avocados mashed with a fork to a cream and honey added, or put in the blender and whipped up, make a delicious dessert and if one wishes, mashed bananas may also be added.  This is an excellent way of adding a healthful, fattening and strengthening food to children’s diets.

Mangoes:  Originally Asian they have migrated to Africa and many other countries and are one of the most delicious fruits in the world, rich in Vitamin A.  There are dozens of varieties, some much better than others, but I found while travelling in Africa that during the season the small ones, which were found in great abundance in town and village markets, not only were very inexpensive but had the best flavour, although they were very stringy.  To overcome this, first wash the fruit very carefully with soap, rinse and dry so no germs will get inside when cutting them, but before cutting, without breaking the skin, massage them to reduce the flesh inside to a pulp; this pulp can then be easily put through a coarse colander and produces a most delicious thick liquid which can be used as a drink, frozen as a sherbet in the freezer compartment of ones refrigerator, served as a custard over other fruits or puddings or used as a dressing on fruit salad.  Needless to say the finest mangoes, which are fleshy and have no strings, are too good to make a pulp of and should be peeled and served in slices or cut in half, the pit taken out, and eaten in their skin with a spoon.  Another way of preparing mangoes, which is done in India, is to get them while they are

still green, in other words not bullet-like in hardness but at the same time not fully ripe and sweet, peel and cut them up and cook them with some onions which have been already browned in oil, adding a little salt and some brown sugar to relieve the sourness of the fruit and some grated fresh ginger or powdered dry ginger; this dish, with or without meat cooked with it, is inexpensive and very tasty.

Bananas:  A positive boon to humanity, this universal, excellent and nourishing fruit has many varieties, from little finger bananas which melt in ones mouth, to perfumed red bananas equally delicious.  Bananas with custard sauce are a wholesome dessert; in the West Indies they slice them in half lengthwise and put them in the oven in a deep dish in layers sprinkled with fat and brown sugar and bake until cooked into a succulent whole; they may be fried and help out as an extra dish with meat or chicken; made into fritters; whipped up as a fancy dessert; used in fruit salad or just eaten plain.  As they shrink a lot when cooked one must calculate much more per portion than when eaten raw.  Bananas are easily digested and hence good for babies and sick people.  One of the greatest advantages of the banana is the ease with which its skin peels off, thus protecting it from dirt and germs on the outside getting inside the fruit; because of this one can afford to eat them from stands in markets while travelling and to give them to children.  Bananas are always picked green and ripened off the tree so one can buy them unripe and keep until ripe.

Papaws:  Also called in many countries papayas, these are another wonderful fruit.  The papaw tree, about 15 feet high, grows with the greatest of ease in most tropical climates; it is not a palm but resembles one with a bunch of

umbrella-like leaves spreading out in a cluster from the top of its trunk and melon-like fruit—in all stages of growth—hanging down thickly under its leaves.  Because of this useful peculiarity papaws stay in season almost the entire year round.  It is the white juice in the skin of this fruit that is used as a meat tenderizer and is prepared for export in many countries.  Papaw, before it is ripe is a green colour and can be cooked like a squash and eaten as a vegetable; when it is ripe it turns yellow or orange and is eaten as a melon:  cut in half lengthwise, the seeds taken out and served with a little lime or lemon juice squeezed onto it, it is a delicate, delicious and refreshing fruit, particularly served at breakfast.  Like bananas it is usually ripened off the tree; one can buy a green papaw, take it home and wrap it up in a piece of paper and it will ripen nicely by itself.  It has a lot of Vitamin A, acts as an excellent natural laxative and is a Godsend to people living in countries where there are many fruits they are afraid to eat because they grow exposed to dust, and hence germs, whereas the papaw, being a melon-like fruit, if carefully washed and dried, can be cut open and eaten with little danger to ones health.  It is a great pity more native people do not understand the merit of this fruit as one often sees it fallen off the trees and never eaten.

Pineapples:  Another valuable addition to ones diet in the tropics is pineapple, which, like papaw, grows very easily:  slice the top off, where the tuft of leaves is sprouting out, and plant it in the ground, with the leaves sticking up in the air; every plant produces only one fruit and is easily propagated by this simple method.  So strong and prolific is the pineapple that many times on jungle roads I saw that a top, thrown away by somebody passing by, had taken root and was vigorously growing.  Pineapple is both refreshing and good for

the health but its juice is tart and with some people not only puckers the mouth but produces sores.  To overcome this, after peeling a pineapple, one can sprinkle it with salt for a few minutes and then rinse it off, which removes some of this caustic quality in the fruit.  One can also boil it a little bit—not cook it—and pour off the water.  Always remember that salt leaches out the juices of vegetables and fruits and their vitamins and consequently should never be allowed to stay on for any length of time.  A large pineapple is sufficient to serve eight or ten people and if one has not used it all up, and the weather is hot and no refrigeration available, by stewing it in a little water, with or without sugar added, one can keep it for at least a day without any danger of its going sour.  To test if a pineapple is ripe pluck out some of the leaves growing from the centre of the top; if they come out easily, it is ripe; in addition it should be brown not green and have the fragrant smell of a pineapple; but even if it is not yet ripe one can still buy it and take it home and wait a few days for it to ripen.  Do not buy any fruit that is damaged or has a soft spot on it as it will rot in an unbelievably short time.

Passion-fruit:  These are found in many countries and a number of varieties, ranging from small, dark brown, almost purplish-coloured fruit the size of a golf ball, with a wrinkled dry skin, to a large and bright orange-coloured one the size of a small grapefruit.  The seeds of this fruit are contained inside in a sweet membrane, the juice of which is absolutely delicious; usually one can swallow the seeds if they are tender without worrying about them but otherwise one can force the pulp through a sieve or colander, using the puree with other fruits or food or diluting it half and half with the rather sour native African orange juice, or with water, as a drink.  It is rich in vitamins.

Guavas:  Some people like to eat these raw when fully ripe but when cooked they make an excellent jelly or compote.  For the latter, peel the fruit, cut in halves, cover with water and boil until the seeds separate from the flesh, then rub through a strainer, return to the cooking pot, add sugar or honey to taste and boil until tender.  Guavas are rich in Vitamin C and also have a strong perfume and flavour.

Lichee:  A large Chinese tree on which grow clusters of round fruit, each about as big as a walnut with a thin reddish-coloured skin covered with prickles like horse chestnut burrs; inside is a large kernel covered by a very tasty, sweet, white flesh, which is the small edible part.

Jack fruit:  A weird fruit  occurs in South East Asia, Africa and other tropical lands and grows on large trees in a very strange way—namely, straight out from the trunk!  Jack fruit looks like a squash, of a yellowy-green colour, covered with huge, rough warts and its skin is so extraordinarily tough that it is almost impossible to cut open.  Inside are elongated seeds, each one enclosed in a silken pod of delicious, perfumed, sweet fruit.  If one oils ones hands first, one can separate the fruit from the seeds easier as the whole process is a sticky one.  People in India and Africa sometimes cook jack fruit when it is green as a vegetable, very much like unripe mangoes; it must, naturally, be skinned and pitted first.

Durian:  Exotic in the extreme this Asian fruit, which grows on large trees, is about the size of a melon with a coarse, prickly, brownish-green skin; it is famous for its odour, which to some foreign noses comes as a perfume and to others as an unbearable stench; one should not take it into the

car or the house as the smell can be overwhelming if one is not used to it.  I found the taste like eating a rare perfume, but a little bit was sufficient.  However, many people become extremely fond of it.

Other fruits:  When travelling one sees many unknown fruits, but the principle is always the same:  if other human beings eat them, so can you; try them, but go easy in the beginning; they are usually rich in vitamins and being fresh and indigenous, cheaper and better for your health than imported fruits.

Dates and figs:  Full of nourishment and natural sugar they are an excellent food, but, due to probable exposure to dysentery germs, a risk for those who have not developed a strong immunity; in theory peeling them should remove the danger but it is much wiser to bring them to the boil in water and pour it off.

Dried fruits:  All types of dried fruits provide, for their weight and bulk, a most concentrated and healthful (and fattening!) source of nourishment.  However, if they have been exposed to flies they can be germ carriers and one risks intestinal infections by eating them without first cooking them.

Coconuts:  Wherever these are found they are usually so common and cheap it is hard to buy one.  That may sound strange but it is literally true.  People have them growing in their back yards, they grow often along the streets of the town, hanging up temptingly above ones reach, they grow in groves and all along the sea shore and unless one pays a boy, or begs somebody to go up and fetch one down, it is

often extremely difficult to purchase a coconut at all.  They are one of the most delicious and useful things that the Creator has provided for the human race.  I once read that there are something like 200 uses to which a coconut palm is put.  In any case, when the coconut is not yet ripe it is full of a most delicious, slightly sweet juice, exceedingly good for the health, which not only quenches ones thirst but because it is sealed into its own nut, is absolutely safe to drink.  Inside, the “cream” forms as the nut matures and this cream can be scraped off and eaten while it is still soft and the nut is half developed; in other words a green nut will have more water and a little cream and an entirely ripe coconut will have very little water and a thick coating of what we usually associate with being coconut—the white flesh.  Maize meal or rice, cooked in coconut water, has a delicate and delightful flavour.  Coconut water also is supposed to be an excellent face lotion and one can use it on ones skin to good advantage.  The meat of ripe coconuts is not only nutritious but when grated can be added to fruit salad, to cakes or to food and in making the sauce for curries; it is full of oil and very heavy; one must remember this, because the flesh is so tempting one eats too much and gets a stomach ache.  In addition to the water and flesh of coconuts being so useful as a drink and for food, the rough outside husk is used as an abrasive to clean pots and polish and scrub floors.

Peanuts:  Also known as “ground-nuts” and called “arachide” in French-speaking Africa, this modest and ubiquitous plant is not a nut at all; it provides a source of excellent and cheap nourishment and is sold all over Africa where one frequently sees people buying a spill of them for a few cents.  Peanuts are sold in their pods, or shelled, but one should make sure they have not been boiled as I found them in this form very

indigestible and not so tasty; strangely enough raw peanuts when freshly picked are delicious and not indigestible.  Generally peanuts are sold roasted which is the best way to eat them.  Some African tribes make a delicious sauce with pounded peanuts; peanut butter is very nutritious (also exceedingly fattening) and can be made at home by shelling, skinning and lightly roasting the nuts, mincing through a meat grinder and adding peanut oil to obtain the right consistency and a little sugar and salt to taste.  Some spoonfuls of peanut butter dissolved in the milk one is going to pour over a dish of cooked macaroni or noodles to be finished in the oven, adds a very interesting taste and extra nourishment.

Kola nuts:  A nut eaten in many parts of West Africa, large and triangular in shape with a pinkish or reddish colour; it has a bitter taste and is not only nourishing but a stimulant like tea.  As one is sometimes offered these nuts as a sign of hospitality and welcome, it is not a bad idea to get used to the taste sufficiently so that one can munch on one as a sign of politeness.  There is a smaller variety of kola nut  tastes much better.  It is interesting to note that African villagers for the most part enjoy bitter, sour and peppery things and do not like sweet things, a preference which is certainly much better for their health.  It is a pity that we do not learn the good habits of other people instead of being so eager to impart our own bad habits to them.

Cashew nuts:  Like the peanut, cashew nuts are widely found in Africa and elsewhere and like all nuts, highly nutritious and very useful to have when travelling as an extra source of food  is concentrated and not bulky to carry.  Wherever there are mountains one will find a wider variety of nuts than

in the tropics; the cashew is a truly tropical nut.  All nuts contain both protein and oil, both of which are needed by the body.

The great staples:  Carbohydrates, mainly in the form of starches found in grains and tubers, are the major source of food for mankind; they are also the cheapest and the most easily digestible form of fuel which the human body converts into energy—the human body being an unbelievably intricate and marvellous chemical laboratory.  The sugars  occur in honey, fruit, vegetables and to a lesser degree other things, are likewise carbohydrates.  How these other elements are synthesized and converted in nature’s laboratory, their value and qualities, are all part of the modern science of nutrition.  If one could see a map of the world divided into starch zones one would see wheat eaters, rice eaters, maize eaters, millet eaters, cassava eaters, potato eaters, plantain eaters and so on.  Starches should always be thoroughly cooked, but fried at high temperatures (like French fried potatoes and chips) they become, like other foods, less digestible.  Ready-to-eat or pre-cooked cereals, which require almost no further cooking time due to the high temperatures used in preparing them, have lost some of their valuable amino acids.  Unpolished rice is far richer in natural minerals than the polished white rice we use all the time, in fact it is the skin that is polished off which contains these minerals and is sold in health food shops as a special addition to ones diet—or bought up by cattle and poultry farmers, who often feed their livestock with more intelligence than we feed ourselves and our children!  If one can get rice or wheat from villagers, who do their own polishing in crude mills or with grindstones, it is much better for ones health and much tastier.

In June 1971 the Reader’s Digest published an article on the findings of a distinguished research doctor who blames the rising incidence of colon cancer in western and westernized nations on the high intake of refined carbohydrates, mostly white flour and white sugar, and goes on to state that Africans eat fewer of these and more roughage and have better bowel movements and cancer of the colon is seldom found.  In other words unpolished grains such as rice, maize, millet and wheat (often available in many wheat-growing countries in the form of cracked wheat) offer the best starch plus a high nutritional value, as well as the roughage which is good for the intestines.  Next to these would come the legumes and the fresh vegetable starches, such as sweet potatoes, yams, bread-fruit, plantain, etc.  The staples which are the least beneficial to our health, but alas the ones consumed most in the modern way of life, are the white flour breads and macaronis and polished rice and the flour made from it.

Over half the world’s population has never eaten bread and never heard of it.  The wheat eaters of Europe, the Antipodes and the Western Hemisphere do not exist where wheat is not the staple food; they are replaced by men having breakfast on rice or rice noodles and an African mother giving her family its porridge of some other grain or a dish of cassava or plantain.  This is a vast subject and only a few staples are mentioned here which may be of use to pioneers in different countries.

Bread-fruit:  Widely found all over the world in the tropics this fruit, resembling in size and colour a grapefruit, grows high up in the branches of a very beautiful tree; blander in taste than even a potato it has not been given its name for nothing because it can be peeled, sliced and toasted and take the place of bread; as it is very dry I personally think it needs

a lot of butter or oil on it to make it palatable.  It can be roasted over a camp fire or in an oven and served with sugar or something sweet or with a savoury sauce or to go with other dishes in place of potatoes or rice; it is a very inexpensive food (or costs nothing if one has access to a tree) and makes excellent imitation potato chips.

Maize:  To Americans this is “corn” but to most of the world corn means grain.  Freshly boiled or roasted (but usually very tough) or the dry kernels ground into a coarse meal (grits) or a finer meal or a flour, maize is an excellent food staple.  The unrefined local cornmeal is, unlike the horrible bleached, pre-cooked rice that one buys in packages in the big cities of many countries—devoid of practically all nourishment and taste—much more nutritious, very much cheaper and very much better for the health.  It can be made into corn bread or muffins or cornmeal mush, or as it is cooked all over southern Africa, a porridge with which is eaten a meat stew or a peppery meat sauce or a vegetable.  In many countries where both maize and coconut are found the native people cook their maize in coconut juice and boiled in this way it is unbelievably delicious; if one adds brown sugar, one can produce an extremely nourishing dessert acceptable to anyone, particularly children.  All the Mexican recipes where maize is used can be made in many other countries to great advantage.

Millet:  In Africa, India and the Middle East millet has been used since prehistoric times and is widely eaten in East Africa and the upper countries of Central and West Africa.  When properly prepared it is a very delicious, highly nutritious food rich in magnesium and potassium, but I found in some villages it was almost inedible because of the stone-

powder from the grinding stones being mixed into it.  There is a red and a grey variety of millet, the tiny grains being about the size of a pinhead.  If ground to a fine flour it can be mixed with wheat flour in the proportion of 1 to 5 to make an interesting bread.

Sorghum:  A grain similar to millet, with larger seeds, and found in the Middle East and some areas of southern Africa.  Sorghum porridge is delicious.

Buckwheat:  A grass-like grain often eaten in Europe and also some parts of Africa; it is high in potassium and phosphorus and can be used as a porridge.  In Nigeria (where it is called “kasha”) they roast the grains before cooking them to bring out the flavour; they also boil them, half and half proportions, with rice.  The native recipes for all kinds of food should always be investigated as they may prove to be not only inexpensive but new in taste and very healthful.

Arrowroot, tapioca and sago:  Arrowroot is sometimes seen for sale in packages as a starch, flour or powder; it is easily digested and can be used in invalid and baby dishes; make a smooth paste by slowly adding warm milk or water and honey as a sweetener.  It can also be used as a substitute for cornstarch and to thicken gravies, soups and fruit compotes.  Tapioca makes an excellent pudding; all three are recommended for babies and weak stomach conditions; they are more digestible than maize, wheat or rice.

Barley:  The whole grain is best in soups and stews but the grits (coarsely ground meal) make an excellent, tasty, quick-cooking cereal for breakfast or served to replace rice, potatoes or macaroni as the main dish.

Cassava:  All over Africa one sees groves of slender short trees with spreading tops; these are the cassava plant, called “manioc” in the French-speaking countries.  The tubers which form part of its root system are the edible portion and when young and roasted in the fire are a little like a baked potato or bread-fruit in flavour but very dry and need some fat or sauce.  There are many varieties, some of which have a poisonous element  must be leached out through involved processes of soaking, drying and grinding (well known to Africans and done efficiently all the time); millions of people live mainly on cassava, prepared in many ways.

Plantain:  Plantains are not a fruit in the strict sense of the word but are invariably cooked and used as a vegetable.  They are a big, green, cooking banana which can be boiled, steamed, cut up and fried as chips or in strips and is quite tasty and very useful although not particularly nutritious.  A mixture of half boiled and mashed plantain and half boiled and mashed potato makes a very good and inexpensive main dish if one has a lot of company; it tastes better than plantain by itself and it costs less than potatoes by themselves, which can be very rare, and hence expensive, in the tropics.

Sweet potatoes:  Wherever these can be found they are usually excellent in taste and a very good starch; if the skins are smooth enough and healthy enough to be eaten, scrub them well with a brush and bake them; in this way the skin itself, which is highly nutritious, may also be eaten and forms a very delicious addition to the meal.  Generally speaking a great deal of the valuable nutrients in food are found under the skin, not only in vegetables and fruits but also, I understand, in the skin of fowls and fish, so whenever possible, and tasty, the skin should be eaten.

Yams:  These are a staple food in many countries.  The African yam is not the same thing at all as the yam that we associate in the southern part of the United States with being a deep orange-coloured sweet potato; it is a heavy root vegetable and has almost no sweet flavour but it can be used as a potato substitute—boiled, fried or mashed—and with a little experimenting can be a useful addition to ones diet.

Flour:  It is extraordinary how many different kinds of flour men have developed:  maize, millet, plantain, cassava, bean, rice, yam—to mention only a few found in Africa alone.  See what the people of the country do with these and copy it or adapt it to your own style of cooking.  The method of making flour is much the same for all the grains or alternatively all the roots one uses; I have gone into a little detail here as a lone pioneer family in a village might find such information useful:  to make a flour from yams, plantain, or cassava, peel and cut them into thin slices, soak in water overnight, drain and dry them thoroughly in the sun; they are then ready to be ground or pounded into a flour; beans or peas must be soaked, skinned, sun dried and similarly made into flour; grains need not be either soaked or sunned (grains have usually been sun dried at harvest time) but all earth and stones must be carefully picked out and the seeds pounded to flour with a pestle and a mortar—in daily use all over Africa—or given to a small local mill to grind up.  The easiest way, of course, is to buy such flours ready-made from the villagers.  Wheat flour is usually imported and is the white, ultra-refined flour used in our very unhealthy civilization; the village flours contain all the natural good of the food and are not over-refined or full of preservatives.

Fats:  The highest fuel (calorie) value per weight of any food is found in fats which are divided into two categories:  the hard fats such as butter, lard, margarine and other solid vegetable fats—all of which in cooking terms come under the heading of shortening—and the liquid fats known as oils.  There are a number of essential vitamins  can be absorbed by the body only if some fat is taken at the same time, Vitamin A being one of them.  Without some fat in the diet one cannot either keep well or lose weight, two tablespoons of vegetable oil a day in ones food being recommended as a minimum requirement in a reducing diet.

Butter, surprisingly enough, is available in many places, even in the smaller cities everywhere throughout Africa.  It is usually imported, fresh or sometimes sold in tins but makes breakfast much more enjoyable.  I no longer cook with butter as it is a saturated fat and very heavy on the liver.  Margarine is also widely available and if one wishes to use it in place of butter, one can do so; I never buy it, however, unless I am forced to because these days one is no longer sure how healthful the ingredients are.  The best thing is to buy the local cooking oils; these vary from place to place and can be made from cotton seed, sunflower seed, maize, soya beans, olives or peanuts and in West Africa from palm nuts, to mention a few; all such natural vegetable oils are excellent for frying, cooking, making salad dressing, etc.  and can certainly do ones health no harm; mineral oil, however, taken internally for any purpose, is dangerous for the health.  The approach of the pioneer to the oils used in native diets is usually a prejudiced one:  “I couldn’t possibly eat that!”  For instance palm oil, used commonly in Nigeria and neighbouring countries, is a deep almost reddish-orange colour and I found it very delicious but some foreigners claim it is too heavy for their digestion.  However, this might very easily be their imagina-

tion because whenever one approaches something that is completely unknown, and one is chary of accepting it, the best excuse for not doing so is to say that it disagrees with you!  The less oils have been refined or homogenized, the better for the health; oils should be clear, with no sediment at the bottom, and rancid fats and oils never used.

Vegetables:  If the great carbohydrate family provides most of our energy and nourishment, and the various animal foods, including milk products, are an important source of our proteins, the fruits, and particularly the vegetables, give us the essential vitamins and minerals which spell good health.  The rule is:  the fresher the vegetable, the less soaked in water and overcooked, the better it is for ones health.  All over the world in native markets one will find strange vegetables for sale; if the local people eat them, so can the pioneer; find out how they prepare them, experiment and remember that any fresh vegetable, no matter how unknown or monotonous as a diet it may be, is better than forever eating tinned foods and also is likely to be very much cheaper.  On my long safari we always went to the local market and bought whatever we could find; this included strange greens we had never seen before and which we found we could cook very much like spinach.  It is remarkable what one can find in a native market if one makes an effort; in the smallest village market there is often one single vegetable available; buy it, whatever it is, as it is valuable for ones health.

I have found that wherever there are people whose ancestors were of Chinese origin—all of North East and a lot of South East Asia—beautiful vegetables in many varieties are found; they not only eat them as the main staple of their diet in conjunction with the ubiquitous bowl of rice, they cook them better than any other people in the world—

so investigate the Chinese cooking method for it is the best and most healthful there is.  In Africa, however, vegetables are often rare so one must take advantage of whatever one can find.  I have mentioned only a few vegetables here, choosing them for special reasons.

Tomatoes:  Of all shapes and sizes these are a most useful vegetable because by plunging them into boiling water for a minute or so (after first carefully washing them with soap and rinsing off well) the skin becomes loose and can easily be peeled off; this enables one to safely eat them raw—a great advantage.  Cut up and fried with onions they make an excellent sauce; they can be added to stews or to vegetables such as beans or squash; peeled and stewed with a little brown sugar—or better still big ones cut in half, sprinkled with bread crumbs, brown sugar and oil and then grilled—they become a delicious and fancy dish.

Cucumbers:  If these are thoroughly washed with soap, rinsed well and dried, they can then be safely eaten raw; or safer still, peel the skins off.  In many countries one sees a huge, fat, almost yellow-coloured variety of cucumber which, although the seeds are well developed, can nevertheless be eaten to advantage raw or, if the seeds are too tough, they can be scooped out and the flesh next to the skin eaten.  It is possible to cook these like squash but they are practically tasteless so need onions or peppers or something to give them flavour.

Cabbages:  Seldom good enough in hot climates to make a fresh salad they can be rapidly steamed—but not overcooked—in a little boiling water and then cooled and used as a salad or just served hot as a vegetable; they may also

be cut up and fried in vegetable oil or the partially cooked leaves be used for dolmeh (stuffed cabbage leaves).  A cup of fried, dry bread crumbs sprinkled over boiled or steamed cabbage makes it much more tasty.

Alfalfa:  Most people do not know that this plant, widely grown in some places as a fodder for cattle, can be eaten raw in salads (if one lives in a dysentery-free place) or fried in oil as a vegetable.

Okra:  Of all the hundreds of vegetables in the world one might wonder why I single this one out for mention; the reason is that it is not well known in North America but is often the only vegetable in season in an African market.  It is a strange looking thing, big at the stem end and tapering to a point with sides to it—not uniformly round, in other words; it is never a dark green but rather the colour of green beans; okra can be tiny (in the southern United States it is often called “lady fingers”) or 2 or 3 inches long.  It has a sticky quality and is full of soft seeds like a tomato but it is an excellent and filling vegetable; stewed with fried onions and tomatoes, with or without meat added, it makes a tasty dish.

Squash:  Native, dark green, round squashes the size of footballs and with skins like cast iron can often be not only bought but kept for some time before cooking them.  We found if we managed to cut one open and peel it the flesh, cut up and stewed with onions and very little water, was delicious; we added vegetable oil and seasoning to it.

Garlic and onions:  Garlic grows in a round cluster of cloves contained in a white paper-like skin and is the bulb of a

plant; it has been known for its medicinal qualities for 5,000 years:  it kills worms if taken regularly—at least in animals, according to a famous English veterinary doctor; it is said to help keep blood pressure down; it is good for ones chest; according to the Persians it reduces flatulence or wind in the stomach; it is a strong intestinal disinfectant if taken raw and some people like every now and then to retire from the companionship of their fellowmen and have a 24 or 48 hour period alone in which they indulge themselves in a garlic cure.  Although garlic was an almost unknown thing in my life, I have now devised a recipe of my own which is delicious but makes one smell of it for at least 36 hours.  Peel many garlic cloves, a cupful or more, depending on whether one wants a large or small quantity, and fry them slowly in oil until they are brown and becoming transparent —a sign they are almost cooked, then add generously some honey and a tiny bit of water to prevent from burning and cover and simmer a few more minutes to let the oil and honey sauce glaze the garlic cloves.  This makes a delicious relish served instead of a pickle or a sweet preserve with meat; avoid everyone for two days as you will not be welcome unless you live in a community of garlic eaters.  Next to garlic, onions are excellent for the health and also have definite medicinal properties.  Onions cut up and fried are an essential addition to almost any kind of stew or soup; they can be served boiled, in a white sauce, as a vegetable or raw, cut up in salads; often, however, they are a luxury in countries that are not used to growing them and are therefore rare and expensive.  Both onions and garlic keep for some time, particularly garlic, so one can lay in a supply if one finds them.

Legumes:  Dried beans, lentils and peas come under this heading and are common in most parts of the world and

much more so in Africa than foreigners realize.  They are, next to meat and fish, high in protein.  They are also inexpensive, nourishing and filling.  Aside from the dried ones, one often finds shelled, fresh lima, soya or other beans for sale in native markets; as these can be cooked without first soaking them they are a quick way of getting a meal which has a high nutritional value.

Luffa:  This vine, which grows very easily in both tropical and sub-tropical countries, produces a long squash-like fruit often called “dishcloth gourds”; when they are tender and young they may be cooked and eaten like squash but when fully ripe and dried out the skin is crumbled off and inside is the fibrous skeleton of the fruit which is used as a rough body sponge or to scrub things in the kitchen.  It is obviously a most useful plant and could be a welcome asset grown in any pioneer’s garden.  In very humid climates the gourds may get wormy before they can ripen so pick them and hang them up to dry out.

Milks:  The best milk is obviously absolutely fresh untreated milk but it is becoming harder and harder to get, even in big milk producing countries.  Fresh local milk, in many countries of Africa and the Pacific area, is rare if not unobtainable and therefore one must fall back on dried or canned milk.  My personal opinion is that the best is dried full-fat milk powder reconstituted with water.  In India and many parts of Asia, however, milk from water buffaloes is abundant and delicious; in some places goats’ milk is obtainable and though strong-tasting sometimes makes a more digestible milk for babies than cows’ milk; sheep’s milk also can be found in some countries; both goats’ and sheep’s milk are excellent for yogurt or cheese.  All fresh milk, when boiled, is safe to use.

Yogurt:  One of the best ways of using any milk is in the form of yogurt, or curd, as it is often called.  As milk must be brought to the boil to make this, it becomes a safe and very healthful addition to the diet.  In many African countries importing from France it can nearly always be bought in the supermarket.  Yogurt may be used in an infinite variety of ways, either plain or on fruit salad in place of cream; it makes a delicious dessert if a spoonful of molasses or honey or some jam is mixed with it; the rientals eat it with meat, vegetables and rice dishes.  As yogurt is a culture and made from itself, one can only make it if one has a sample to begin with.  Anywhere in the world where there are East Indian people living one can usually procure yogurt from them; the same is true of people of Arab and Greek origin and as most people are surprisingly kind if asked for a favour, one can beg from these yogurt-eaters enough to start a culture with, a half a cup being sufficient; any unsweetened milk may be used.  Remember that each time one makes yogurt a half a cup must be kept back to make the next culture.  The bacteria found in yogurt are manufacturers of Vitamin B and very good for the intestines, especially after the normal bacterial content has been impaired through taking antibiotics.  (See how to make yogurt in “Basic Recipes”)

Tea:  During my long, exhausting drives in the Land Rover across the continent of Africa, often in tropical heat that was almost unbearable, we discovered the best pickup was to stop the car, make some very strong tea and drink it with a good deal of sugar added.  Although this made us burst into drenching perspiration—adding to what was usually a very drenched state in the beginning—it also had a remarkably cooling and refreshing effect and more than anything else seemed to put us back on our feet.  It gave us a lift very much

stronger than coffee did and we found that it did not wear off as fast, although normally I am more fond of drinking coffee.  Remembering that the Báb Himself highly praised the qualities of tea, we were not surprised at the good effect it produced.  Tea has a wonderful effect on the eyes:  a strong infusion of dark tea, either used as an eye bath right on the eye balls or soaking cotton in it and using it as pads over the closed eyes, refreshes and strengthens them and removes inflammation.  ‘Abdu’l-Bahá recommended tea for this purpose on many occasions; this does not mean, however, that one should wash ones eyes daily with tea, but it may be used in a medicinal manner when required.

Herbal teas or infusions:  In all of Europe, West Africa and many other places one can buy special herbal teas packaged and ready to be used to make an infusion exactly in the same manner as an ordinary tea bag:  rose hips, camomile, vervain, mint, and so on; one of the best is rose hips.  These teas are refreshing and soothing, will not keep one awake at night and provide a hot beverage to which, if one desires, one can add honey or sugar for sweetening; one bag is sufficient for two cups.  Another herb that has remarkable qualities is citronella, called fever tea in many countries of Africa and also used throughout the West Indies; in fact I imagine it is probably a universal herb; in Africa one often finds it growing in villages around the huts of the people; it grows about 5 metres high and looks like a tall, coarse variety of wild grass, but when one crushes it between ones fingers it smells strongly of lemon.  It is this herb that in my childhood was used to produce an oil called citronella which we rubbed on as a strong mosquito repellent.  In addition to making a delicious infusion to drink as tea, in both Africa and the West Indies it is used to reduce the temperature of

people who are suffering from fever.  There is no doubt that it has this effect because we discovered that some nights, when we drank a cup or two before going to bed in order to refresh ourselves, we actually felt chilly although the weather was very hot.  The way to make it is to boil about five or so long blades of the grass in water for a few minutes until the flavour and colour come out; it can be drunk with or without sugar, as desired.  Another way of using citronella  is very delicious is to brew ordinary tea in the water the citronella was boiled in; in other words, one makes a tea composed of ordinary tea leaves with the citronella tea poured over it to brew it.  They say that citronella planted around ones house in clumps helps keep mosquitoes away.  It grows very easily and one can take a root from a plant and start ones own patch of it at home.

Tamarind:  In Africa the Indians sell it, imported from India, but it is obtainable in many countries, including South American ones.  It looks a sticky and horrible dark brown mess, like a mass of dates that have all got stuck together, but it produces a delicious, refreshing, very healthful drink.  The method is to boil a handful or more in about four glasses of water, both to sterilize it and bring out the flavour, which is sour and tart.  One has to learn the quantity one likes by experimenting; strain the juice through a cheese cloth or fine sieve and drink, either hot or iced, with any sweetening added that one desires.

Citrus beverages:  The juice of lemons, limes, oranges and grapefruit can all be used as a drink diluted with water and sweetened if required:  wash each fruit thoroughly by hand with soap, rinse, dry and then cut in two and juice it; in this way one reduces the danger of having germs enter the fruit

when it is cut open; do this always whether you are going to eat the fruit or use its juice.

Doogh:  The Persians make an extremely refreshing drink called “doogh”:  for one glass use between a quarter and a half a cup of yogurt, depending on how thick it is, and mix well with cold water, adding a pinch of salt to taste.  This refreshing beverage can be drunk with meals or at any time.  Yogurt has a very important action on the intestinal tract and facilitates the assimilation of Vitamin B and the development of bacteria that are good for ones health, so the more one includes it in ones diet, the better.

Sekanjabeen:  This is another delicious Persian drink:  make a syrup by boiling together half sugar and half fruit vinegar by measure (one to one) and when it has thickened bottle it; this sherbet is then diluted with ice water to taste and makes a very cooling drink in hot weather.

Bachelor’s corner

As many young men arise to pioneer often not, it would seem, with enough experience to even boil an egg or make a cup of tea, this section is for them and should help them to economize and live better abroad if they are not boarding but have a home of their own.  Let us start with how to offer a guest a warm drink.

How to make a decent cup of tea:  If one really enjoys tea then make an effort to have it good:  first, buy a good tea; tea is better if not in individual packages and is always better for being two kinds or more mixed together.  In other words, a

small tin of orange pekoe or Ceylonese tea and one of a Darjeeling or Indian blend of tea, or even a little Chinese tea mixed with it, tastes much better than one brand all by itself, though there is no objection to using just one brand of tea.  If, however, you are not a connoisseur and do not want to be bothered with tea leaves in your teapot, try putting, for two or three people, one tea bag into the pot, or for four or five cups, two tea bags.  It is always more economical if you want to make a number of cups of tea to put the bags in the teapot as in this way the tea will go further than just handing each person a single tea bag for his own cup.  To make good tea the water must be really boiling and a little should be poured into the teapot to heat its stomach, so to speak.  Pour this off, then put your tea, or your tea bags, into the heated pot and add the boiling water.  Allow it to stand, preferably keeping hot over the pot of boiling water, for five to ten minutes.  Tea tastes very much better if it is properly steeped and there is no comparison in the pleasure of drinking it when it has been brewed for a few minutes instead of just having hot water poured on it and then poured right off it into the tea cup.  If you are making tea with loose tea leaves instead of tea bags, count one level teaspoon for the teapot and another teaspoon for every cup.  This may be made very strong, in a small teapot, and a little then poured into the bottom of each of the tea cups, filling them up with hot water afterwards, which is the Persian way of serving tea.  A handful of peanuts or other nuts makes a good snack to take with a cup of tea if you are planning to have it between meals instead of the interminable habit of eating a cookie.  Remember tea has a very stimulating effect and, strangely enough, very hot tea in very hot weather has a much more cooling effect than a cold beverage.  Indeed, strong tea is an antidote for heat stroke.

How to make coffee:  Coffee made from grains instead of one of the instant powdered coffees available nowadays is better for the health and usually tastes better.  It can be made in one of many ways, a percolator, a drip percolator, or by just putting the coffee in a pan with boiling water and allowing it to boil up once and then passing it through a fine strainer to take out the grains.  The proportion should be about one heaping teaspoonful for every cup of water but it can be more or less according to the taste of the person making it.

How to make cocoa:  About two level dessert-spoonfuls of cocoa for each large cup or glass:  pour first a little cold water onto the cocoa powder and mix completely with a spoon to take away any lumps, then add the boiling milk or boiling water or a dilution of half and half, stirring all the time to avoid lumps, and put the mixture back on the stove and bring to the boil as this thoroughly cooks the starch grains in the cocoa and makes it more digestible.  Sugar may be added while it is boiling or each person to his own taste afterwards.

Economy:  If the bachelor will learn to make his own breakfast he will not only save money but eat much better than he could possibly do in a restaurant or a hotel.  Also, if he will take the trouble to learn to cook a little—remembering that all the world’s most famous chefs are men—and he does not want to cook three times a day, he can make his dinner or lunch at home and plan to have one meal out in a restaurant.  By this method he can afford to eat very much better and more nutritious food.  Cooking is fun and once one gets a little interested in it one can enjoy very much improvising and seeing what one can do for ones self and

ones guests.  I firmly believe that young people who are out on their own, travelling and pioneering, should study at least a little about food values and conscientiously make an effort to see that they get the fruit, vegetables and proteins needed to keep their bodies in good condition.

Far too many people these days ignore the importance of breakfast literally to break ones fast after not eating all night; the blood sugar is low in the morning; a good breakfast not only raises it but keeps it up for hours and gives one the energy needed to tackle the day’s work.  Have some bread, preferably brown, on hand, honey or jam, butter or margarine, if one can keep it fresh, and tea or coffee and then consider adding eggs, done in any of the following ways, from easy to more complicated.

How to boil eggs:  Have water boiling hard; if possible avoid taking an egg out of the refrigerator and putting it in the boiling water as it is almost sure to crack, so have the egg at room temperature to begin with.  Lower it carefully on a spoon into the water.  Three and a half to four minutes in fast-boiling water makes a soft-boiled egg; eight to ten minutes is sufficient to boil eggs hard.  To skin hard-boiled eggs put them immediately in cold water and the shell will come off more easily.

How to fry eggs:  Heat any fat or oil in a pan; break the eggs first into a separate dish or cup to be sure that they are fresh, and if they do not smell or have any blood mixed into the yolks or look unhealthy, then slide them into the fat in the pan; in this way the yolks will not break.  Either put a lid on to cook the white part through or tilt the pan a little and with a spoon baste or pour over the eggs the hot fat.

How to scramble eggs:  Break the number of eggs desired, counting at least one egg per person, into a dish and add a tablespoonful of either milk or water for each egg, also pepper and salt to taste, and mix very well together with a fork.
